# Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
<note>original: 四庫全書 (Siku Quanshu). This was the largest collection of books in Chinese history, commissioned by the Qianlong Emperor in 1772 to encompass the vast majority of Chinese literature and knowledge.</note>

### Masters Division
<note>original: 子部 (Zibu). In traditional Chinese bibliography, books are divided into four "Treasuries": Classics, History, Masters, and Literature. The Masters Division includes works on philosophy, religion, military science, medicine, and the arts.</note>

</insert>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
<note>original: 欽定四庫全書 (Qinding Siku Quanshu). The term "Imperially Commissioned" indicates that this copy was produced under the direct authority and oversight of the Qianlong Emperor.</note>
<insert>
### Masters Division
<note>original: 子部 (Zibu). This category contains works by "the Masters," covering a wide range of subjects including philosophy, medicine, and the supernatural.</note>
### In Search of the Supernatural, Volumes 1 to 5
<note>original: 搜神記 (Soushen Ji). Compiled by Gan Bao in the 4th century, this is a landmark collection of Chinese mythology, legends, and "strange stories" involving ghosts and spirits.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
<note>original: 欽定四庫全書 (Qinding Siku Quanshu). This marks the text as part of the massive 18th-century project to categorize and preserve all significant Chinese literature.</note>

### Masters Division XII
<note>original: 子部十二 (Zibu shier). The "Masters" division included philosophy, science, and technical arts, as well as "fiction" or miscellaneous records like this one.</note>

## In Search of the Supernatural
<note>original: 搜神記 (Soushenji). This is one of the most famous early collections of "strange tales" (zhiguai), focusing on the paranormal, spirits, and folklore.</note>

### Abstract

->Fiction Category II: Under the Branch of Strange Tales<-
<note>original: 小說家類二 異聞之屬. In the imperial classification system, works like this were not considered "literature" in the modern sense, but rather a sub-category of the "Masters" division titled "Fiction" (Xiaoshuo).</note>

Your servants <note>Referring to the imperial editors of the Siku Quanshu project</note> respectfully note that the twenty-volume edition of *In Search of the Supernatural* is traditionally attributed to Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty. Gan Bao, whose courtesy name was Lingsheng, was a native of Xincai. During the reign of Emperor Yuan <note>Reigned 317–323 CE, the first emperor of the Eastern Jin</note>, he served as a <term>Gentleman of Writing <note>original: 著作郎 (Zhuzuo Lang). An official post responsible for drafting documents and compiling historical records.</note></term> and was in charge of the national history. He was later promoted to <term>Cavalier Attendant-in-Ordinary <note>original: 散騎常侍 (Sanqi Changshi). A prestigious high-ranking advisory position at court.</note></term>. His official biography states that Bao was deeply moved by the miraculous event of his father’s maid returning to life <note>According to legend, the maid survived for ten years in his father's tomb by eating the food offerings provided for the deceased, which prompted Gan Bao's interest in the afterlife</note>; because of this, he compiled a collection of accounts involving ancient and contemporary spirits, divine anomalies, and the transformations of people and things into a single work. His original preface is preserved within his official biography. This book was listed in the bibliographic records of the Sui and Tang histories, but in the Song...

<summary>This page provides the bibliographical abstract for the classic collection of supernatural tales, "In Search of the Supernatural," detailing the life of its author, Gan Bao, and the miraculous personal event that inspired him to document anomalies.</summary>
<keywords>Gan Bao, Soushenji, Jin Dynasty, Siku Quanshu, Chinese folklore, spirits, supernatural tales, Gentleman of Writing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Abstract" (Tiyao) for "In Search of the Supernatural." # Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Abstract

### Masters Division XII

## In Search of the Supernatural

### Abstract

The bibliographers <term>Chao Gongwu<note>A Southern Song Dynasty scholar (c. 1105–1180) whose catalog is a primary source for ancient Chinese bibliography.</note></term>, <term>Chen Zhensun<note>A 13th-century bibliographer known for his detailed descriptive catalog of books.</note></term>, and various other masters do not record this work in their collections. However, it is cited by <term>Wang Yinglin<note>One of the most prolific scholars of the late Song Dynasty.</note></term> in his encyclopedia, the *Ocean of Jade* <note>original: 玉海 (Yuhai).</note>.

The *General Catalog of the Congwen Library* <note>original: 崇文總目 (Congwen Zongmu), a major imperial catalog of the Song Dynasty.</note> states that the *Comprehensive Record of In Search of the Supernatural* <note>original: 搜神總記 (Soushen Zongji).</note> consists of ten volumes and does not name an author. Some suggest it was written by Gan Bao, but this is incorrect. 

Furthermore, a postscript by <term>Hu Zhenheng<note>A Ming Dynasty scholar (1569–1645) famous for his work on Tang poetry and bibliography.</note></term> mentions that this book refers to a certain "Xie, General of the West." Upon investigation, <term>Xie Shang<note>A prominent Jin Dynasty general and official (308–357).</note></term> was only granted the title "General of the West" during the <term>Yonghe era<note>345–356 CE.</note></term> of Emperor Mu. Historical records indicate that Gan Bao’s book was completed and shown to <term>Liu Tan<note>A famous scholar-official known for his expertise in "Pure Conversation" philosophy.</note></term>, yet Liu Tan died much earlier, during the <term>Daning era<note>323–326 CE.</note></term> of Emperor Ming. 

Therefore, the title "General of the West" appeared more than twenty years after the book was supposedly finished. It is suspected that these sections were added by later hands and were not part of Gan Bao’s original text. 

Additionally, an examination of the *Record of the World during the Taiping Era* <note>original: 太平寰宇記 (Taiping Huanyu Ji), a massive 10th-century geographical work.</note> reveals that under the entry for "Qingling Terrace," it cites the story of <term>Han Ping<note>A legendary figure whose wife was stolen by a king; the couple eventually committed suicide and their souls became butterflies.</note></term> transforming into butterflies from *In Search of the Supernatural*. However, the current edition we are examining lacks this particular story.

<summary>This page presents a scholarly critique of the text's authenticity, using anachronisms regarding titles and missing famous stories to argue that the current version contains later additions.</summary>
<keywords>Siku Quanshu, bibliography, Gan Bao, In Search of the Supernatural, Xie Shang, Han Ping, textual criticism, Song Dynasty catalogs</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page concludes the bibliographic abstract (Tiyao) for the "In Search of the Supernatural" (Soushenji). The previous page discussed the historical origins of the text and potential misattributions; this section focuses on the physical discrepancies in volume counts between different Song Dynasty catalogs and concludes with the formal submission by the Qing Dynasty editors led by Ji Yun.</meta>

Upon checking the citations in the <term>Extensive Records of the Taiping Era<note>original: 太平廣記 (Taiping Guangji), a massive 10th-century anthology of stories and supernatural lore.</note></term>, they once again align one-by-one with this current edition. Both of these works were compiled during the early Song Dynasty, yet it remains unclear why they differ from one another. It is suspected that the version cited by <term>Yue Shi<note>A Song Dynasty scholar and geographer (930–1007).</note></term> was the *Extensive Records*, whereas the version cited by <term>Li Fang<note>The lead editor of the *Extensive Records of the Taiping Era* (925–996).</note></term> was the *Comprehensive Record*. 

Later scribes, in the process of manual copying, likely split each original volume into two; this would explain why the current volume count does not match the ten volumes recorded in the <term>General Catalog of the Congwen Library<note>original: 崇文總目 (Congwen Zongmu), a major imperial library catalog of the Song Dynasty.</note></term>. Following the scholarly principle of "transmitting the doubt" <note>An editorial practice of preserving uncertain or conflicting information for posterity rather than forcing a correction that might be wrong.</note>, we have for now retained the original title as it appears in our records.

Respectfully submitted in the third month of the 43rd year of the <term>Qianlong reign<note>Corresponding to March or April 1778.</note></term>.

->Chief Editors: <gloss>Your servant</gloss> **Ji Yun**, <gloss>Your servant</gloss> **Lu Xixiong**, <gloss>Your servant</gloss> **Sun Shiyi**<-

->Chief Collator: <gloss>Your servant</gloss> **Lufeichi**<-

It ends with the formal certification by the Siku Quanshu editorial board in 1778.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general front matter and bibliography of the *Siku Quanshu* (The Complete Library in Four Sections) to the specific text of the *Soushenji* (In Search of the Supernatural). -># *In Search of the Supernatural*<-

# Original Preface to *In Search of the Supernatural*

Although I have investigated the intentions of the ancients within the records and gathered stories that had been lost or overlooked in my own time, these accounts are certainly not things I personally heard or witnessed with my own ears and eyes. How then could I dare to claim that there is no deviation from the truth? 

When Duke Shuo of Wei <note>original: 衛朔 (Wei Shuo). A ruler of the State of Wei during the Spring and Autumn period.</note> lost his state, the two commentaries <note>Refers to the *Zuo Zhuan* and the *Gongyang Zhuan*, two major historical commentaries that provide conflicting dates and details for the same events.</note> differed in their reports. In the matter of Lü Wang <note>Also known as Jiang Ziya, the famous strategist who helped found the Zhou Dynasty.</note> serving the Zhou, Zichang <note>The courtesy name of Sima Qian (c. 145–86 BCE), China's most famous historian and author of the *Records of the Grand Historian*.</note> preserved both versions of the story. Similar cases of conflicting accounts occur quite often. 

Viewed from this perspective, the difficulty of verifying what one hears or sees has existed for a very long time. If the formal wording of official death notices and the records kept in the national archives <note>original: 國史 (Guoshi). Official dynastic histories.</note> are still subject to such inconsistencies, how much more difficult is it to recount records from a thousand years ago? To describe the strange customs of distant lands, to piece together fragments of text from damaged scrolls, or to seek out the deeds of the past from the elderly? 

To demand that events leave no conflicting traces and that accounts never diverge before they can be considered "authentic" was a challenge that even the historians of old struggled with. Nevertheless, the state does not abolish the officials in charge of recording history and keeping notes <note>original: 注記之官 (Zhuji zhi guan). Imperial scribes whose job was to document the daily words and actions of the emperor and the state.</note>.

<summary>This page contains the opening of Gan Bao's original 4th-century preface, where he defends the value of recording supernatural and oral traditions by pointing out that even "standard" historical records often contain contradictions.</summary>

<keywords>Gan Bao, In Search of the Supernatural, Soushenji, preface, historiography, Sima Qian, historical reliability, Chinese folklore</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Original Preface" by Gan Bao, the 4th-century historian and government official. Having established the inherent difficulty of historical accuracy in the previous section, he now explains his rationale for compiling these supernatural accounts: to preserve what is significant and to demonstrate the reality of the divine realm.</meta>

<header>By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections</header>
<header>Original Preface</header>

## Original Preface

Is it not because what is lost is small while what is preserved is great that scholars never cease their labors of recitation and reading?

If this current collection contains matters inherited from the records of former times, then I am not to blame for their contents. However, if in gathering the events of recent generations there should be any errors or falsehoods, I am willing to share in the mockery and slander alongside the virtuous men and scholars of the past. As for the writing of this work itself, it is sufficient to demonstrate that the **Way of the Spirits** <note>original: 神道 (shendao). This refers to the divine or supernatural order of the universe, suggesting that spirits and the afterlife are an undeniable part of reality.</note> is not a falsehood.

The sayings of the various schools of thought are too numerous to be fully perused, and that which the ears and eyes perceive is too vast to be entirely recorded. Now, I have roughly selected enough to elaborate upon the essence of the **Eight Summaries** <note>original: 八畧 (Ba lüe). Likely a reference to an expanded classification of knowledge based on the "Seven Summaries" (Qi lüe), the first Chinese bibliographical catalog.</note>, forming these **minor sayings** <term>weishuo: literally "small talk" or "micro-discourses," a term for anecdotal literature that preceded the modern concept of the novel</term> and nothing more.

It is my hope that curious scholars of the future will record the foundations of these stories, finding therein something to occupy their minds and rest their eyes upon, without finding fault with me.

->**Gan Bao, courtesy name Lingsheng**<- <note>Gan Bao (c. 286–336 CE) was a historian of the Eastern Jin dynasty. He reportedly compiled this work after his brother was revived from death and his father's concubine survived for years in a tomb, events which convinced him of the reality of spirits.</note>

<summary>In this concluding portion of his preface, Gan Bao justifies his collection of ghost stories and anomalies as a serious intellectual endeavor intended to prove the existence of the supernatural world. He positions his work within the tradition of scholarly recording, albeit as a "minor" genre compared to official history.</summary>

<keywords>Gan Bao, Soushenji, Jin Dynasty, anomalies, Way of the Spirits, Eight Summaries, Chinese historiography, preface</keywords>

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in the Four Treasuries
## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume One
-> Composed by **Gan Bao** <note>original: 干寶. A 4th-century historian of the Jin Dynasty, commissioned to write the official history of the era, who also possessed a deep fascination with the paranormal.</note> of the Jin Dynasty <-

**Shennong** <term>Divine Farmer: A legendary prehistoric ruler and culture hero credited with teaching humans agriculture and herbal medicine.</term> used a red whip to strike the "hundred herbs" <note>A traditional phrase meaning all varieties of plants.</note>. Through this, he fully understood their properties—whether they were neutral, toxic, cold, or warm—as well as the specific scents and flavors they possessed. He used this knowledge to sow the "hundred grains" <note>The various staple crops of ancient China.</note>; for this reason, the world called him **Shennong**.

**Chisongzi** <note>original: 赤松子 (Master Red Pine).</note> was the **Rain Master** <term>Yushi: A deity in Chinese mythology responsible for directing the rains.</term> during the time of Shennong. He consumed a powder made of "water jade" <note>original: 水玉散 (shuiyu san). This likely refers to ground quartz or crystal, believed by ancient seekers of immortality to have alchemical properties.</note> and taught Shennong its secrets. He could enter fire without being burned. He traveled to the **Kunlun Mountains** <note>The mythological home of the gods and the source of the yellow river.</note>, where he would often enter the stone chamber of the **Queen Mother of the West** <note>original: 西王母 (Xi Wangmu). A powerful ancient goddess who governed immortality and resided in the far west.</note>. He would ascend and descend following the wind and rain. The younger daughter of the **Yan Emperor** <note>original: 炎帝 (Yandi). The "Flame Emperor," often identified as the same figure as, or a relative of, Shennong.</note> followed him and likewise attained immortality; they departed together. During the era of **Gao Xin** <note>Emperor Ku, one of the legendary Five Emperors who ruled ancient China.</note>, he appeared again as the Rain Master, wandering among the human world. The Rain Master of the present day was originally a horse.

<margin>In Search of the Supernatural</margin>

<summary>This opening page introduces the legendary foundations of Chinese civilization, beginning with the Divine Farmer (Shennong) and the transcendence of the Rain Master (Chisongzi). It establishes the connection between the earthly labor of agriculture and the divine mastery of nature and immortality.</summary>
<keywords>Shennong, Chisongzi, immortality, Rain Master, Queen Mother of the West, herbal medicine, mythology</keywords>

<header>Complete Collection of the Golden Plains of Bashu, Volume One</header>

### Chijiang Ziyu

**Chijiang Ziyu** <note>A legendary figure often associated with early Daoist traditions of longevity and transcendence.</note> was a man of the era of the **Yellow Emperor** <term>Huangdi: The mythical "Yellow Emperor," a foundational cultural hero and patron of the esoteric arts.</term>. He did not consume the "five grains" <note>The practice of *bigu* (avoiding grains) was believed by seekers of immortality to clear the body of impurities.</note>, but instead ate only the blossoms of the "hundred herbs." By the time of the Emperor **Yao**, he served as a woodworker. He possessed the ability to ascend and descend along with the wind and rain. He frequently sold silken bird-hunting cords at the city gates; for this reason, he was also known as the **Father of Cords**.

### Ning Fengzi

**Ning Fengzi** was a man of the era of the **Yellow Emperor**. Tradition holds that he served as the Emperor's **Master of Pottery** <term>Taozheng: An ancient official title for the overseer of kiln works and ceramic production.</term>. A mysterious stranger once visited him and managed the kiln fires on his behalf, producing smoke of five different colors. After a long time, the stranger taught these secrets to Fengzi. Eventually, Fengzi built up a great fire and burned himself within it; his spirit was seen ascending and descending within the smoke and vapor. When the people later inspected the ashes, his bones remained. They buried him in the **Ningbei Mountains**; thus, he was called **Ning Fengzi**.

### Wo Quan

**Wo Quan** was a herb-gatherer from Mount Huai. He was fond of eating pine seeds <note>A common dietary staple in hagiographies of immortals, thought to grant the ability to fly and grow protective fur.</note>. His physical body grew a coating of hair that was seven inches long. Both... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; likely describing his eyes or his fleetness of foot.</note>

<summary>This page provides brief hagiographies of three legendary immortals—Chijiang Ziyu, Ning Fengzi, and Wo Quan—detailing the specific dietary practices and elemental masteries that led to their transcendence.</summary>
<keywords>Yellow Emperor, Yao, Chijiang Ziyu, Ning Fengzi, Wo Quan, immortality, Master of Pottery, pine seeds, bigu, Daoist hagiography</keywords>

...his eyes became square <note>In ancient Daoist texts, "square eyes" were a physical sign of extreme longevity and spiritual attainment.</note>, and he gained the ability to fly and outrun galloping horses. He gave pine seeds to the Emperor **Yao** <term>Yao: One of the Three Sage Kings of high antiquity, a model of Confucian virtue.</term>, but Yao was too busy with affairs of state to take them. 

The variety of pine was the **Jian Pine** <note>original: "Jian Song." Likely a specific variety of pine thought to hold higher spiritual potency.</note>. Those who took the seeds during that time all lived to be three hundred years old.

### Peng Zu

**Peng Zu** <term>Peng Zu: The "Chinese Methuselah," a legendary figure synonymous with longevity.</term> was a high official during the **Yin** <note>Also known as the Shang dynasty (approx. 1600–1046 BCE).</note> dynasty. His surname was Qian and his given name was Keng. He was the grandson of the Emperor **Zhuanxu** <note>A sky-god and one of the legendary Five Emperors.</note> and a middle son of the **Lu Zhong** clan. 

He lived through the Xia dynasty until the end of the Shang, and was said to be seven hundred years old. He regularly consumed cinnamon and **divine fungi** <term>Zhi: Often translated as "Lingzhi" or "Reishi" mushrooms, believed to grant immortality.</term>. In Liyang, there is a "Shrine of the Immortal Peng Zu." Previous generations claimed that prayers offered there for wind or rain were always answered. Two tigers were said to always reside at the left and right sides of the shrine. Even today, after a sacrificial ceremony is completed at the site, the tracks of two tigers are visible on the ground.

### Shi Men

**Shi Men** was a disciple of **Xiao Fu** <note>A master of "spirit whistling" and fire manipulation mentioned in earlier sections.</note>. He possessed the power to command fire and ate only peaches and **ginseng** <note>original: "Tao Shen." Peaches are traditional symbols of immortality in Chinese lore.</note>. He served as the **Dragon Master** <term>Longshi: An official title in mythic history for those tasked with the care and taming of dragons.</term> for the King **Kong Jia** of the Xia dynasty. 

However, Kong Jia was unable to align his own heart and mind with the master’s teachings. In a fit of temper, the King killed Shi Men and buried him in the wilderness. One morning, a great storm of wind and rain arrived to fetch him. The trees of the mountain...

<summary>This page describes three legendary figures who attained longevity or supernatural powers: Wo Quan, who offered longevity to Emperor Yao; Peng Zu, the famous 700-year-old official; and Shi Men, a dragon-tamer who transcended death.</summary>
<keywords>Wo Quan, Peng Zu, Shi Men, longevity, pine seeds, cinnamon, Emperor Yao, Kong Jia, dragon master, Daoist immortals</keywords>

### Ge You

**Ge You** was originally a man of the **Qiang** <term>Qiang: An ancient ethnic group from the western regions of China, often associated with mountain culture and early Daoist traditions.</term> people from the state of **Shu** <note>Modern-day Sichuan province.</note>. During the reign of **King Cheng of Zhou** <note>The second king of the Zhou Dynasty, traditionally reigned 1042–1021 BCE.</note>, he took a liking to carving wooden sheep and selling them. One day, he mounted one of his wooden sheep and rode it into the heart of Shu. 

The kings, dukes, and noblemen of Shu pursued him up to **Sui Mountain**. Sui Mountain is located to the southwest of **Mount Emei**; it is filled with peach trees and its heights are limitless. Those who followed him there never returned, for they all attained the **Way of the Immortals** <term>Xian Dao: The spiritual path or discipline aimed at achieving transcendence, longevity, and supernatural powers.</term>. Because of this, a local proverb says: 

->“To obtain a single peach from Sui Mountain—even if one cannot become an immortal, it is enough to make one a person of great wealth and influence.”<-

Consequently, dozens of shrines were established at the foot of the mountain to honor him.

### Cui Wenzi

**Cui Wenzi** was a native of **Mount Tai** <note>One of the Five Sacred Mountains of China, considered a portal to the spirit world.</note>. He studied the arts of immortality under **Wang Ziqiao** <term>Wang Ziqiao: Traditionally identified as Prince Jin, the son of King Ling of Zhou, who became a famous Daoist immortal associated with the sheng (reed pipe).</term>. 

One day, Ziqiao transformed into a **white rainbow** <note>original: "bai ni." While "ni" typically refers to a secondary rainbow, in Daoist hagiography it often represents a celestial, dragon-like manifestation or a bridge between heaven and earth.</note> and descended, carrying divine medicine to give to Wenzi. Wenzi, startled by the strange sight, raised a halberd and struck the rainbow. The creature fell, dropping the medicine. Wenzi looked down...

<summary>This page recounts the legends of Ge You, who led followers to immortality on the peach-covered peaks of Sui Mountain, and Cui Wenzi, who encountered his master in the form of a celestial rainbow.</summary>

# In Search of the Supernatural
### Page Three

...and they looked at it, only to find that it was the corpse of **Wangzi Qiao** <note>Wangzi Qiao, also known as Prince Qiao, was a legendary crown prince of the Zhou Dynasty who supposedly attained immortality and is often depicted riding a crane.</note>. They placed the body inside a room and covered it with a bamboo basket. In a short while, it transformed into a large bird. When they opened the basket to look, the bird fluttered its wings and flew away.

---

**Guan Xian** was a man of the state of **Song** <note>A significant state during the Spring and Autumn period, located in what is now Henan province.</note>. He made his living by fishing and resided by the banks of the **Sui River** for over a hundred years. When he caught fish, he would sometimes release them, sometimes sell them, or sometimes eat them himself. He always wore a formal cap and sash <term>Guan Dai: The traditional dress of a gentleman or official, suggesting that despite his humble trade as a fisherman, he possessed the dignity of a scholar.</term>. He was fond of planting litchi trees and sustained himself by eating their blossoms and fruit.

**Duke Jing of Song** <note>Reigned c. 516–469 BCE.</note> questioned him regarding his "Way" <note>The secret to his longevity and spiritual cultivation.</note>, but Guan Xian would not reveal it. The Duke then had him put to death. Several decades later, however, Guan Xian was seen sitting atop the city gates of the Song capital, playing the **zither** <term>Qin: A seven-stringed instrument often associated with refinement and the communication of spiritual truths.</term>. After remaining there for dozens of days, he finally departed. Subsequently, every household in the state of Song established a shrine to offer him sacrifices.

---

**Qin Gao** was a man of the state of **Zhao**. He was a master of the zither and served as a retainer to **King Kang of Song**. He practiced the longevity arts of **Juan Zi** and **Peng Zu** <term>Juan-Peng: A reference to two legendary long-lived figures, Juan Zi and Peng Zu; the latter is famously said to have lived for over 800 years.</term>. He wandered between the regions of **Jizhou** and **Zhuo Commandery** for more than two hundred years. Later, he bid farewell and entered the waters of the **Zhuo River** to capture a dragon’s offspring. With the [disciples...] <note>The text here refers to his promise to meet his disciples again upon his return from the underwater realm.</note>

<summary>This page details the miraculous departures and reappearances of three legendary figures: Wangzi Qiao, who transforms into a bird; Guan Xian, a fisherman who survives execution to play music upon the city gates; and Qin Gao, a master musician seeking a dragon.</summary>

...his disciples. On the appointed day, and the day following, they all purified themselves and fasted while waiting by the water's edge. They established a shrine-house. Just as promised, he emerged riding a **red carp** <note>In Chinese mythology, the carp is a symbol of transformation and perseverance, famously leaping over the "Dragon Gate" to become a dragon.</note> and sat within the shrine. Thousands upon thousands of people came to witness this. He remained for one month before once more entering the water and departing.

---

**Tao Angong** was a master metalsmith from **Liu'an** <note>A city in modern-day Anhui province, known historically for its mineral resources.</note>. He frequently tended the smelting fires. One morning, the fire scattered and a purple glow surged toward the heavens. Angong prostrated himself before the furnace and pleaded for mercy. Shortly after, a **Vermilion Bird** <term>Zhu Que: A mythological spirit creature representing the South, the season of summer, and the element of fire.</term> perched atop the furnace and spoke: "Angong, Angong! Your smelting is in communion with Heaven. On the seventh day of the seventh month, I shall come to receive you with a **Red Dragon**." 

When the appointed time arrived, Angong mounted the dragon and departed toward the southeast. Tens of thousands of people from the city held a farewell ceremony <term>Zu: A traditional sacrificial rite performed for a traveler to ensure a safe journey.</term> to see him off, and all bid him a final farewell.

---

There was a man who entered **Mount Jiao** <note>Located in the Yangtze River, this mountain is famous for its Taoist associations and "Stone Cliff" inscriptions.</note> for seven years. **Lord Lao** <note>Laojun: The deified form of the philosopher Laozi, revered as a high deity in the Taoist pantheon.</note> gave him a wooden drill and commanded him to bore through a flat stone. The stone was five <note>The text ends mid-sentence; based on similar accounts, this likely refers to the thickness of the stone, such as "five feet thick."</note>

...and said, "When this stone is worn through, you shall attain the Way." After forty years had passed, the stone was indeed pierced, and he consequently obtained the secret formula for the **Divine Immortal Elixir** <note>original: "神仙丹訣" (Shenxian Danjue). In Chinese alchemy, these formulas were the ultimate goal for those seeking to transcend mortality.</note>.

---

**Lu Shaoqian** was a man from **Shanyang** <note>A commandery located in modern-day Shandong province.</note>. **Emperor Wen of Han** <note>Reigned 180–157 BCE. He was famously interested in the Daoist philosophy of "non-action" and longevity.</note> once visited him in secret <term>Weifu: To travel in civilian clothes to avoid the recognition and ceremony usually afforded to an Emperor.</term> while carrying gold, wishing to inquire about his "Way." Shaoqian came to answer the door leaning on a **golden staff** and holding an **ivory fan**.

---

**Liu An, King of Huainan**, <note>A grandson of the Han Dynasty's founder, he was a famous patron of scholars and occultists, eventually credited with the philosophical work *Huainanzi*.</note> was fond of the Daoist arts. He established a kitchen and chef specifically to host traveling guests. On the morning of the first month, **eight old men** <note>Later known as the "Eight Lords" (Ba Gong). Legend says they eventually shared the secret of immortality with the King.</note> arrived at the gate seeking an audience. The gate official reported this to the King, but the King instructed the official to discourage them, saying: "My King loves the art of long life. Since you gentlemen do not possess the technique to halt the decay of old age, I do not dare report your arrival." 

Knowing they would not be received in their current forms, the old men transformed themselves into **eight young boys** with complexions as vibrant as **peach blossoms**. The King then granted them an audience, performing grand rituals and arranging music to honor the Eight Lords. They took up the **zither** <term>Qin: A seven-stringed plucked instrument used as a vehicle for spiritual and intellectual expression.</term> and sang to its strings: "The brilliant High Heaven shines upon the earth below, knowing my love for the Way..."

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 1

"He descends! The Lord and I shall grow feathers and wings <note>original: "毛羽" (maoyu). In Daoist tradition, "growing feathers" is a metaphor for the transformation of a mortal body into that of a celestial being or 'feathered immortal'.</note>. We ascend to the azure clouds and tread upon **Mount Liangfu** <note>A sacred mountain in modern-day Shandong province, traditionally associated with the spirits of the dead and ancient ritual sacrifices.</note>. We gaze upon the **Three Lights** <note>The sun, moon, and stars.</note> and meet the **Big Dipper**. Driving through the wind and clouds, we command the **Jade Maidens**." This is what is known today as the **Huainan Composition** <note>original: "淮南操" (Huainan Cao). A musical piece traditionally attributed to the court of Liu An, the King of Huainan.</note>.

---

**Liu Gen**, whose courtesy name was **Jun'an**, was a native of **Chang'an in Jingzhao** <note>Chang’an was the capital of the Han Dynasty; Jingzhao was the metropolitan region surrounding it.</note>. During the reign of **Emperor Cheng of Han** <note>Reigned 33–7 BCE.</note>, he entered **Mount Song** <note>One of the Five Sacred Mountains of China, long a center for Daoist cultivation and meditation.</note> to study the Way. There, he encountered an extraordinary person who bestowed secret instructions upon him, through which he attained immortality. He possessed the power to summon spirits.

**Shi Qi**, the Governor of **Yingchuan**, believed Liu Gen was a charlatan practicing sorcery. He sent men to fetch Gen, intending to execute him. When Gen arrived at the government office, the Governor said, "You claim to make people see spirits. Can you make them manifest? If not, you shall be put to death."

Gen replied, "That is quite easy." He borrowed the Governor’s own brush and inkstone to write a **talisman** <term>Fu: A mystical diagram or set of characters written on paper or silk, believed to command spirits or cosmic forces.</term>, then struck the desk. After a brief moment, five or six spirits suddenly appeared, binding two prisoners before Qi. As Qi looked closely, he realized one of the prisoners was his own deceased father.

<summary>This page concludes a mystical song associated with the King of Huainan and introduces the immortal Liu Gen, who proves his power to summon spirits to a skeptical governor by manifesting the governor's own deceased father.</summary>
<keywords>Huainan Composition, Liu Gen, Mount Song, Shi Qi, talismans, summoning spirits, Han Dynasty, immortality</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of the immortal Liu Gen and his confrontation with Governor Shi Qi, then introduces the miraculous travels of the official Wang Qiao.</meta>

Page Five
->**In Search of the Supernatural**<-

The mother <note>The deceased mother of Governor Shi Qi, whom Liu Gen had summoned from the afterlife to prove his powers.</note> also kowtowed toward **Gen** and said, "My son is crude and lacks manners; he deserves ten thousand deaths." She then scolded **Qi**, saying, "You, as a descendant, have failed to bring glory to your ancestors. Why have you offended a celestial immortal, bringing such distress upon your own parents in this way?" **Qi**, overcome with grief and terror, wept and struck his head against the ground to beg for forgiveness. **Gen** remained silent and suddenly vanished; no one knew where he went.

During the reign of **Emperor Ming of Han** <note>Reigned 58–75 CE; he was a noted patron of both Confucianism and early Buddhism in China.</note>, a **Gentleman of the Secretariat** <note>original: "尚書郎" (Shangshu Lang). A high-ranking administrative post within the imperial cabinet.</note> named **Wang Qiao** of **Hedong** <note>A region east of the Yellow River, in modern-day Shanxi province.</note> served as the **Magistrate of Ye** <note>original: "葉令" (Ye Ling). Ye was a county located in present-day Henan province.</note>. **Qiao** possessed divine arts. On the first day of every lunar month, he would travel from his county to the imperial court. The Emperor was puzzled by the frequency of his visits, especially since no carriages or horses were ever seen accompanying him. He secretly commanded the **Grand Historian** <note>original: "太史" (Taishi). An official responsible for astronomy, the calendar, and observing omens for the Emperor.</note> to keep watch for his arrival.

The Historian reported that whenever **Qiao** was about to arrive, a pair of **wild ducks** <note>original: "雙鳧" (shuang fu). In Chinese folklore, ducks are often depicted as the mounts or transformations of immortals traveling through the air.</note> would fly in from the southeast. Consequently, officials lay in ambush; when they saw the ducks, they cast a net to catch them. However, they found only a single **ceremonial shoe** <note>original: "舄" (xi). A heavy, double-soled shoe worn during formal court rituals.</note>. The Emperor had the Secretariat examine it; it was identified as a shoe bestowed by the office upon its subordinate officials four years prior.

<meta>This page continues the accounts of extraordinary individuals and transcendents. It follows the story of Wang Qiao from the previous page, shifting focus to the legend of Ji Zixun, another figure noted for his mastery over time and physical limitations.</meta>

# Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries, Volume 1

<term>Ji Zixun</term> <note>A famous Taoist transcendent of the Han dynasty, celebrated in various hagiographies for his longevity and supernatural abilities.</note> came from origins that no one knew. During the time of the <term>Eastern Han</term> <note>The period of Chinese history from 25–220 CE.</note>, he arrived in <term>Luoyang</term>. He visited the residences of several dozen high-ranking officials; at each place, he carried only a single jar of wine and a small piece of dried meat. He would say, "I have come from afar and have nothing to offer, so I present these as a small token of my regard." 

Even when hundreds of people were seated together, they could drink and eat all day long without exhausting the supply. After he left, observers saw white clouds rising, which persisted from dawn until dusk. At that time, there was a hundred-year-old man who said that when he was a child, he had seen **Ji** selling medicine in the markets of <term>Kuaiji</term> <note>A commandery in the coastal region of modern-day Zhejiang and Jiangsu.</note>, and his appearance then was exactly the same as it was now. **Ji** took no pleasure in staying in **Luoyang** and eventually vanished.

During the <term>Zhengshi</term> era <note>The reign period from 240–249 CE during the Cao Wei state of the Three Kingdoms period.</note>, someone saw him at <term>Bacheng</term>, east of <term>Chang'an</term>. He was seen with an old man, both of them stroking a <term>bronze man</term> <note>original: "銅人" (tongren). These were massive bronze statues famously cast by the First Emperor of Qin (r. 221–210 BCE). Ji's claim of seeing them cast "500 years ago" fits the historical timeline from the Qin to the Zhengshi era.</note>. He said to the old man, "I just saw this being cast nearly five hundred years ago." Those who saw him called out, "Master Ji, wait a moment!" and tried to walk alongside him. Although he appeared to be walking at a slow and leisurely pace, even a galloping horse could not catch up to him.

<summary>This page recounts the legends of the immortal Ji Zixun, detailing his miraculous ability to multiply food and his apparent agelessness across several centuries. It highlights his presence in both the Eastern Han and the later Three Kingdoms period, emphasizing his transcendence of human aging and speed.</summary>

<keywords>Ji Zixun, Eastern Han, Luoyang, immortality, Taoism, bilocation, Zhengshi era, bronze statues, longevity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of extraordinary figures from the "Sou Shen Ji" (In Search of the Supernatural). It moves from the stories of long-lived transcendents like Ji Zixun to accounts of mysterious, supernatural beggars and figures who survived repeated deaths and predicted natural disasters.</meta>

## The Beggar of Hanyin

**Han Yinsheng** <note>original: "漢陰生" (Hànyīn Shēng). The name implies someone "Born South of the Han River."</note> was a young beggar who lived under the **Wei Bridge** <note>A famous bridge spanning the Wei River in the capital city.</note> in **Chang’an**. He spent his time in the marketplace. The people of the market grew annoyed and weary of his presence, and they threw filth at him to drive him away. However, he would almost immediately reappear in the marketplace to beg; his clothes never appeared soiled and looked exactly as they had before. 

When the local officials heard of this, they arrested him and bound him in **shackles and stocks** <note>original: "桎梏" (zhigù). Traditional wooden restraints used in ancient Chinese judicial punishment.</note>. Despite being bound, he continued to be seen begging in the market as usual. The officials arrested him again and intended to execute him, but he vanished. Shortly thereafter, the houses of those who had thrown filth at him collapsed on their own, killing more than a dozen people. A rhyme began to spread through the city of **Chang’an**:

-> "Give the beggar boy fine wine, <-
-> To avoid the blame of a broken house." <-

## Ping Changsheng of Gucheng

**Ping Changsheng** <note>His name literally means "Ordinary Life."</note> of **Gucheng District** was a man of unknown origins. He was known to have died and returned to life several times. While the people of his time did not believe his claims or recognize his nature, a massive flood eventually struck, causing widespread destruction and many deaths. Throughout the disaster, **Ping** was frequently seen on **Quemen Mountain**, shouting loudly: "Ping Changsheng is here!"

He declared that the heavy rains would continue for five more days and then must cease. When the rains stopped exactly as he predicted, the people climbed the mountain to seek him out and offer sacrifices at a shrine in his honor.

<summary>This page recounts the legends of two supernatural figures: a beggar in Chang'an who could not be harmed or restrained, and a man named Ping Changsheng who predicted the end of a catastrophic flood.</summary>
<keywords>Han Yinsheng, Chang'an, Wei Bridge, Ping Changsheng, Quemen Mountain, flood prediction, supernatural beggars, Han Dynasty folklore</keywords>

## Zuo Ci

**Zuo Ci**, whose courtesy name was **Yuanfang**, was a native of **Lujiang**. From his youth, he possessed supernatural powers. He was once a guest at a banquet hosted by **Lord Cao** <note>original: "曹公" (Cáo Gōng). This refers to Cao Cao (155–220 AD), the brilliant and often ruthless warlord who laid the foundations of the Cao Wei state during the Three Kingdoms period.</note>. The Lord looked around at his many guests with a smile and said, "At today’s grand assembly, the fine delicacies are nearly all provided. The only thing we lack is sea bass from the **Wusong River** <note>The Wusong River flows through modern-day Shanghai and Suzhou; its "perch" or "bass" (lucao) was a legendary delicacy in ancient China.</note> to make **sashimi** <term>original: "膾" (kuài). An ancient dish consisting of finely shredded or sliced raw meat or fish.</term>."

**Yuanfang** replied, "That is easily obtained." He then asked for a copper basin filled with water. Using a bamboo pole with a baited hook, he fished inside the basin. In a brief moment, he pulled a sea bass out of the water. **Lord Cao** clapped his hands loudly in delight, and everyone at the gathering was astonished.

The Lord said, "One fish is not enough to serve the whole table. It would be better if we could have two." **Yuanfang** baited his hook again and cast it into the basin. Moments later, he pulled out another fish; both were over three feet long, fresh, and vibrant. **Lord Cao** personally stepped forward to slice the fish and distributed the portions to all the guests at their seats. 

The Lord then remarked, "Now that we have the bass, I only regret that we have no fresh ginger from **Shu** <note>original: "蜀中" (Shǔzhōng). Shu is the ancient name for the Sichuan region, famous for its pungent ginger and spices.</note> to accompany it."

**Yuanfang** said, "That, too, can be obtained."

The Lord, fearing that he might simply go to a nearby <insert>market</insert>...

An attendant named **Jia** then said, "I previously sent a messenger to **Shu** <note>Shu refers to the region of modern-day Sichuan, which was thousands of miles away from Cao Cao’s base in the north and famous for its high-quality textiles.</note> to purchase **brocade** <term>original: "錦" (jǐn). A luxury fabric woven with complex, colorful patterns; "Shu brocade" was one of the most prized materials in ancient China.</term>. You could command someone to tell my messenger to increase our purchase by two bolts." 

A man <note>referring to Zuo Ci’s magical projection or messenger</note> departed and returned in a mere moment, bringing the fresh ginger <note>requested by Cao Cao in the preceding passage</note>. He also reported, "I found the Lord's messenger at the brocade market, and he has already been commanded to increase the purchase by two bolts."

After more than a year had passed, the Lord’s messenger returned, and he had indeed brought two extra bolts of brocade. When questioned about it, the messenger said, "On such-and-such a day of a certain month last year, I saw a man at the market who gave me your command, My Lord."

Later, the Lord went to the near outskirts of the city, accompanied by hundreds of officials and scholars. **Fang** <note>referring to Zuo Ci, using a shortened form of his courtesy name Yuanfang</note> then appeared, bringing only a single jug of wine and one **pound** <note>original: "斤" (jīn). A traditional unit of weight; one jin is roughly 500–600 grams.</note> of **dried meat** <term>original: "脯" (fǔ). Strips of salted, dried meat similar to jerky, used as a snack or travel food.</term>. With his own hand, he tilted the jug and served wine to all the officials. Among the hundreds of officials present, there was not one who did not become both drunk and full. 

The Lord found this strange and sent someone to investigate the cause. The investigator visited the local wine shops and discovered that all their wine and dried meat from the previous day had **vanished** <note>Zuo Ci had not created the food from nothing, but had used magic to "borrow" it from local merchants without payment.</note>. 

The Lord was furious and secretly intended to kill **Fang**. While **Fang** was seated in the Lord’s presence, and just as they were about to arrest him, he stepped back into the wall and **suddenly** <note>original: "霍然" (huòrán). Describes an abrupt, startling change or disappearance.</note> vanished. The Lord then ordered his likeness to be drawn so he could be tracked. Someone spotted him in the marketplace and attempted to capture him, but the market...

### Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries, Volume 1

Everyone [pursuing him] took on the same appearance, so that no one could distinguish who was who. Later, people encountered **Ci** <note>Zuo Ci, the Daoist master and trickster</note> at the summit of **Yangcheng Mountain** <note>A mountain in modern-day Henan province, historically significant in Daoist traditions.</note>. They pursued him once more, and he fled into a flock of sheep.

**The Lord** <note>Referring to the warlord Cao Cao (155–220 AD).</note>, realizing that he could not be captured, ordered his men to call out to him from among the sheep, saying: "Lord Cao no longer seeks to kill you. He was merely testing your magical arts. Now that they have been proven, he only wishes to meet with you."

Suddenly, there was an old **ram** <term>original: "羝" (dī). A male sheep.</term> that knelt on its two front knees, stood upright like a human, and spoke, saying: "Is it really so sudden?" <note>original: "遽如許" (jù rú xǔ). An expression of skepticism or surprise, effectively asking "Why such a sudden change of heart?"</note> The men immediately said, "This sheep is the one!" and they rushed toward it.

At that moment, the entire flock of several hundred sheep all transformed into rams; they all knelt on their front knees, stood upright, and said: "Is it really so sudden?" Consequently, the men did not know which one to take.

**Laozi** <note>The semi-legendary founder of Daoism. This is a direct quote from Chapter 13 of the *Dao De Jing*.</note> said: "The reason I have great suffering is because I have a body. When I no longer have a body, what suffering could I have?" If those of the same ilk as Laozi can be said to be capable of being "without body," is that not a truly profound state?

<summary>Zuo Ci evades Cao Cao's men by transforming into a sheep and then causing an entire flock to mimic his human-like behavior, concluding with a philosophical reflection on the Daoist ideal of transcending the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Zuo Ci, Cao Cao, Yangcheng Mountain, transformation, Laozi, Dao De Jing, magic, sheep</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous accounts of supernatural masters in the "Sou Shen Ji." While the previous page detailed Zuo Ci's evasions of Cao Cao, this passage transitions to a different conflict between a secular ruler and a Daoist master: the warlord Sun Ce and the miracle-worker Yu Ji.</meta>

### In Search of the Supernatural

**Sun Ce** <note>Sun Ce (175–200 AD) was a powerful warlord and the elder brother of Sun Quan, the eventual founder of the Eastern Wu state.</note> intended to cross the Yangtze River to launch a surprise attack on **Xu** <note>Xuchang, then the seat of the imperial court under Cao Cao's control.</note>. He was traveling together with **Yu Ji** <note>A famous Daoist master and healer who gained a massive following among the common people and soldiers.</note>. At that time, there was a great drought, and the heat was scorching and severe.

**Ce** urged his various generals and soldiers to pull the boats forward quickly. He would often go out early in the person to supervise and press them. However, he saw that many of the officers and officials were gathered at **Ji’s** place. Because of this, **Ce** became incensed, saying: "Am I not as good as **Ji**? Is that why you hasten to attend to him first?"

He then ordered the arrest of **Ji**. When **Ji** was brought before him, **Ce** scolded and questioned him, saying: "The sky is parched and it does not rain. The waterways are difficult and treacherous, and we cannot pass through in a timely manner. That is why I go out early myself. Yet you do not share our grief and anxiety; instead, you sit comfortably in your boat, putting on the airs of a supernatural being <note>original: "鬼物態" (guǐ wù tài). Literally "ghost-thing behavior," implying Yu Ji was acting like a spirit or using deceptive magic.</note> and ruining the discipline of my troops. Today I must eliminate you!"

He ordered his men to bind **Ji** and place him on the ground, exposing him to the sun <term>original: "暴之" (pù zhī). The practice of exposing a ritual specialist or an effigy to the sun to force the heavens to grant rain.</term> to make him pray for rain. **Ce** declared: "If you can move Heaven so that it rains by midday, I shall pardon you. If not, I will carry out your execution."

A classical expression meaning that clouds gather from even the smallest areas until they cover the sky.</note>. Everyone expected that **Ji** <note>Yu Ji, the Daoist master mentioned on the previous page.</note> would be pardoned, and they went to congratulate and comfort him. After **Ce** had killed **Ji**, whenever he sat alone, he seemed to see **Ji** standing there beside him. He detested this in his heart and began to act quite erratically. # In Search of the Supernatural: Middle Volume

The King of Wu <note>Likely Sun Quan (r. 229–252 AD), the founding emperor of the Eastern Wu state.</note> several times sent messengers to inquire after **Yan’s** well-being. **Yan** would sometimes appear as a young boy and at other times as an old man. He never ate or drank, and he refused to accept any gifts or provisions sent to him. 

The King of Wu wished to learn **Yan's** magical arts <term>original: "術" (shù). Refers to specialized Daoist techniques, including alchemy, invisibility, and longevity practices.</term>, but **Yan** believed that because the King kept so many concubines in his inner chambers, he was spiritually unfit. Consequently, even after many months, **Yan** refused to teach him. Enraged, the King ordered his guards to bind **Yan** and commanded his armored soldiers to fire their crossbows at him. When the bows were loosed, the ropes remained in place as if still binding a body, but **Yan** had vanished. No one knew where he had gone.

During the Wu period, there was also a man named **Xu Guang**, who used to perform his magical arts in the local marketplaces. He once begged for a melon from a vendor, but the owner refused to give him one. **Xu Guang** then asked for a single seed. He planted it in the ground using his staff, and in an instant, a melon sprouted. Its vines spread out, flowers bloomed, and fruit ripened. **Xu Guang** then picked the melons and ate them, sharing the rest with the onlookers. When the stunned onlookers eventually looked back at the vendor's stall, they realized that all the melons he had been selling had disappeared <note>This is a classic "theft by illusion" motif common in early Chinese supernatural tales.</note>. 

Whenever **Xu Guang** spoke of upcoming floods or droughts, his words proved very accurate. Once, while passing the gate of the **Great General Sun Chen** <note>A powerful and notoriously arrogant regent of Eastern Wu who was eventually executed for his tyranny.</note>, **Xu Guang** hiked up his robes and ran past quickly. When those standing nearby spat on the ground and trampled it <note>A gesture of contempt or a ritual to ward off evil.</note>, some asked him the reason...

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Volume 1

Therefore, he <note>Referring to the miracle-worker Xu Guang from the previous page.</note> replied: "The smell of flowing blood is foul and intolerable." 

When **Sun Lin** <note>A powerful and famously cruel regent of the Eastern Wu state who dominated the throne during the mid-3rd century.</note> heard this, he grew hateful and had him executed. When his head was severed, no blood came out. Later, after **Sun Lin** had deposed the Young Emperor <note>Sun Liang (r. 252–258 AD).</note> and installed **Emperor Jing** <note>Sun Xiu (r. 258–264 AD).</note>, he was preparing to board his carriage to pay respects at the imperial tombs. Suddenly, a violent wind rocked **Sun Lin's** carriage, causing it to tilt. He looked up and saw **Xuan** <note>The text here says "Xuan," likely referring back to the subject, though historical accounts of this specific event usually name Xu Guang. Given the next paragraph, the text may be blurring these two figures.</note> sitting atop a pine tree, clapping his hands and pointing, laughing at him. **Sun Lin** asked his attendants what they saw, but none of them could see anything. Shortly thereafter, **Emperor Jing** had **Sun Lin** executed.

**Ge Xuan**, whose courtesy name was **Xiaoxian**, received the *Scripture of the Nine-Elixir Liquid* <term>original: "九丹液仙經" (Jiǔ dān yè xiān jīng). An early Daoist alchemical text focused on the creation of elixirs for immortality.</term> from **Zuo Yuanfang** <note>Also known as Zuo Ci, a legendary transcendent and trickster known for defying Cao Cao.</note>. 

Once, while **Ge Xuan** was eating with a guest, the conversation turned to the subject of "transformations" <term>original: "變化" (biànhuà). The Daoist art of changing one's form or altering the nature of physical objects.</term>. The guest said: "When we are finished, perhaps the Master could perform a small feat for our amusement?" 

**Ge Xuan** replied: "Would you perhaps like to see something right now?" 

He then spat out the rice he was chewing. In an instant, the rice turned into hundreds of large bees that swarmed all over the guest’s body. However, they did not sting anyone. After a long while, **Ge Xuan** opened his mouth, and the bees all flew back inside. **Ge Xuan** then chewed and swallowed them as rice once again.

# Records of Searching for the Spirits

## Page 10

Therefore, while at a meal, he would point at toads and various crawling insects, as well as swallows, sparrows, and their kind, making them dance in time to a rhythm as if they were human. 

In winter, he would provide his guests with fresh melons and dates, and in summer he could bring forth ice and snow. Furthermore, he once had someone take several dozen coins and scatter them into a well. **Ge Xuan** placed a vessel <note>original: "噐" (qì); an archaic form of "器," referring to a container or bowl.</note> above the well and called out to them; the coins flew out of the well one by one and entered the vessel.

When he set out wine for guests, there was no need for anyone to pass the cups; the cups would travel to the guests of their own accord. If a guest did not finish their drink, the cup would not leave them.

He once sat upon a high tower with the **Lord of Wu** <note>Sun Quan (r. 222–252 AD), the founding emperor of the Eastern Wu state during the Three Kingdoms period.</note> and saw people making clay figures to pray for rain. The Emperor asked, "The common people long for rain; can it be obtained?" 

**Ge Xuan** replied, "Rain is easily obtained." 

He then wrote a talisman <term>original: "書符" (shū fú). A ritual practice in Daoism where mystical symbols are written on paper or silk to command spiritual forces, spirits, or natural elements.</term> and placed it within the local shrine. In an instant, the sky and earth grew dark and dim, and a great rain fell until the water overflowed. 

The Emperor then asked, "Are there fish in this water?" 

**Ge Xuan** again wrote a talisman and threw it into the water. After a brief moment, several hundred large fish appeared. He then ordered his people to prepare and cook them <note>original: "治之" (zhì zhī), which in this context means to clean and prepare the fish for a meal.</note>.

## Volume 1

**Wu Meng** was a man of Puyang. He served the state of **Wu** <note>One of the Three Kingdoms (222–280 AD).</note> as the Magistrate of Xi’an <note>Located in present-day Jiangxi province.</note>, and subsequently made his home in Fenning. 

By nature, he was extremely filial. He encountered the **Perfected Man** <term>Zhiren: In Daoist philosophy, a "True" or "Perfected" person who has achieved spiritual transcendence and harmony with the Dao.</term> **Ding Yi**, who bestowed upon him divine prescriptions. He also acquired secret methods and sacred talismans, and his Daoist arts became widely celebrated. 

He once witnessed a violent windstorm. He wrote a talisman and threw it upon the roof; a blue bird took it in its beak and flew away, and the wind immediately ceased. When someone asked the reason for this, he replied: 

“On the South Lake, there is a boat caught in this wind; a Daoist priest was pleading for rescue.” 

Upon verification, this was indeed found to be the case. 

When **Gan Qing**, the Magistrate of Xi'an, had been dead for three days, **Wu Meng** said, “His allotted span of years is not yet exhausted; I shall appeal his case to Heaven.” He then lay down beside the corpse for several days, after which he and the Magistrate both rose up together. 

Later, while leading his disciples back to **Yuzhang**, the river current was so rapid that people could not cross. **Wu Meng** then used the **white feather fan** in his hand to draw across the river water. The current flowed crosswise and a land path was formed. They walked across at a leisurely pace, and once they had crossed, the water returned to its original state. Those who watched were struck with terror and amazement. 

While he was staying in **Xunyang**, a violent wind suddenly rose at the home of the Military Consultant **Zhou**. **Wu Meng**...

**Yuanke** was a native of **Jiyin** <note>A commandery in what is now Shandong Province.</note>. He was a man of great beauty, and many people in his town wished to marry their daughters to him, yet **Yuanke** never took a wife. He spent his time cultivating fragrant herbs of five colors, and for several decades, he consumed their fruit. 

Suddenly, a divine moth of five colors alighted upon the fragrant herbs. **Yuanke** gathered it and placed it upon a cloth, and from it, silkworms were produced. When the time came for the silkworms to spin, a divine woman arrived one night to assist **Yuanke** in raising them. She also fed the silkworms the fragrant herbs. They produced one hundred and twenty cocoons, each as large as a jar <term>Weng: A large earthenware storage vessel or urn.</term>. It took six or seven days to reel the silk <note>The process of unwinding silk filaments from a cocoon.</note> from just a single cocoon. When the reeling was finished, the woman and **Yuanke** ascended to the heavens as immortals together, and no one knew where they went.

**Dong Yong** of the **Han Dynasty** was a native of **Qiancheng**. Having lost his mother at a young age, he lived alone with his father. They labored tirelessly in the fields, and **Dong Yong** used a **deer cart** <note>A small, narrow handcart or wheelbarrow, often used by the poor or those traveling through narrow paths.</note> to carry his father with him wherever he went.

When his **father died**, he had no means to pay for the burial, so he **sold himself as a slave** <note>In ancient China, selling oneself into debt bondage was a desperate measure to fulfill filial duties, such as providing a proper burial, which was considered essential for the spirit's peace.</note> to provide for the funeral rites. The master, recognizing his virtue, gave him ten thousand coins and sent him away. 

Once **Yong** had completed the **three-year mourning period** <note>The traditional duration of mourning for a parent in Confucian practice, signifying deep respect.</note>, he sought to return to the master to fulfill his duties as a slave. Along the way, he met a woman who said, "I wish to be your wife," and so she accompanied him.

The master said to **Yong**, "I gave that money to you as a gift."

**Yong** replied, "By your grace, my father’s remains were laid to rest. Though I am a man of lowly status, I am determined to work diligently and exert all my effort to repay your great kindness."

The master asked, "What skills does this woman possess?"

**Yong** replied, "She can weave."

The master said, "If that is so, simply have your wife weave **one hundred bolts** <term>Pi: A standard unit of measurement for cloth, approximately 40 yards in length.</term> of **fine silk** <term>Jian: A specific type of high-quality, double-threaded, and closely woven silk fabric.</term> for me."

Thereupon, **Yong’s** wife wove for the master’s household, finishing the entire task in only ten days. As she stepped out the door, she said to **Yong**:

-> "I am the **Weaver Girl of Heaven** <note>A celestial deity in Chinese mythology, often identified with the star Vega.</note>. Because your **filial piety** <note>original: "至孝" (zhì xiào) — refers to the ultimate or perfect degree of devotion to one's parents.</note> reached the highest degree, the **Celestial Emperor** ordered me to help you repay your debt." <-

Having finished speaking, she soared into the air and vanished, and no one knew where she went.

Originally, **Lady Gouyi** <note>Lady Zhao, a favorite concubine of Emperor Wu of the Han. Her title comes from "Gouyi" (Hooked Hand) because her hands were purportedly clenched in fists from birth until the Emperor opened them.</note> committed an offense and died as a result of a reprimand. After she was prepared for burial, her body did not emit a foul odor; instead, a **fragrance** could be smelled for more than ten leagues. 

She was subsequently buried at **Yunling** <note>The burial mound located in present-day Shaanxi province.</note>, and the Emperor mourned her deeply. However, suspecting that she was no ordinary human, he ordered the tomb to be opened and inspected. The coffin was found to be **empty with no body inside**; only a pair of shoes remained. One account says that when **Emperor Zhao** <note>The son of Lady Gouyi and successor to Emperor Wu.</note> ascended the throne and moved her burial site, the coffin was empty of a body, and only **silk shoes** <term>Sixie: Traditional shoes made of woven silk, often worn by the nobility.</term> remained there.

During the Han Dynasty, there was a woman named **Du Lanxiang** who claimed to be a native of **Nankang**. In the spring of the fourth year of the **Jianye** era <note>The text attributes this to the Han, but "Jianye" is an era name typically associated with the later Wu or Jin periods; such chronological discrepancies are common in ancient folk collections.</note>, she frequently visited **Zhang Chuan**. 

**Chuan** was seventeen years old when he looked out and saw her carriage outside his gate. A maidservant relayed a message: "I was sent by the **Holy Mother** <note>Likely referring to the Queen Mother of the West (Xi Wangmu), a major goddess in Chinese mythology.</note> to be bestowed as your mate; will you not respectfully comply?" 

**Chuan's** name had previously been changed to **Shuo**. **Shuo** called the girl forward to look at her; she appeared to be about sixteen or seventeen years old. When she spoke, she described matters that were **distant and ancient** <note>Original: 邈然久遠 (miǎorán jiǔyuǎn) — implying she possessed knowledge of the deep past far beyond a human lifespan.</note>. She was accompanied by two maidservants: the elder was named **Xuanzhi** and the younger was named **Song**.

Her carriage was inlaid with silver and mother-of-pearl, drawn by a **blue ox** <note>In Chinese mythology, the blue ox is a mount for immortals, most famously associated with the philosopher Laozi.</note>. Food and drink were all prepared within. She then composed a poem, which said:

> The Mother <note>original: "Amu" (阿母); referring to the Queen Mother of the West, the supreme goddess of the immortals.</note> resides upon the sacred peak,

In the year of the **Grand Orbit at the East, the Wood Rabbit** <note>original: "Dasui Dongfang Mao" (大嵗東方卯); a specific year in the sexagenary cycle, likely indicating she would return in a future year of the Rabbit.</note>, she would return to seek him. When Lanxiang was about to depart, Shuo asked her about the efficacy of prayers and sacrifices. Lanxiang replied, "**Eliminate demons.**" <note>original: "Xiomo" (消魔); a term used in early Daoist texts referring to internal purification and the casting off of worldly attachments or literal evil spirits.</note>

# The Jade Maiden Cheng-gong Zhi-qiong

During the **Jiaping era** <note>The reign period of 249–254 AD during the Cao Wei dynasty of the Three Kingdoms period.</note> of the Wei dynasty, there was a man named **Xian Chao** (whose courtesy name was Yiqi), who served as an **Assistant Official** <term>Yuan (掾): A subordinate administrative post in the local commandery government.</term> in Jibei Commandery. One night, while he was sleeping alone, he dreamed that a divine maiden came to follow him. 

She called herself a **Jade Maiden** <note>original: "Yunu" (玉女); a minor female deity or celestial attendant in the Daoist hierarchy.</note> from Heaven. A native of Dong Commandery, her surname was **Cheng-gong** and her courtesy name was **Zhi-qiong**. She told him that she had lost her parents early in life, and the Celestial Emperor, pitying her loneliness and hardship, had ordered her to descend to the mortal realm to find a husband. 

While Xian Chao was in the midst of this dream, his spirit felt a profound awakening. He admired her extraordinary beauty, which was unlike that of any ordinary person. When he awoke, he longed for her, and she remained in his thoughts as if she were simultaneously present and absent. 

After three or four such nights, she suddenly appeared openly in the light of day. She arrived in a **covered carriage** <term>Ziping (輜軿): A type of screened or walled carriage traditionally used by noblewomen for privacy.</term> followed by eight handmaidens, all dressed in robes of silk, gauze, and fine embroidery. Her countenance and form were like those of a flying immortal. 

She claimed to be eighty years old, yet she appeared to be a young woman of fifteen or sixteen. Upon her carriage were five sets of vessels—pots and flasks made of **blue and white lapis lazuli** <note>original: "Liuli" (瑠璃); at this time, this referred to colored glass or precious gemstones imported from the West, considered a material of the gods.</note>—containing various foods and drinks.

"I am a **Jade Maiden** <note>original: "Yunu" (玉女); a minor female deity or celestial attendant in the Daoist hierarchy.</note> from the heavens. I have been sent down to marry you, and thus I have come to follow you. It is not necessarily because of any merit from your previous lives, but rather that your spirit and your destiny have met the right time, making it proper for us to be husband and wife. 

I cannot bring you profound spiritual benefit, nor will I cause you harm. However, through our association, you shall always be able to drive light carriages and ride sturdy horses. For your meals, you shall always have exotic flavors and **strange delicacies** <term>Yishan (異饍): Rare or supernatural foods not found in the mortal world.</term>. For your clothing and daily needs, you shall always have **fine silks** <term>Zengsu (繒素): A general term for various types of silk fabrics, representing wealth.</term> in abundance. 

Even so, as I am a divine being, I will not bear you children. Nor do I possess a jealous nature; I will not interfere with the propriety or duties of your mortal marriage."

Thereupon, they became husband and wife. She presented him with a poem, which read:

Floating and drifting, rising and meeting <gloss>one version reads: "tangled"</gloss>, wandering <gloss>one version reads: "strolling"</gloss> through the clouds. The stones nourish the numinous fungi; a single blossom requires no moisture <gloss>one version omits the word "attain"</gloss>. Perfect virtue meets its appointed time; the immortals proclaim the Great Void. Resonance and response follow the movement of fate, seeking this union. To accept me brings honor to your five clans; to reject me brings **calamity** <term>Zi (菑): An archaic term for a disaster or misfortune sent down from the heavens.</term>.

This is the general gist of her poem. Its text continued...

...more than a hundred words, which cannot all be recorded here. She also annotated the **Book of Changes** <term>Yi (易): Also known as the I Ching, an ancient manual for divination and a cornerstone of Chinese philosophy.</term> in seven volumes, which included hexagrams and symbols and was categorized under the **Ren and Tuan** <term>Ren Tuan (壬彖): Specific technical systems of divination. "Ren" refers to the "Six Ren" (Liu Ren) system, and "Tuan" refers to the judgments on hexagrams.</term> systems. 

Because her commentaries contained both profound philosophical principles and the practical means to divine good and bad fortune, they were comparable to Yang Xiong’s **Supreme Mystery** <term>Taixuan (太玄): A complex work by the Han dynasty scholar Yang Xiong that attempted to synthesize the Book of Changes with Daoist and Confucian thought.</term> or Master Xue’s **Middle Classic** <note>original: "Xue Shi Zhongjing" (薛氏中經); a divinatory text attributed to the scholar Xue Shi, which has largely been lost to time.</note>. Chao was able to master all of their hidden meanings and used them for the practice of divination.

They lived together as husband and wife for seven or eight years. After Chao’s parents arranged for him to marry a mortal wife, the Jade Maiden would feast with him on separate days and sleep with him on separate nights. She arrived at night and departed at dawn, moving as swiftly as if in flight. Only Chao could see her; she was invisible to others. Even when they were in a darkened room, people would suddenly hear the sound of voices and often see traces of her presence, yet no one could perceive her physical form.

Later, when others grew suspicious and questioned him, Chao let the secret slip. The Jade Maiden then prepared to depart, saying:

"I am a divine being. Although I have shared this intimacy with you, I did not wish for other people to know of it. However, your nature is careless and indiscreet. Now that the details of our relationship have been exposed, I shall no longer associate with you. The bond we have cultivated over these many years..."

->**The Story of the Maiden Zhi Qiong**<-

"Our bonds of affection are not light, and to separate so suddenly—how could we not feel bitter sorrow? Yet circumstances dictate that it must be so. Let us each strive onward." 

She then called her attendants <term>shiyu (侍御): Celestial handmaidens or servants who wait upon a deity.</term> to serve wine. Amidst her sobbing, she opened a trunk and took out two sets of woven silk robes <note>original: "zhicheng qunshan" (織成裙衫); high-quality garments made from patterned silk brocade, often associated with the divine or the elite.</note> to give to Chao as a parting gift. She also presented him with a poem, then held him by the arm to bid him farewell, her tears falling in torrents. With a solemn air, she ascended her carriage and departed as swiftly as if in flight.

Chao’s grief and longing weighed on him for many days, until he was nearly overcome by exhaustion. 

Five years after her departure, Chao was commissioned on a regional mission to the capital, Luoyang. When he arrived at the foot of Mount Yu <note>Mount Yu (Yushan) is located in the Jibei region, in what is now Shandong province.</note>, he was traveling westward along a path. Looking far ahead to the end of a winding road, he saw a carriage and horses that resembled those of Zhi Qiong. 

He hurried forward, and indeed it was her. He drew back the carriage curtains, and they gazed at one another, their hearts a mixture of joy and sorrow. He took the reins in his left hand and held the mounting cord <term>sui (綏): A strap or cord used to help a passenger climb into a carriage.</term> to assist her, and they traveled together to Luoyang. There they established a household once more, rekindling their former love. 

By the middle of the Taikang era <note>The Taikang era (280–289 CE) was the reign of Emperor Wu of the Jin Dynasty, a period of temporary unification.</note>, she was still with him. However, they did not see each other every day; instead, she visited only on the third day of the third month, the fifth day of the fifth month, and the seventh day of the seventh month <note>These dates represent significant traditional festivals: the Double Third (Spring), the Dragon Boat Festival, and the Qixi Festival (the meeting of the Cowherd and Weaver Girl).</note>...

From that time onward, on the ninth day of the ninth lunar month and on the fifteenth day of the moon, she would regularly descend from the heavens to visit him. She would always stay overnight and then depart the following morning.

## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 2

->Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty<-

<margin>In Search of the Supernatural</margin>
The Marquis of Shouguang was a man who lived during the reign of Emperor Zhang of the Han dynasty <note>Emperor Zhang reigned from 75–88 CE</note>. He possessed the power to summon and judge <term>he</term> <note>original: "劾" (hé). A term derived from legal vocabulary meaning "to impeach" or "to bring charges." In this context, it refers to a ritual power to command and prosecute spirits as if they were subjects of a court</note> all manner of ghosts and demons, forcing them to bind themselves and reveal their true forms. 

In his village, there was a woman who had fallen ill because of a demon. The Marquis issued a summons against it, and a giant snake several <term>zhang</term> <note>a unit of measurement; one zhang is approximately 3.3 meters or 11 feet</note> in length was found dead outside the gate. The woman was thereafter restored to health. 

There was also a large tree inhabited by a spirit; anyone who rested beneath it would die, and even birds flying over it would fall from the sky. The Marquis summoned the spirit of the tree, and in the height of summer, the tree suddenly withered and its leaves fell. A giant snake, seventy to eighty feet long, was found hanging dead among the branches. 

When Emperor Zhang heard of these feats, he summoned the Marquis and questioned him. The Marquis replied, "It is indeed as you have heard." The Emperor said, "Beneath the palace halls, there is a strange occurrence. After midnight, several people are often seen dressed in crimson robes <note>original: "絳衣" (jìngyī)</note> with their hair hanging loose and disheveled <note>original: "披髮" (pīfà); in Chinese tradition, loose hair often signaled madness, mourning, or the presence of the supernatural</note>, carrying torches and following one another. Are you able to summon and judge them?" The Marquis replied:
