"This minor creature is easily dispelled," the Marquis said. The Emperor had secretly commanded three men to wait and act as the apparitions. However, once the Marquis performed his ritual, the three men immediately collapsed to the ground, breathless. The Emperor, startled, cried out, "These are not demons! I was merely testing you!" He then ordered the spell to be undone.

Another account says that during the reign of Emperor Wu of Han <note>reigned 141–87 BCE</note>, monsters were frequently seen beneath the palace halls, dressed in crimson robes with disheveled hair, following one another while carrying candles. The Emperor asked Liu Ping <note>a scholar and practitioner of the occult</note>, "Can you rid us of these?" Liu Ping replied, "I can." He then threw a **Green Talisman** <term>qingfu</term> <note>original: "青符" (qīngfú). A ritual charm used in Daoist exorcism, often associated with the element of Wood and the power of the East</note> toward them. Several "ghosts" were seen collapsing onto the ground. The Emperor, shocked, said, "I was only testing you!" The men were then revived.

Fan Ying lived in seclusion on Mount Hu. Once, a violent wind rose from the southwest. Fan Ying said to his students, "There is a great fire raging in the city of Chengdu." He then took a mouthful of water and sprayed it out. Later, he ordered his students to record the exact time and date. Some time later, travelers arrived from the region of Shu <note>modern-day Sichuan province</note> and reported: "On that day, a massive fire broke out, but a cloud suddenly rose from the east and a heavy rain fell in an instant, and the fire was extinguished."

## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 2

->Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty<-

In the region of Min <note>modern-day Fujian province</note>, there was a person named Xu Deng. Originally a woman, Xu had transformed into a man. He and Zhao Bing of Dongyang were both exceptionally skilled in the occult arts <term>fangshu</term> <note>original: "方術" (fāngshù). A broad category of supernatural techniques including alchemy, divination, and ritual magic</note>.

During a time of war and chaos, the two encountered one another at a stream. Each boasted of his own abilities. Xu Deng first cast a spell to bind <term>jin</term> <note>original: "禁" (jìn). Literally "to prohibit" or "to forbid." In this context, it refers to ritual magic used to stop or restrain physical forces</note> the stream so that its waters ceased to flow. Zhao Bing followed by binding the willow trees so that they grew into a ladder. The two men looked at one another and laughed. Because Xu was older, Zhao served him as his master.

Later, after Xu Deng passed away, Zhao traveled east to the capital of Chang'an. At first, the common people did not know of his powers. One day, Zhao climbed onto the roof of a thatched house, set up a tripod cauldron <term>ding</term>, and began to cook <term>cuan</term> <note>original: "爨" (cuàn). To light a fire for the purpose of cooking</note>. The homeowner was shocked and terrified, but Zhao merely smiled and did not respond. When he was finished, the roof was not damaged at all.

On another occasion, Zhao Bing stood by the water and requested passage on a ferry, but the boatman refused him. Zhao then unfurled a silk canopy <note>a carriage umbrella or screen</note> and sat within it. He let out a long, piercing whistle <note>original: "長嘯" (chángxiào). A Daoist breathing and vocal technique believed to summon spirits or influence the elements</note> to call forth the wind, and he sailed across the churning currents. 

Following this, the common people were struck with awe and followed him as if returning to their own kin. The Magistrate of Chang'an, however, detested how Zhao "deluded the masses" and had him arrested and executed. The people established a shrine for him in Yongkang. To this day, neither flood-dragons nor stinging gnats <note>original: "蛟蚋" (jiāoruì). "Jiao" are mythical water serpents that cause floods; "Rui" are mosquitoes or midges. The shrine is said to be a sanctuary from both pests and natural disasters</note> are able to enter the site.

<summary>This page recounts the legendary feats of Xu Deng and Zhao Bing, two masters of magic who demonstrated power over nature, including gender transformation, stopping rivers, and fire-walking. It concludes with the martyrdom of Zhao Bing and the establishment of a protective shrine in his honor.</summary>
<keywords>Xu Deng, Zhao Bing, occult arts, ritual binding, Chang'an, Yongkang shrine, supernatural transformation, flood-dragons</keywords>

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections

## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 2

Xu Deng and Zhao Bing held the virtues of purity and frugality in high esteem. In their rituals to worship the spirits, they used only water flowing from the east and shaved strips of mulberry bark to serve as dried meat <term>fu</term> <note>original: "脯" (fǔ). Traditionally, these were thin strips of dried meat used in offerings; here, the practitioners use bark to demonstrate their asceticism</note>.

When Chen Jie visited various deities, the Lord of the Eastern Sea presented him with a gift: a single short jacket <term>ru</term> <note>original: "襦" (rú). A waist-length jacket or tunic</note> woven in a vibrant green.

Bian Hong of Xuancheng served as a military officer <term>lingxiao</term> <note>original: "領校" (lǐngxiào). A rank responsible for overseeing soldiers or administrative tasks</note> in Guangyang. When his mother passed away, he returned to his family home to observe the mourning period. The diviner Han You <note>Han You was a famous master of occult arts known for his ability to perceive spiritual disturbances and omens</note> went to pay his respects. As the day reached its end, Han You emerged from the house and commanded his attendants to pack their belongings at once, insisting they depart that very night.

His attendants said, "The sun has already set, and we have dozens of miles to travel through thick grass and wilderness. Why must we leave so urgently?"

Han You replied, "This entire place is covered in blood. How could we possibly remain here?"

Despite the family's earnest efforts to persuade him to stay, Han You would not be moved. That same night, Bian Hong was suddenly <note>original: "欻" (chuā). An onomatopoeia or adverb describing a sudden, lightning-fast occurrence</note> seized by a violent madness. He strangled his two sons and killed his wife. He then took a blade and hacked at his father and two servant girls, all of whom sustained wounds. Following the slaughter, he fled and went missing for several days. He was eventually discovered in the forest in front of his residence, where he had already died by hanging himself <term>zijing</term> <note>original: "自經" (zìjīng). A formal historical term for suicide by hanging</note>.

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections

## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 2

Ju Daolong was highly skilled in the arts of illusion. He once recounted that Master Huang of the Eastern Sea <note>original: "東海人黃公" (Dōnghǎi rén Huáng Gōng). A legendary figure often cited in the history of Chinese theater and wrestling</note> was an expert at magical restraints, capable of subduing snakes and commanding tigers. Master Huang always wore a knife of red gold and wrapped his head in a red silk cloth. However, he was prone to excessive drinking. At the end of the Qin dynasty, a white tiger appeared in the region of the Eastern Sea. An imperial decree was issued for Master Huang to go and suppress the beast using his red knife. Because his magical arts failed to work <note>likely due to his physical decline or the "exhaustion" of his spiritual power through alcohol</note>, he was ultimately killed by the tiger.

Xie Jiu once hosted a banquet for guests. He took a talisman written in vermilion ink <term>zhu shu fu</term> <note>original: "朱書符" (zhū shū fú). Talismans written in red ink were believed to hold more spiritual power than those in black</note> and threw it into a well. Suddenly, a pair of carp leapt out of the water. He immediately ordered them to be prepared as *kuai* <term>kuai</term> <note>original: "膾" (kuài). A dish of finely sliced or minced raw fish</note>. Every guest seated at the table was able to receive a helping of the fish.

<column-break/>

During the Yongjia era <note>307–313 AD</note> of the Jin dynasty, a foreigner from India <term>tianzhu huren</term> <note>original: "天竺胡人" (Tiānzhú húrén). 'Tianzhu' refers to ancient India; 'Huren' was a general term for people from Central or South Asia</note> crossed the Yangtze River. This man possessed magical skills; he could cut off his own tongue and then reattach it, and he could also breathe fire. People from the surrounding areas gathered in large numbers to watch him. When he was about to cut his tongue, he would first stick it out to show the guests. Then, he would slice it off with a knife until blood covered the ground. He would then take the tongue, place it in a vessel, and pass it around for everyone to see. Looking at the tongue—

...the front half of his tongue was still there. Shortly after, he took the severed piece back, held it in his mouth to reattach it, and sat for a moment. When the onlookers looked at his tongue, it appeared exactly as it had before; they could not tell if it had truly been severed or not. 

As for his feat of "mending the severed" <note>original: "續斷" (xùduàn). A common category of magic involving the restoration of cut objects.</note>, he would take a piece of silk cloth and have another person hold one end while he held the other. They would then cut the cloth right through the middle. Afterward, he took the two severed pieces and held them together. Upon inspection, the silk cloth was once again a single continuous piece, with no sign of the cut. At the time of the demonstration, many were skeptical, believing it to be a mere illusion <term>huan</term> <note>original: "幻" (huàn). In this period, the term referred specifically to Indian conjuring arts or "phantasmagoria."</note>. However, upon testing the remains, they found it had indeed been a real, severed piece of silk.

Regarding his fire-breathing: he first placed a medicinal substance in a vessel. He then took a spark of fire and mixed it with millet sugar <note>original: "黍糖" (shǔ táng). This was likely a form of maltose used as a fuel or binding agent for the combustible "medicine."</note>. After blowing and breathing on the mixture several times, he opened his mouth, and it was filled with flames. He then used these flames to ignite a stove for cooking, proving it was real fire. Furthermore, he took items such as written paper, ropes, and threads and threw them into the fire. The crowd watched together as the objects were completely consumed by the flames. Yet, when he poked through the ashes and lifted them out, they were the original objects, completely unharmed.

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections

## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 2

<page-num>Four</page-num>

Fan Xun, the King of Funan <term>Funan</term> <note>original: "扶南" (Fúnán). An ancient kingdom located in the Mekong Delta, covering parts of modern-day Cambodia and Vietnam.</note>, kept tigers on a mountain. When people were accused of crimes, he would throw them to the tigers. If the tigers did not devour them, they were pardoned. For this reason, the mountain was named "Big Bug" <note>original: "大蟲" (dàchóng). An ancient Chinese colloquialism or euphemism for a tiger.</note>, and it was also known as the "Great Spirit." 

The King also kept ten crocodiles. If someone was accused of a crime, they would be thrown to the crocodiles. If the crocodiles did not eat them, they were granted a pardon. It was believed that the crocodiles would never eat those who were innocent; thus, there was a designated "Crocodile Pond." 

Furthermore, the King would sometimes have water boiled until it was scalding. He would then throw a gold finger-ring into the boiling water and command the accused to reach in and retrieve it. If the person was upright and honest, their hand would not be scalded; however, if they were guilty, their hand would burn and wither the moment it touched the water.

***

Jia Peilan, who had been a serving girl to Lady Qi <note>Lady Qi was a high-ranking and ill-fated consort of Emperor Gaozu, the founder of the Han Dynasty.</note>, later left the palace and became the wife of Duan Ru, a man from Fufeng. She recounted that during her time in the palace, they often entertained themselves with stringed and wind instruments, songs, and dances. They would compete to wear "exotic attire" <note>original: "妖服" (yāofú). Literally "demon clothes" or "strange garments." In this context, it refers to extravagant, non-traditional, or auspicious ritual costumes used to mark special occasions.</note> to celebrate auspicious times. 

On the fifteenth day of the tenth month, they would all enter the Temple of the Spiritual Woman. There, they would offer sacrifices of pork and millet to please the gods, play the flute, strike the *zhu* <term>zhu</term> <note>original: "筑" (zhù). An ancient Chinese stringed instrument that was played by striking the strings with a stick, similar to a hammered dulcimer but held like a lute.</note>, and sing the hymn "To the Higher Spirit."

...dances. After the songs, they would link arms and stomp the ground to keep time, singing "The Red Phoenix Comes." This was a shamanistic custom <term>shamanistic custom</term> <note>original: "巫俗" (wūsú). These were folk religious practices, often involving rhythmic movement and chanting to invoke or welcome spirits.</note>. 

On the seventh day of the seventh month, they would gather at the Hundred Children Pond to perform music from Khotan <term>Khotan</term> <note>original: "于闐" (Yútián). An ancient Iranian Buddhist kingdom on the Silk Road, famous for its music, which was considered exotic and prestigious in the Chinese capital.</note>. When the music concluded, they used five-colored threads to bind one another, a practice they called "interconnected ribbons."

On the fourth day of the eighth month, they would go out through the northern door of the carved chambers to sit beneath the bamboo and play the game of Go <term>Go</term> <note>original: "圍基" (wéijī). An archaic term for Weiqi, the ancient strategy board game.</note>. The winner was believed to enjoy good fortune for the remainder of the year, while the loser was destined to suffer from illness. To avoid this fate, the loser would take silk threads and pray to the North Star for longevity; only then would they be exempt from the misfortune.

On the ninth day of the ninth month <note>This refers to the Double Ninth Festival, a time associated with autumn and warding off danger.</note>, they wore sprigs of dogwood <term>dogwood</term> <note>original: "茱萸" (zhūyú). Specifically the Cornelian cherry, whose strong scent was believed to drive away pestilence and evil spirits.</note>, ate mugwort cakes, and drank chrysanthemum wine, as these acts were said to grant people long life. When the chrysanthemums were in full bloom, they would gather the stalks and leaves, mixing them with glutinous millet to brew the wine. The brew was not ready for drinking until the ninth day of the ninth month of the following year; hence it was called "chrysanthemum wine."

On the first "Dragon" day <note>original: "上辰" (shàngchén). The first day of the Dragon in the lunar calendar, usually occurring in early spring.</note> of the first month, they went to the edge of the pond to wash and purify themselves, and they ate mugwort cakes to exorcise evil spirits <term>exorcise evil spirits</term> <note>original: "祓妖邪" (fú yāoxié). A ritual cleansing intended to remove spiritual impurities and bad luck.</note>. On the Double Third Festival <term>Double Third</term> <note>original: "上巳" (shàngsì). Traditionally the first "Snake" day of the third lunar month, a festival of spring cleansing and social gatherings by the water.</note>, they performed music beside flowing streams. In this manner, they observed such customs throughout the entire year.

<summary>This page details the ritual calendar of the Han Dynasty palace as recounted by a former servant, describing seasonal music, games of chance for health, and the consumption of medicinal wine and herbs to ensure longevity and ward off evil.</summary>

...there.

In the time of Emperor Wu of the Han dynasty, he showed great favor to Lady Li. After the lady passed away, the Emperor’s longing for her was never-ending. An occultist <term>occultist</term> <note>original: "方士" (fāngshì). These were practitioners of esoteric arts, alchemy, and spiritual methods who were frequently employed by the Han emperors to seek immortality or communicate with spirits.</note> from the state of Qi named Li Shaoweng claimed he could summon her spirit. 

One night, he set up a series of curtains and lit bright candles, then instructed the Emperor to sit in a separate tent. Looking from a distance, the Emperor saw a beautiful woman sitting inside the curtains, her appearance identical to that of Lady Li. She rose and paced within the enclosure and then returned to her seat, yet the Emperor was not permitted to go near to look at her closely. This caused the Emperor’s sorrow and emotion to intensify even further, leading him to compose a poem:

->Is it she? Is it not?<-
->I stand and gaze from afar.<-
->How graceful, how slow—why does she come so late?<-

He then ordered the various musicians of the Music Bureau <term>Music Bureau</term> <note>original: "樂府" (Yuèfǔ). An imperial department responsible for collecting and performing music for court ceremonies and entertainment.</note> to set the poem to strings and sing it.

A year or so later, the man himself passed away. When his family went to bury him and opened the family tomb, they saw the hem of the robe resting beneath the lid of the wife’s coffin.

## Page Sixty-Two

At that time, both men reported seeing a woman around thirty years of age. She was wearing a head-wrap of green brocade <note>original: "青錦束頭" (qīngjǐn shùtóu). Brocade was a luxury fabric, indicating her high status even in the afterlife.</note>, a purple and white lined gown <term>lined gown</term> <note>original: "袷裳" (jiá cháng). The accounts of the two men matched perfectly, without them having consulted one another. Consequently, the grave was opened, and the clothing of the deceased was found to be exactly as they had described.

Xiahou Hong <term>Xiahou Hong</term> <note>original: "夏侯弘" (Xiàhóu Hóng). A figure of the Jin Dynasty famous in *zhiguai* (anomaly) literature for his supernatural perceptions.</note> claimed that he could see ghosts and converse with them. The horse ridden by Xie Shang <term>Xie Shang</term> <note>original: "謝尚" (Xiè Shàng). A famous general and refined scholar of the Eastern Jin period; he held the title 'General Who Guards the West'.</note>, General of the West, suddenly died, causing the General extreme distress. Xie said to Hong, "If you are able to bring this horse back to life, then I shall believe you truly do see ghosts." 

Hong departed and remained away for a long time. Upon his return, he said, "The deity of the local temple took a liking to your horse and therefore took its spirit; but it shall now return to life." As Shang sat facing the dead horse, a horse suddenly came galloping in from outside the gate. It reached the horse's carcass and then vanished into it. Immediately, the horse was able to move.

...[ready to] set out, Xie said, “That I have no heir is a punishment upon my own person.” For some time, Hong had nothing to report, saying:

“The ones I have seen lately are but minor ghosts; they surely cannot discern the root cause of this matter.”

Later, he suddenly encountered a ghost approaching in a brand new carriage, accompanied by about ten followers wearing robes of blue silk cloth. Hong stepped forward and seized the ox's nose <note>original: "提牛鼻" (tí niú bí). This refers to grabbing the bridle or the ring in the ox's nose to halt the carriage.</note>. The person inside the carriage said to Hong, “Why do you obstruct my path?”

Hong replied, “I have a question to ask. Xie Shang <term>Xie Shang</term> <note>original: "謝尚" (Xiè Shàng). A prominent official and general of the Jin Dynasty known for his refined tastes.</note>, General of the West <term>General of the West</term> <note>original: "鎮西將軍" (Zhènxī Jiāngjūn). A high-ranking military title indicating he was in charge of the western frontier.</note>, has no sons. This gentleman is of such refined character and excellent reputation that it would be wrong to allow his ancestral sacrifices to be severed <term>ancestral sacrifices</term> <note>original: "絕祀" (jué sì). In ancient China, failing to produce a male heir meant the family line ended and no one would perform the ritual offerings to the ancestors, which was considered a spiritual catastrophe.</note>.”

The person in the carriage was visibly moved and said, “The one you speak of is indeed my son. When he was young, he had a liaison with a household maidservant and swore a solemn oath never to marry another. However, he later broke that vow. Now, this maid has died and brought her grievance to Heaven; this is the reason he has no sons.”

Hong reported the entirety of this to Xie, who remarked, “When I was young, such an event did indeed occur.”

Later, while in Jiangling <note>original: "江陵" (Jiānglíng). A historical city located in what is now Hubei Province.</note>, Hong saw a great ghost carrying a spear and a halberd, followed by minor...

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections: Volume Two

Several ghosts appeared before Hong. Filled with fear, he stepped off the road to avoid them. Once the larger ghosts had passed, he caught one of the smaller spirits and asked, "What are those objects you carry?"

The spirit replied, "We use these spears and halberds to kill people. If they strike the heart or the abdomen, the victim never fails to die instantly."

Hong asked, "Is there a way to treat this sickness?"

The ghost said, "If you apply a poultice of black-boned chicken <term>black-boned chicken</term> <note>original: "烏雞" (wū jī). Specifically the Silkie chicken, which has black skin and bones; it has been used in Chinese medicine for centuries to treat "cold" or "spirit-based" illnesses.</note>, the patient will recover immediately."

Hong then asked, "Where are you traveling to now?"

The ghost replied, "We are heading to the provinces of Jing and Yang <note>original: "荆、揚" (Jīng, Yáng). These were two vast administrative regions in ancient China, covering much of the central and lower Yangtze River basin.</note>."

At that time, a plague of heart and abdominal pain broke out, spreading day after day; no one who caught it survived. Hong then taught the people to slaughter black-boned chickens and apply them as poultices to the affected areas. Of every ten people treated, eight or nine were saved. The fact that practitioners today always use black-boned chicken poultices to treat sudden evil-affliction <term>evil-affliction</term> <note>original: "中惡" (zhòng è). A traditional diagnostic term for a sudden, violent illness or stroke believed to be caused by a direct hit from a malevolent spirit or "ghostly wind."</note> is entirely due to Hong's intervention.

## In Search of the Supernatural, Volume 3

### Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty <note>original: "晉 干寶 撰" (Jìn Gān Bǎo zhuàn). Gan Bao was a historian and official who compiled these tales to prove the existence of spirits.</note>

During the Yongping era <note>58–75 AD, during the reign of Emperor Ming of the Han Dynasty.</note> of the Han Dynasty, Zhongli Yi (whose courtesy name was Zi’a) of Kuaiji served as the Chancellor of Lu <note>original: "魯相" (Lǔ xiàng). Lu was the home state of Confucius; the Chancellor was the highest civil official in the region.</note>. Upon arriving at his post, he took thirteen thousand coins from his own private funds and gave them to the Household Official, Kong Xin, to repair the carriage of the Master <note>original: "夫子" (fū zǐ). A respectful title referring specifically to Confucius.</note>. He personally entered the temple to wipe the ritual tables, the mats, the swords, and the shoes.

A man named Zhang Bo was clearing the weeds below the hall when he discovered seven jade discs <term>jade discs</term> <note>original: "玉璧" (yù bì). Flat, circular jade ornaments with a hole in the center, used in ancient China to symbolize heaven and high moral status.</note> in the earth. Bo hid one of them in his robes and reported the other six to Yi. Yi ordered the Master of Records to place the six discs in front of the ritual table.

In the hall where Confucius used to teach, there was an urn hanging at the head of the bed. Yi summoned Kong Xin and asked, "What kind of urn is this?"

Xin replied, "It is the Master’s urn. On the back, there is writing in cinnabar <term>cinnabar</term> <note>original: "丹書" (dān shū). Red ink made from powdered cinnabar, often used for sacred texts or seals to signify importance and longevity.</note>; no one has ever dared to open it."

Yi said, "The Master was a sage. The reason he left this urn and intended it to be hung..."

## In Search of the Supernatural, Volume 3

### Continued: The Relics of Confucius

Within the urn was a message intended to be **shown to later worthies**. Upon opening it, they found a text written on plain silk <note>original: "素書" (sù shū). Plain, un-dyed silk was a common medium for important manuscripts before the widespread use of paper.</note> which said: 

"In later generations, the one who repairs my books will be Dong Zhongshu <note>179–104 BC; a famous Han scholar who promoted Confucianism as the state ideology.</note>. The one who protects my carriage, wipes my shoes, and opens my scroll-tube will be Zhongli Yi of Kuaiji."

The text further noted that there were **seven** jade discs hidden within the wall. Knowing this, Yi immediately summoned Zhang Bo for questioning. 

"The wall contained seven discs," Yi said. "Why did you hide one of them?" 

Zhang Bo kowtowed in fear and produced the missing disc.

### The Prescience of Duan Yi

Duan Yi (whose courtesy name was Yuanzhang) was a native of Xindu in Guanghan <note>Modern-day Xindu District, Chengdu, Sichuan Province.</note>. He was a dedicated student of the *Book of Changes* and was profoundly skilled in the art of <term>Wind Corners</term> <note>original: "風角" (fēng jiǎo). An ancient form of divination that involved interpreting the direction, strength, and sound of the winds to predict the future or hidden events.</note>. 

A certain student came to study under him for many years. Eventually, the student believed he had fully mastered the essential techniques and requested leave to return to his home village. As a parting gift, Yi prepared a medicinal ointment and a letter written on bamboo slips <note>original: "簡書" (jiǎn shū). Before paper, records were often kept on bound strips of bamboo.</note>. He sealed both inside a tube and told the student:

"If you find yourself in an emergency, open this and look."

When the student reached Jiameng <note>A strategic pass and ferry crossing in northern Sichuan.</note>, he became embroiled in a dispute with a local official over the order of crossing the ferry. In the heat of the argument, the official struck the student's servant, breaking the skin on his head. 

The student opened the tube and found the letter. It read: 

"Upon arriving at Jiameng, you will fight with an official. For the one whose head is broken, wrap the wound with this ointment." 

The student followed these instructions, and the servant's wound healed immediately.

<summary>This page illustrates the theme of prophetic knowledge, featuring a hidden message from Confucius that accurately names his future caretakers and a diviner, Duan Yi, who foresees a specific roadside accident.</summary>

# In Search of the Supernatural

### The Haunting of Zang Zhongying

Zang Zhongying, a native of the Right Fufeng region <note>An administrative area near the imperial capital, modern-day Shaanxi province.</note>, served as an **Imperial Censor** <note>original: "侍御史" (shì yù shǐ). A high-ranking official responsible for maintaining discipline and supervising the conduct of other bureaucrats.</note>. His household became the site of many bizarre and unsettling events. Whenever his family prepared food and set the table, foul dust and filth would fall from nowhere into the pots, polluting the meal. At other times, when the food was nearly cooked, the cooking pot would suddenly vanish, and no one could discover where it had gone.

Weapons and crossbows began to move as if they had a will of their own. Fires would spontaneously ignite inside storage chests and baskets; curiously, while the clothing inside would be completely incinerated, the baskets themselves remained entirely unharmed. One morning, all the women and maidservants in the house simultaneously lost their mirrors. A few days later, the mirrors were suddenly hurled from beneath the main hall into the courtyard, while a voice called out: 

"I am returning your mirrors!"

On another occasion, a granddaughter only three or four years of age went missing. The family searched for her in a panic but could not find her anywhere. Two or three days later, she was finally discovered crying in the garden, hidden beneath a pile of refuse. Incidents of this nature happened more than once.

There was a man from Runan named Xu Jishan, who was widely known for his mastery of <term>divination</term> <note>original: "卜卦" (bǔ guà). An ancient practice using various methods—such as stalks or shells—to interpret the will of spirits or the hidden causes of misfortune.</note>. Zang Zhongying sought his counsel. After performing a reading, Xu Jishan said:

"There is an old, dark-furred dog <note>original: "老青狗" (lǎo qīng gǒu). The word 'qing' can mean blue, green, or a dark, grizzled black; in Chinese folklore, long-lived animals were often thought to gain supernatural or shapeshifting powers.</note> in your home. One of your inner attendants named **Yixi** is conspiring with this creature to cause these disturbances. If you truly wish to put an end to this, you must kill the dog and dismiss Yixi, sending him back to his home village."

Zhongying followed this advice, and the strange occurrences ceased immediately. Later, he was promoted to Chief Clerk to the Grand Commandant and eventually transferred to the state of Lu.

<summary>This passage describes a series of supernatural disturbances in the home of the official Zang Zhongying, which are revealed by a diviner to be the work of a domestic servant and an elderly dog with malevolent powers.</summary>
<keywords>Zang Zhongying, Xu Jishan, divination, poltergeist, Han Dynasty, In Search of the Supernatural, Soushen Ji, animal transformation</keywords>

# Omens

Grand Commandant Qiao Xuan, whose courtesy name was Gongzu, was a native of the Liang Kingdom. Early in his career, he served as the Chief Clerk to the Minister over the Masses <note>One of the "Three Excellencies," the highest-ranking civil officials in the Han dynasty; the Chief Clerk was a powerful administrative position.</note>.

At the end of the fifth lunar month, he was sleeping near the middle gate of his residence. After midnight, he suddenly saw the eastern wall turn brilliant white, shining as brightly as if a door had been thrown open. He called out to his attendants, asking them about the light, but none of them could see anything unusual. 

He then stood up and went to the wall himself, <term>feeling and touching</term> <note>original: 捫摸 (mén mō). This implies a physical investigation to ensure the wall was still solid and not actually broken open.</note> the surface with his hands. The wall felt perfectly normal, just as it always had. However, when he returned to his bed, he saw the light once more. He became deeply afraid.

His friend Ying Shao <note>A famous scholar and author of the *Fengsu Tongyi* (Comprehensive Meaning of Customs), who lived c. 140–206 AD.</note> happened to visit him at that time. During their conversation, Qiao Xuan told him of the occurrence. Shao said:

"In your region, there is a man named Dong Yanxing. He is the maternal grandson of Xu Jishan <note>The master diviner mentioned in the previous account.</note>. In his ability to explore the profound, seek out the hidden, and understand the spiritual transformations of the world, even the great masters Mu Meng or Jing Fang <note>Famous Han dynasty cosmologists known for their expertise in the *I Ching* and interpreting natural disasters.</note> could not surpass him. However, he is by nature somewhat prickly and feels it is beneath him to be associated with common <term>diviners</term> <note>original: 卜筮 (bǔ shì). Specifically those who use tortoise shells and yarrow stalks.</note>. He is currently visiting his teacher, Wang Shumao. I suggest you go and invite him."

In a short time, they returned together. Gongzu received him with the utmost humility and respect.

<summary>Grand Commandant Qiao Xuan witnesses a mysterious, door-shaped light on his wall that only he can see. On the advice of his friend Ying Shao, he recruits the temperamental but brilliant diviner Dong Yanxing to investigate.</summary>
<keywords>Qiao Xuan, Ying Shao, Dong Yanxing, divination, supernatural omens, Han Dynasty, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Qiao Xuan (Gongzu) and his consultation with the diviner Dong Yanxing regarding a mysterious white light that appeared on his wall.</meta>

<page-num>Three</page-num>
<meta>Catchword: History of Central Ni</meta>

A grand feast was prepared and the mats were laid; as the wine cups were circulated, Yanxing introduced himself, saying, "I am but a humble scholar from the countryside, possessing no extraordinary talents. However, since your gifts are so generous and your words so kind, if there is indeed some <term>extraordinary omen</term> <note>original: 𨁏踔 (zhuò chuō), meaning something that leaps beyond the ordinary or a supernatural anomaly.</note> that can be discerned, I am willing to take up the matter." Gongzu politely demurred his modesty several times.

Yanxing then listened to the account and said: "Your Honor, you have indeed encountered a strange occurrence: a white light as bright as an open door. However, it shall bring no harm. During the first ten days of the sixth month, at the first crowing of the cock, you will hear the sound of weeping from the house to your south. This will be a sign of good fortune. By the time of the Autumn Festival, you will be transferred to lead a northern commandery that has 'Gold' in its name. Your rank will eventually reach that of General and the Three Excellencies."

Gongzu replied: "With an omen as strange as this, I am busy enough trying to protect my clan from disaster; how could I dare hope for what I never even imagined? You are surely just being kind to me."

On the ninth day of the sixth month, before dawn, the Grand Commandant <term>Yang Bing</term> <note>A prominent official of the Eastern Han (92–165 AD), known for his "three non-desires": no desire for wine, women, or wealth.</note> suddenly passed away. On the seventh day of the seventh month, Qiao Xuan was appointed the <term>Governor of Julu</term> <note>The prophecy is fulfilled here because the first character of Julu, *Ju* (鉅), contains the radical for "gold" or "metal" (金) on its left side.</note>. Later, he served as the <term>General Who Crosses the Liao</term> <note>A military post responsible for defending the northern borders against the Xianbei and Xiongnu tribes.</note> and eventually ascended to the rank of the <term>Three Excellencies</term> <note>The three highest-ranking officials in the imperial government, acting as the Emperor's closest advisors.</note>.

**Vocabulary:**
Yang Bing, Governor of Julu, General Who Crosses the Liao, Three Excellencies, Extraordinary Omen

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Volume Three

### The Account of Guan Lu

**Guan Lu**, whose <term>courtesy name</term> <note>A name given at adulthood to be used by peers and superiors as a mark of respect.</note> was Gongming, was a native of Pingyuan. He was highly skilled in <term>Yi divination</term> <note>original: 易卜 (yì bǔ), divination based on the *I Ching* or *Book of Changes*.</note>. 

**Wang Ji**, the Grand Protector of Anping (whose courtesy name was Boyu and who hailed from Donglai), experienced several strange occurrences at his home. He requested that Lu perform a <term>stalk divination</term> <note>original: 筮 (shì), a method of divination using milfoil or yarrow stalks to form hexagrams.</note>. When the hexagram was completed, Lu said:

"Your Honor’s hexagram indicates that a woman of low station in your household gave birth to a boy who, as soon as he touched the ground, ran directly into the **stove** and died. Furthermore, upon your bed, there appeared a **large snake** holding a brush in its mouth; both the old and the young of the house witnessed this together, and after a short while, it departed. Additionally, a **bird** flew into the room and fought with a **quail**; the quail died and the bird left. Because of these three omens, Ji was greatly shocked."

Ji said: "The heights to which your profound insight reaches are indeed like this! I humbly ask you to divine their fortune or calamity." 

Lu replied: "These do not portend any other disaster. It is simply that these guest <gloss>one version reads 'official'</gloss> quarters have been inhabited for a very long time, and the <term>mountain and water demons</term> <note>original: 魑魅罔兩 (chī mèi wǎng liǎng), a collective term for various shapeshifting spirits and sprites of the wilderness.</note> are together causing these oddities. As for the child who was born and immediately ran, he did not run by his own power; it was the demon <term>Song Wuji</term> <note>A legendary fire spirit or essence of fire, often said to appear in the form of a man or a child in a red robe.</note> who led him into the stove. The large snake holding a brush was merely an old <term>clerk</term> <note>original: 書佐 (shū zuǒ), a subordinate administrative scribe who handled documents.</note>. As for the bird fighting the quail—"

<summary>This page introduces the master diviner Guan Lu and details his uncanny ability to identify specific domestic hauntings at the home of Governor Wang Ji. Guan Lu explains these bizarre events not as omens of doom, but as the activity of long-resident nature spirits and ghosts.</summary>

<keywords>Guan Lu, Wang Ji, I Ching, divination, Song Wuji, fire spirits, omens, Three Kingdoms period, demonology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the diviner Guan Lu and the official Wang Ji regarding strange omens in the governor's residence. The previous page established three specific omens: a boy running into a stove, a snake with a brush, and a bird fighting a quail.</meta>

"...is merely an old **attendant**. Truly, the upright essence of the divine cannot be harmed by demons, and the transformations of the ten thousand things cannot be halted even by the <term>Way</term> <note>original: 道 (dào), the fundamental principle or path of the universe.</note>. The floating spirits of the distant past are simply part of the fixed cycles of nature. Currently, within the hexagram, I see the images of these events, but I do not see calamity. Thus, we know these are merely figures of borrowed identities, not omens of disaster or blame. You have no cause for worry."

"In ancient times, a pheasant crowed upon the sacrificial tripod of **Gaozong** <note>The temple name of Wu Ding (r. 1250–1192 BCE), a King of the Shang Dynasty. The pheasant's crow was traditionally seen as a warning that he turned into an opportunity for reform.</note>, and a mulberry tree grew by the steps of **Taiwu** <note>Another Shang Dynasty King. Legend says a mulberry and a grain tree grew in his court overnight; he treated it as an omen to cultivate his virtue, leading to a prosperous reign.</note>. Yet, because the wild bird cried once, Wu Ding became the 'Exalted Ancestor,' and because the mulberry and grain appeared briefly, Taiwu brought about a great restoration. How do we know these three events are not similarly auspicious?"

"I wish for your Honor to find peace, cultivate your virtue, and act with magnanimity. Do not let these spectral tricks tarnish your innate nature."

Later, there was indeed no misfortune. Ji was later promoted to <term>Military Governor</term> of **Annan**, and eventually returned to his home district. Later, in **Taiyuan**, he asked Master Luo about the time he discussed those strange occurrences for Governor Wang, saying: "The old <term>scribe</term> <note>original: 書佐 (shū zuǒ), a minor clerical official in the local administration.</note> became the snake, and the old attendant..."

<summary>Guan Lu reassures Wang Ji that the strange omens are merely harmless spirits of the past and encourages him to use them as motivation to cultivate virtue, citing historical precedents from the Shang Dynasty. The account concludes with Wang Ji’s eventual promotion and a retrospective discussion of the events in Taiyuan.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the diviner Guan Lu’s explanation to Wang Ji. Having established that the strange events in the residence are not necessarily omens of doom, Guan Lu now addresses the nature of metamorphosis itself, arguing that human transformation into animals is a natural, if rare, phenomenon reflected in the cosmic order.</meta>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 3

...and below [the person] became a bird. Since these were all originally human, why regard their transformation as a sign of something base or lowly? Did you see this in the images of the hexagram lines <term>yaoxiang</term> <note>original: 爻象. The visual symbols of the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) that represent the shifting states of the universe.</note>, or did it arise from your own thoughts?

To speak lightly is easy; but if one does not understand human nature and the <term>Way of Heaven</term> <note>original: 天道 (tiāndào). The fundamental natural order of the universe.</note>, how could they turn away from the hexagram images and rely solely on their own whims?

The transformation of the "ten thousand things" <note>A phrase representing all phenomena and living beings in the world.</note> has no constant form. The strange mutations of human beings have no fixed physical substance. Sometimes the great becomes small, or the small becomes great; there is truly no inherent superiority or inferiority. The transformation of all things follows a single, consistent principle.

For this reason, **Gun** of the **Xia** was the father of a Son of Heaven <note>Gun was the father of Yu the Great, the legendary founder of the Xia Dynasty.</note>, and **Prince Ruyi of Zhao** was the son of **Emperor Gao** of **Han** <note>Emperor Gaozu (r. 202–195 BCE), the founder of the Han Dynasty. Ruyi was his favorite son, who was later murdered.</note>. Yet **Gun** was transformed into a **Yellow Bear** <note>original: 黃能 (huáng néng). Ancient lore suggests Gun was transformed into this creature after his death; some scholars interpret the term as a three-legged turtle.</note>, and **Ruyi** became a **Dark Dog** <note>According to legend, Ruyi's spirit returned as a large blue-black dog to haunt his murderer, Empress Lü.</note>. These individuals held the most honored positions, yet they were transformed into the likeness of creatures with spots and beaks.

Furthermore, the snake corresponds to the <term>Earthly Branches</term> of *Chen* and *Si* <note>Positions in the Chinese sexagenary cycle; *Si* is specifically associated with the zodiac sign of the Snake.</note>, and the bird is the very **Essence of the Sun** <note>original: 太陽之精 (tàiyáng zhī jīng). This refers to the mythical three-legged crow that lives inside the sun, representing solar energy.</note>. These are the bright manifestations of rising shadows and the flowing radiance of the daylight. If the **Clerk** and the **Attendant** each transformed their physical forms into a snake and a bird based on these signs, is it not a fitting transition?

<summary>Guan Lu explains that human transformation into animals is a natural phenomenon governed by the "Way of Heaven," citing historical figures of high rank who became animals. He argues that the snake and bird are actually noble symbols associated with the zodiac and the sun, rather than lowly omens.</summary>

When **Guan Lu** <note>A famous master of divination and physiognomy during the Three Kingdoms period (220–280 CE).</note> arrived in **Pingyuan**, he saw a young man named **Yan Chao** whose facial features suggested he was predestined to die young. **Yan’s** father then begged **Lu** to find a way to extend his son's life. **Lu** said:

"When you return home, seek out a keg of clear wine and one pound of dried venison. On a **Mao day** <note>original: 卯日 (mǎorì). The fourth day in the traditional twelve-day cycle of the Chinese calendar.</note>, go to the large mulberry tree south of the wheat-reaping field. There, you will find two men playing **Go** <term>weiqi</term> <note>original: 圍碁. An ancient strategic board game of territory and capture.</note>. While they are focused on their match, simply pour the wine and set out the venison. When they finish drinking, refill the cups; continue this until the supplies are exhausted. If they ask you anything, simply bow to them and do not speak. There will surely be someone there to save you."

**Yan** followed these instructions and went to the spot. He did indeed find two men engaged in a game of **Go**. **Yan** placed the venison and poured the wine before them. The men were so absorbed in their game that they simply drank the wine and ate the meat without looking up.

After several rounds of the game, the man sitting on the north side suddenly noticed **Yan** was there and asked, "Why are you here?" 

**Yan** responded only with a bow. 

The man sitting on the south side then spoke: "We have just now finished drinking his wine and eating his meat; how can we be so heartless <note>original: 寧無情乎. Literally "how could we be without feeling?" This implies a debt of hospitality that must be repaid.</note>?" The one sitting on the north...

The person sitting to the north is the **North Dipper** <note>original: 北斗 (Běidǒu). In Chinese cosmology, this deity/constellation governs the registers of death.</note>, and the person sitting to the south is the **South Dipper** <note>original: 南斗 (Nándǒu). This deity/constellation governs the registers of life.</note>. The **South Dipper** presides over life, while the **North Dipper** presides over death. All people, from the moment they are conceived in the womb, pass from the South Dipper's jurisdiction to that of the North Dipper. Therefore, all prayers for the extension of life must be directed toward the North Dipper."

In the household of the **Magistrate of Xindu**, the women were plagued by terror and fell ill one after another. Their heads are within the wall, while their feet are outside the wall. The one holding the spear is responsible for piercing the head; this is why the heads of the sufferers feel heavy and painful, to the point where they cannot be lifted. The one holding the bow...

"The arrows are aimed at the chest and abdomen, which is why the residents feel a sharp, hanging pain in their hearts and cannot eat or drink. From that point on, there was no more illness in the house.

In the district of **Licao**, a commoner named **Guo En** (whose courtesy name was **Yibo**) and his two brothers all suffered from a **chronic wasting disease** <term>zhuji</term> <note>original: 疰疾. Within that tomb resides a female ghost. She is likely either your father's sister or the wife of your father's brother. Long ago, during a time of famine, someone coveted a few liters of rice and pushed her into a well, crushing her..."

...[the stone made a] clattering sound. He pushed a large stone down, crushing her head. This lonely soul <note>original: "孤魂" (guhun) — a spirit with no descendants to offer it sacrifices, often becoming a restless or malevolent ghost.</note>, suffering such bitter injustice, cried out her grievance to Heaven.

**Chunyu Zhi**, courtesy name **Shuping**, was a native of Lu in **Jibei**. He possessed a profound and contemplative nature. Having been a scholar in his youth, he was highly skilled in the **art of divination by the Book of Changes** <term>yishi</term> <note>original: "易筮". A method of fortunetelling using yarrow stalks to consult the I Ching.</note> and the **techniques of suppression and warding** <term>yansheng</term> <note>original: "厭勝". A form of ancient esoteric magic used to overcome evil influences or spirits through talismans and symbolic rituals.</note>.

**Liu Rou** of **Gaoping** was lying in bed one night when a rat bit the middle finger of his left hand. Disturbed by this ominous event, he consulted **Zhi**. **Zhi** performed a divination for him and said: "The rat originally intended to kill you, but it failed. We should make it die in your place instead." He then used **vermilion ink** <term>zhushu</term> <note>original: "朱書". Red ink made from cinnabar, traditionally believed to have the power to repel demons and stabilize the soul.</note> to write the character for **"field"** (田) on **Liu's** wrist, three inches behind the horizontal crease. The character was approximately one and a fifth inches square. He instructed **Liu** to sleep with his hand exposed that night. By morning, a large rat was found dead on the floor before him.

The family of **Bao Yuan** from **Shangdang** suffered from many deaths, frequent illnesses, and crushing poverty. **Chunyu Zhi** performed a divination for him and declared: "The placement of your residence is improper; this is why you are in such distress. To the northeast of your house stands a large mulberry tree. You should go directly to the market; upon entering the gate, count..."

"...sell her twenty miles to the southeast. Be careful not to haggle over the price; only then can this **demon** <term>yao</term> <note>original: "妖". In this context, it refers to a malevolent omen or a supernatural entity masquerading as a human.</note> be removed."

**Pu** <note>Guo Pu (276–324 AD).</note> secretly sent someone to buy this maidservant at a low price. He then cast a **talisman** <term>fu</term> <note>original: "符". A Daoist ritual object, usually a strip of paper or wood inscribed with celestial script, used to command spirits or provide protection.</note> into the well. Thousands of figures dressed in **red clothing** appeared and, one by one, threw themselves into the well. The master of the house was greatly pleased. **Pu** took the maidservant and departed. Several weeks later, **Lujiang** fell. <note>Guo Pu correctly foresaw the invasion of Lujiang. By "exorcising" the maid, he was actually securing her safety and his own departure before the city was captured.</note>

The horse ridden by **Zhao Gu** suddenly died. He felt great grief and cherished the animal deeply, so he consulted **Guo Pu**. **Pu** said:

"You may send several dozen men carrying bamboo poles thirty miles to the east. There they will find a forest on a hill; have them stir and beat the trees. A certain creature will emerge, which they must quickly capture and bring back."

Following his instructions, they obtained a creature resembling an **ape**. When they brought it through the gate, the creature saw the dead horse and leaped toward it. It went to the dead horse’s head and began **breathing and sucking** at its nostrils. After a short moment, the horse was able to get up. It moved with its usual vigor, neighing, eating, and drinking as it always had. The creature from before was never seen again. **Gu** <note>Referring to Zhao Gu.</note> found this extraordinary and **generously rewarded** [Guo Pu].

<summary>This page illustrates the miraculous powers of Guo Pu, showing how he used his foresight to save a life during the fall of Lujiang and his ability to summon a mythical creature to resurrect a general's horse.</summary>
<keywords>Guo Pu, Zhao Gu, Lujiang, talismans, resurrection, mythical animals, Jin Dynasty, divination</keywords>

## Provisions

**Gu Qiu**, the **Administrative Assistant** <term>biejia</term> <note>original: "別駕". A high-ranking provincial official who traveled in a separate carriage from the Governor.</note> of **Yangzhou**, had an elder sister who fell ill when she was only ten years old. By the time she was over fifty, she was still suffering, so he asked **Guo Pu** to perform a **divination** <term>shi</term> <note>original: "筮". A method of divination using yarrow stalks to consult the I Ching (Book of Changes).</note>.

**Pu** obtained the hexagram **Great Excess** <term>daguo</term> <note>Hexagram 28. It often symbolizes a structure under too much stress, like a beam sagging.</note> changing into **Ascending** <term>sheng</term> <note>Hexagram 46. It symbolizes upward movement or growth.</note>. The prophetic verse said:

"The hexagram of **Great Excess** signifies an inauspicious destiny. At the tombs, the withered willow tree bears no blossoms. Shaken by wandering souls, one sees a **dragon carriage**. The body is burdened by heavy weights, entangled by **demonic evils** <term>yaoxie</term> <note>original: "妖邪". Malignant supernatural influences.</note>. The cause stems from the cessation of sacrifices and the killing of a **spirit snake**. This is not your own fault, but a flaw inherited from your ancestors. When judging this by the frame of the hexagram, what can be done?"

**Gu Qiu** then investigated his family's past. He discovered that an ancestor had once cut down a large tree and found a massive snake inside, which they killed. Soon after, the girl had fallen ill. After her illness began, thousands of birds would often circle and soar above their house. Everyone found this strange but did not know the reason. Once, a local farmer passing by the house looked up and saw a **dragon pulling a carriage**. It was dazzling with five brilliant colors and was of an extraordinary size. After a short while...

<summary>Guo Pu uses the I Ching to diagnose a woman's fifty-year illness as the result of an ancestral sin—the killing of a sacred snake. The divination is confirmed by historical family accounts and sightings of supernatural phenomena like dragon carriages.</summary>

<keywords>Guo Pu, Gu Qiu, divination, I Ching, Great Excess hexagram, dragon carriage, spirit snake, ancestral karma, Yangzhou</keywords>

### Finally Extinguished

**Fang Shubao** of **Yixing** contracted a **typhoid fever** <note>original: "傷寒". In ancient Chinese medicine, this term referred to a broad category of acute febrile diseases caused by external cold.</note> and was on the verge of death. He asked **Guo Pu** to perform a divination for him, but the result was inauspicious. **Pu** instructed him to find a **white cow** to perform a **suppression ritual** <term>yan</term> <note>original: "厭" (yanshi). A magical practice intended to suppress or ward off evil influences through symbolic substitution.</note> to save him.

They searched but could not find one; only **Yang Zixuan** possessed a white cow, and he refused to lend it. **Pu** then performed a ritual to summon one. That very day, a massive white cow appeared from the west and walked straight to **Shubao's** bedside. **Shubao** was startled by the sight, and his illness was immediately cured.

---

**Fei Xiaoxian** of **Xichuan** <note>Western Sichuan.</note> was skilled in the art of **leather-map divination** <term>guige</term> <note>original: "軌革". A rare form of divination involving leather materials or specific geometric patterns.</note>, and his fame was known throughout the world. A man from **Dasheng** named **Wang Min**, who had traveled to **Chengdu** for **trade** <term>huozhi</term>, sought a reading from him.

**Xiaoxian** told him:

"When told to stay, do not stay.
When told to wash, do not wash.
A **picul** <term>shi</term> of grain, when pounded, yields three **bushels** <term>dou</term> of rice <note>In traditional measurements, 10 dou make 1 shi. If only 3 dou of rice remain, it implies there is an unusually large amount of 7 dou of husk or bran (kang). This is often a riddle pointing to a specific location or name.</note>.
Encounter the light and live; encounter the darkness and die."

He warned him repeatedly, telling him that simply reciting these words would be enough. **Min** memorized them. As he set out on his journey home, he encountered a great rainstorm and went to rest under a shelter where many other travelers had gathered.

<summary>This page concludes an account of Guo Pu using a summoned white cow to miraculously cure a dying man and introduces Fei Xiaoxian, who provides a merchant with a series of life-saving cryptic warnings.</summary>
<keywords>Guo Pu, Fei Xiaoxian, Wang Min, divination, typhoid healing, white cow ritual, prophecy, Soushen Ji, Sichuan history</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Wang Min, a merchant who received a cryptic prophecy from the diviner Fei Xiaoxian. On the previous page, Xiaoxian gave Min three warnings: "When told to stay, do not stay," "When told to wash, do not wash," and a riddle about a "picul of grain yielding three bushels of rice."</meta>

# Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries: Volume 3

Min thought to himself: "The master said, 'When told to stay, do not stay.' Could this be what he meant?" He then braved the rain and left the shelter. Not long after, the building collapsed; he alone managed to escape.

Now, Min’s wife had been having an affair with a neighbor. Desiring to be together for the rest of their lives, they waited for Min to return so they could carry out a plot to poison him. When Min finally arrived home, the wife made a pact with her lover, saying: "The one who has freshly bathed tonight is my husband." 

As they were about to eat, she urged Min to bathe and change his **headcloth and comb** <term>jinzhi</term> <note>original: "巾櫛". A term for the tools of grooming, here referring to the act of washing and dressing up for the evening.</note>. 

Min suddenly realized the meaning of the prophecy: "The master said, 'When told to wash, do not wash.' Is this not exactly the situation?" He resolutely refused to follow her suggestion. His wife, in a fit of rage and without thinking, went and bathed herself instead. In the middle of the night, she was killed <note>presumably by her lover, who mistook the bather for the husband</note>. 

When Min woke and discovered her, he cried out in alarm to the neighbors. They all came to see, but no one could fathom how the murder had occurred. Consequently, Min was imprisoned and subjected to **interrogation by torture** <term>kaoxun</term> in the **criminal court** <term>yu</term>. He was unable to defend himself or prove his innocence. 

As the **Prefect** <term>junshou</term> <note>The administrative head of a commandery or prefecture.</note> was recording the final report of the case, Min wept and said, "If I must die, then I die. My only regret is that the words of **Xiaoxian** <note>The diviner Fei Xiaoxian.</note> have ultimately failed to come true." 

The attendants reported these words to the Prefect. The Prefect ordered that the execution be stayed and summoned Min to question him further.

<summary>This page narrates how Wang Min narrowly escapes death twice by following Fei Xiaoxian’s cryptic warnings, only to be framed for his wife's murder. The story highlights the tension between prophetic truth and judicial misunderstanding.</summary>

<keywords>Wang Min, Fei Xiaoxian, divination, prophecy, criminal trial, wrongful imprisonment, Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries</keywords>

### In Search of the Supernatural
**Page Ten**

The **Prefect** <term>junshou</term> asked, "Who are your neighbors?" 

Min replied, "One is named **Kang Yi** <note>original: "康乙"</note>."

The Prefect then sent his men to arrest the man. "The person who killed your wife," he declared, "must be this man." And indeed, upon investigation, it was proven true. 

The Prefect later said to his subordinates: "If **one picul of grain** <term>shi</term> <note>A unit of dry measure roughly equal to 100 liters.</note> is pounded and yields only **three bushels of rice** <term>dou</term> <note>A unit of dry measure, one-tenth of a picul.</note>, does that not leave seven parts of **chaff** <note>The word for chaff or husks is "kang" (糠), which sounds identical to the neighbor's surname, Kang (康). The riddle implies that what is "left over" from the rice is the key to the killer's identity.</note>? Thus, it had to be Kang Yi." 

Through this, the case was cleared and the truth brought to light. It was truly a testament to the efficacy of a life being saved when a person in trouble encounters a brilliant and perceptive official.

---

**Wei Zhao** was a commoner from Hongshou Pavilion in **Ruyin** <note>A commandery in present-day Anhui Province.</note>. He was highly skilled in the **Classic of Changes** <term>Yi</term> <note>Also known as the *I Ching*, an ancient Chinese divination text used to predict future events based on hexagrams.</note>. 

As he lay dying, he wrote a message upon a wooden tablet and handed it to his wife, saying: "After I pass away, a great famine will strike. Even if you find yourself in the direst of straits, you must be careful not to sell our house. In the spring of the fifth year after my death, an imperial messenger will arrive and stay at this pavilion. His surname will be **Gong** <note>original: "龔"</note>. This man owes me a debt of gold. When he arrives, take this tablet to him and demand payment. Do not forget these words."

After Wei Zhao died, a great famine did indeed occur. His wife was frequently on the verge of selling the house due to her poverty, but each time she remembered her husband’s warning and stopped herself. When the appointed time arrived, a messenger named Gong did indeed stop at the pavilion to rest. The wife then brought the tablet to him to demand the debt. The messenger held the tablet, but did not...

<summary>This page concludes the tale of a clever Prefect solving a murder through a wordplay riddle and begins the story of Wei Zhao, a diviner who predicts a famine and a future debt-collector to protect his family.</summary>

The person addressed by the message said, "Throughout my life, I have never owed your husband any money. Why would he write such a thing?"

The wife replied, "When my husband was near death, he **wrote upon the wooden tablet** with his own hand and gave me these instructions. I would not dare to speak falsely."

The **Messenger** <note>original: "使者" — likely an official or a specific friend of Wei Zhao who was an expert in divination.</note> pondered in silence for a long time and then suddenly understood. He ordered that **milfoil stalks** <term>shishi</term> <note>Dried yarrow stalks used in ancient Chinese divination rituals to generate the hexagrams of the *I Ching*.</note> be brought to perform a divination. When the hexagram was complete, he clapped his hands and sighed with admiration: "How marvelous! **Master Wei** <note>Wei Zhao</note> possessed brilliant insight, yet he lived in such obscurity that no one heard of him. He truly could be called one who 'mirrored failure and success' and 'perceived the depths of good and bad fortune.'"

He then told the wife: "I do not owe him any gold. Rather, your worthy husband possessed gold himself. He knew that after his death you would face a temporary period of poverty, so he hid the gold to wait for a time of peace. The reason he did not tell you or the children was for fear that the gold would be exhausted too early and your hardships would never end. Knowing that I am **skilled in the Changes** <term>Yi</term> <note>Referring to the *I Ching*, or *Classic of Changes*.</note>, he wrote upon the tablet to convey his meaning through a riddle that only a practitioner could solve."

"There are **five hundred catties** <term>jin</term> of gold, stored in **green jars** <term>ying</term> and covered with **bronze cases** <term>xia</term>. They are buried at the eastern end of the main hall, one **zhang** <term>zhang</term> <note>A unit of length equal to ten *chi*, roughly 3.3 meters or 11 feet.</note> from the wall and **nine feet** <term>chi</term> <note>A traditional Chinese foot, roughly 33 centimeters. Nine *chi* is approximately 3 meters.</note> deep."

The wife returned and dug at the spot, and it was found exactly as he had said.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Wei Zhao, a diviner who used a riddle and his knowledge of the I Ching to hide gold for his family, ensuring it would only be found during a time of peace by an expert who could decode his message.</summary>
<keywords>Wei Zhao, I Ching, divination, hexagram, milfoil stalks, hidden gold, Ruyin, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

## Volume Three

->**The gold was obtained exactly as the divination predicted.**<-

**Han You**, whose courtesy name was **Jingxian**, was a native of Shu in Lujiang. He was exceptionally skilled in the art of divination and practiced the techniques of **Apotropaic Magic** <term>yasheng</term> <note>original: "厭勝" — A ritual practice used to suppress or ward off evil spirits through charms or symbolic actions.</note> as taught by the masters **Jing Fang** and **Fei Zhi** <note>Jing Fang and Fei Zhi were legendary figures of the Han Dynasty known for their specialized interpretations of the *I Ching* and esoteric occult sciences.</note>.

The daughter of a man named **Liu Shize** had been afflicted by a **demonic possession** <term>mei</term> <note>original: "魅" — A type of spirit or demon, often associated with wild animals or forests, capable of enchanting or sickening humans.</note> for many years. Exorcists and shamans had attempted to cure her through aggressive prayers and rituals. They had scoured the countryside, searching through **empty tombs** and the ruins of ancient cities, where they captured dozens of wildcats and alligators, yet the girl’s illness did not improve.

**Han You** performed a divination using **milfoil stalks** <term>shi</term>. Following the results, he ordered a cloth bag to be made. He waited until the girl was about to suffer another fit, then he stretched the bag open and positioned it between the **window and the shutters**. **Han You** then closed the doors and focused his **inner energy** <note>original: "作氣" — Likely referring to a focused cultivation of *qi* or a ritualistic shout to drive out spirits.</note> as if he were physically driving something out of the room. 

In a very short time, the bag was seen to swell up significantly, as if someone were blowing air into it. Suddenly, the bag burst open. 

As the girl’s fit broke out again with great intensity, **Han You** constructed two new bags made of **leather** <note>original: "皮囊" — Sturdier than cloth, suggesting the spirit was powerful enough to break through the first vessel.</note>, placing one inside the other. He set them up at the window just as he had done before. The bags once again filled and swelled to their limit. This time, **Han You** quickly tied the mouth of the bags shut and hung them from a tree.

After about twenty days, the bags gradually began to shrink. When they were finally opened and inspected, they contained **two pounds of fox fur**. The girl’s illness was thereafter completely cured.

<summary>This page narrates the miraculous healing of Liu Shize's daughter by the diviner Han You, who used leather bags to trap and manifest the fox spirits possessing her. It highlights the transition from Wei Zhao's legacy to the exploits of other masters of the occult arts.</summary>

<keywords>Han You, divination, exorcism, fox spirits, apotropaic magic, Jing Fang, Fei Zhi, Lujiang, demonic possession</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections
## Volume Three

**Yan Qing** of **Kuaiji** <note>Kuaiji was a historical region in modern-day Zhejiang Province, renowned for its scholars and masters of the occult.</note> was exceptionally skilled in **divination** <term>bushi</term> <note>original: "卜筮" — A general term for methods of foretelling the future using tortoise shells (bu) or milfoil stalks (shi).</note>. 

A fellow villager named **Wei Xu** intended to travel to the east. It was a year of famine, and the roads were plagued by many bandits and marauders. Wei Xu requested that **Yan Qing** perform a divination for his journey. 

**Yan Qing** warned him, saying: "You must be cautious. You absolutely must not travel to the east. You will certainly encounter a violent harm, yet it will not be a case of robbery."

**Wei Xu** did not believe the warning. 

**Yan Qing** then said: "Since you insist on not staying, you ought to have a means to **ward off the evil** <term>rang</term> <note>original: "禳" — A ritual or sacrifice intended to avert disaster or expel a malignant influence.</note>. You should seek out a white male dog from the home of a widow living outside the western city wall, and tie it to the front of your boat."

They searched for such a creature but were only able to find a **spotted dog** <term>bo</term> <note>original: "駁" — Refers to a coat of mixed or contrasting colors, such as a brindled or piebald pattern.</note>; there were no pure white ones to be found. 

**Yan Qing** remarked: "A spotted one will suffice, though I regret that its color is not pure. There will remain a small amount of residual **harm** <note>original: "小毒" — Literally 'small poison,' referring here to the lingering spiritual toxicity or misfortune that the ritual could not fully absorb.</note> that will only affect the livestock and their kind. You need not worry any further."

When **Wei Xu** was halfway through his journey, the dog suddenly cried out in extreme distress, as if it were being beaten by someone. By the time they went to check on it, the dog was already dead, having vomited more than a **gallon** <note>original: "斗" — A *dou*, a traditional unit of dry measure roughly equivalent to ten liters, used here hyperbolically to describe a large amount.</note> of black blood. 

That same evening, several white swans at **Wei Xu’s** country villa died for no apparent reason. However, **Wei Xu’s** family remained **safe and unharmed** <note>original: "無恙" — A term signifying that one is free from illness, disaster, or misfortune.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts how the diviner Yan Qing used a ritual involving a sacrificial dog to protect the traveler Wei Xu from a supernatural threat. While the dog and some livestock perished, the ritual successfully absorbed the "poisonous" misfortune intended for the family.</summary>

<keywords>Yan Qing, Wei Xu, divination, ritual protection, Kuaiji, animal sacrifice, supernatural harm</keywords>

## Volume Three

**Hua Tuo** of **Pei Commandery**, whose courtesy name was **Yuanhua**, was also known by the name **Fu** <gloss>also written as **Ya** or **Xie**</gloss> <note>Hua Tuo (c. 140–208 AD) is arguably the most famous physician in Chinese history. The gloss refers to variant characters used for his secondary name in different historical records.</note>. 

**Liu Xun**, the **Governor of Henei** <note>A high-ranking administrative official in the region of modern-day Henan.</note>, had a daughter nearly twenty years of age. She suffered greatly from a recurring ailment on her leg; inside her left knee, a **sore** <term>chuang</term> <note>original: "瘡" — A general term for a lesion, ulcer, or skin eruption.</note> would develop that was itchy but did not cause pain. This sore would heal for several dozen days and then break out again, a cycle that had persisted for seven or eight years. 

They sent for **Hua Tuo** to examine her. **Hua Tuo** said, "This is easily treated. You must obtain one **yellow dog** fed on rice bran and two **fine horses**." 

He instructed them to tie a rope around the dog's neck and have a rider on a galloping horse pull the dog. When the first horse became exhausted, they immediately switched to the second horse. After the horses had run for over thirty **li** <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly 500 meters.</note>, the dog was no longer able to walk. He then ordered men on foot to drag the dog further, totaling nearly fifty *li*. 

**Hua Tuo** then gave the daughter a **medicinal drink** <term>yaoyin</term> <note>original: "藥飲" — This likely refers to *Mafeisan*, the legendary anesthetic powder Hua Tuo is credited with inventing.</note>. The daughter immediately fell into a peaceful sleep and lost consciousness. He then took a large knife and cut open the belly of the dog near its hind legs. He positioned the incision of the dog toward the opening of the **sore**, holding it at a distance of two or three inches. 

After a brief moment, something resembling a **snake** began to emerge from the sore. **Hua Tuo** then took an **iron awl** <term>tiezhui</term> <note>original: "鐵椎" — A pointed tool or spike used here as a surgical instrument to secure the parasite.</note> and drove it horizontally through the creature...

<summary>A detailed account of the legendary physician Hua Tuo treating the Governor of Henei's daughter for a chronic, parasitic leg sore using an unusual method involving an exhausted dog as bait.</summary>
<keywords>Hua Tuo, Liu Xun, surgery, parasite, ancient medicine, Mafeisan, dermatology</keywords>

...the snake's head. The snake moved back and forth beneath the skin for a long while. They applied a medicinal ointment and powder into the **sore**, and in seven days, she was healed.

**Hua Tuo** was once traveling along a road when he saw a man suffering from a throat ailment. Though he craved food, he could not swallow it. The illness will naturally depart." They followed **Hua Tuo's** words, and the man immediately vomited up a single snake.

In Search of the Supernatural

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections

## In Search of the Supernatural, Volume 4

->Written by **Gan Bao** of the **Jin Dynasty**<-

The **Wind Lord** and the **Rain Master** are both stars. The **Wind Lord** <term>Fengbo</term> <note>original: "風伯"</note> is the **Winnowing Basket Star** <note>The "Ji" star constellation, located in Sagittarius; it was traditionally believed to control the winds.</note>. The **Rain Master** <term>Yushi</term> <note>original: "雨師"</note> is the **Net Star** <note>The "Bi" star constellation, located in Taurus; its appearance was said to herald heavy rain.</note>. The scholar **Zheng Xuan** <note>A prominent Confucian commentator of the Han Dynasty (127–200 CE).</note> states that they are the fourth and fifth stars of the **Literature Star** constellation <term>Wenxing</term> <note>original: "文星" — part of the Big Dipper</note>, namely the **Governor of the Interior** <term>Sizhong</term> and the **Governor of Fate** <term>Siming</term>. The **Rain Master** is also known by the names **Pingyi**, **Haoping**, or **Xuanming**.

**Zhang Kuan**, whose courtesy name was **Shuwen**, was a native of the **Shu Commandery**. During the reign of **Emperor Wu of Han**, he served as a **Palace Attendant**. While he was accompanying the Emperor to perform sacrifices at **Ganquan** and had reached the **Wei Bridge**, they encountered a woman bathing in the **Wei River**. Her breasts were seven feet long <note>A "foot" (chi) at this time was roughly 23 cm, suggesting breasts nearly 1.6 meters (over 5 feet) in length—a clear sign of a supernatural being.</note>. The Emperor, struck by this bizarre sight, sent an attendant to question her. The woman replied, "The man in the seventh carriage behind the Emperor knows where I have come from." At that time, **Zhang Kuan** was indeed in the seventh carriage. He responded to the inquiry, saying, "She is a **Heavenly Star**."

**King Wen** <note>The legendary founder of the Zhou Dynasty (c. 1112–1050 BCE), revered as a model of wisdom and virtue.</note> appointed **Taigong Wang** <note>Also known as Jiang Ziya, King Wen’s primary military strategist and a legendary figure in Chinese folklore.</note> as the **Magistrate of Guantun** <term>Guantun Ling</term>. After he had been in office for a full year, the wind did not even cause the branches to rustle <note>original: "風不鳴條" — a classical Chinese idiom describing a state of perfect peace and harmonious governance where even nature is tranquil.</note>. 

**King Wen** once dreamed of a woman who was exceptionally beautiful. She stood in the middle of the road, weeping. When he asked for the reason, she replied: "I am the daughter of **Mount Tai** <note>The most sacred of the Five Great Mountains of China, often associated with the transition between the living and the dead.</note>, and I am married to the spirit of the **East Sea**. I wish to return home, but the current **Magistrate of Guantun** is so virtuous that he blocks my path. My passage must be accompanied by violent winds and torrential rains, but such storms would damage his reputation for virtue <note>Her divine passage naturally causes storms; however, the Magistrate's moral power is so strong that it suppresses such disturbances, effectively trapping her.</note>." 

When **King Wen** awoke, he summoned **Taigong** to question him. On that very day, there were indeed torrential rains and violent winds, but they passed only along the outskirts of **Taigong’s** town. **King Wen** thereafter appointed **Taigong** as the **Great Minister of War** <term>Da Sima</term>.
