### The Encounter of Hu Mu Ban

An attendant dressed in crimson robes called out to **Ban** <note>Hu Mu Ban, a figure from the Jin Dynasty known for his dealings with the spirit world.</note>, saying: "The **Lord of Mount Tai** <note>The supreme deity of the most sacred mountain in China, believed to govern the souls of the dead and the register of births and deaths.</note> summons you." **Ban** was seized by alarm and hesitated, not knowing how to reply.

Another attendant then emerged and called to him. **Ban** followed him for several dozen paces, and the attendant requested that **Ban** close his eyes for a short while. Suddenly, he beheld palaces and chambers of immense and solemn majesty. **Ban** entered the inner hall to pay his respects. The Lord provided a feast and spoke to him: "I have requested your presence for no other reason than to ask you to deliver a letter to my son-in-law."

**Ban** asked where the lady was to be found. The Lord replied: "My daughter is the wife of the **River God** <term>He Bo</term> <note>The deity of the Yellow River, a powerful figure in the celestial hierarchy.</note>." 

**Ban** replied: "I shall surely deliver the letter, though I do not know by what means I might reach him." 

The Lord answered: "When you reach the middle of the river, simply strike the side of the boat and call for the **Green Maid** <note>original: "青衣" (Qing Yi) — a traditional term for a young female servant, named for the color of her uniform.</note>; someone will naturally appear to take the letter from you."

**Ban** then took his leave and exited. The attendant from before again ordered him to close his eyes. In a moment, he was back on the old road as if nothing had happened. He continued his journey westward and, following the deity's instructions, called for the **Green Maid**. In an instant, a female servant indeed appeared, took the letter, and disappeared beneath the waves. A short while later, she emerged once more, saying that the **River God**...

His father replied: "Since my unfortunate death, I have been sentenced to three years of labor. Now, two years have already passed..."

# In Search of the Supernatural, Volume 4

"...for [many] years now, and the hardship is unbearable. I know that you are now an acquaintance of the **Lord of the Mount** <note>original: "明府" (Mingfu). A respectful title for a high official, here referring to the divine Governor of Mount Tai.</note>; could you speak on my behalf?

I beg to be excused from this labor; I only wish to be appointed as a **Local Earth Deity** <term>She Gong</term> <note>original: "社公". A minor tutelary god responsible for a small local community or village shrine.</note>."

**Ban** followed these instructions, kowtowing and presenting the request to the **Lord of the Mount**. The Lord said: "the paths of the living and the dead are different <note>original: "生死異路" (sheng si yi lu). A recurring theme in Chinese folklore emphasizing that interaction between ghosts and the living is fundamentally dangerous for the latter.</note>; they must not be brought into proximity. I have no attachment to his [service] <note>Meaning the Lord is willing to release the father from his labor.</note>."

**Ban** pleaded earnestly until the Lord finally granted the request. Thereupon, **Ban** took his leave and returned home. After a year or so had passed, his sons had nearly all died. **Ban**, struck with terror, returned once more to **Mount Tai** <note>The sacred mountain in eastern China believed to be the gateway to the underworld.</note>, struck the tree, and sought an audience. The same groom from before emerged to welcome him and lead him inside.

**Ban** then explained that his previous request had been clumsy and ill-advised, for since returning home, his children had died until almost none were left. Now, fearing that the calamity was not yet over, he had come to report this, hoping for mercy and salvation. The **Lord of the Mount** clapped his hands and laughed loudly, saying: "It is exactly as I told you before: the paths of the living and the dead are different and must not be brought close. This [proximity] is the reason for your misfortune."

He immediately ordered that **Ban's** father be summoned from outside. In a short while, the father arrived in the courtyard, and the Lord questioned him: "Previously, you asked to return to your village shrine to serve as the **Local Earth Deity**..."

During the **Song** era <note>likely referring to the Liu Song dynasty (420–479 CE), or perhaps an earlier mythological attribution</note>, there was a man named **Feng Yi** from **Dishou** in **Tongxiang**, **Huayin**, located in **Hongnong**. On the first *Geng* day <note>A day in the traditional sixty-day sexagenary cycle used for Chinese dating.</note> of the eighth lunar month, he drowned while crossing the river. The **Celestial Emperor** <term>Tiandi</term> <note>The supreme ruler of the heavens and the cosmos in Chinese folk religion.</note> appointed him as the **River God** <term>He Bo</term> <note>original: "河伯". The deity of the Yellow River, often depicted as a powerful but sometimes fickle god.</note>.

Furthermore, the *Book of the Five Elements* <note>original: "五行書" (Wuxing Shu). An ancient text on cosmology, divination, and the natural order.</note> states: "The **River God** died on the day of *Geng-Chen* <note>Another specific day in the sexagenary cycle.</note>; therefore, on this day, one must not repair boats or travel far, or they will drown and never return."

In the thirty-sixth year of the reign of the First Emperor of Qin <note>211 BCE, one year before the Emperor's death.</note>, an envoy named Zheng Rong was traveling from the east of the pass. He was heading west toward the Hangu Pass when he arrived at Huayin. Looking up, he saw a plain white carriage drawn by a white horse descending from Mount Hua <note>original: "華山" (Huàshān). One of China's Five Great Mountains, traditionally considered a home for spirits and immortals.</note>. Suspecting that these were not mortal beings, he pulled over and waited for them.

When they reached him, they asked Zheng Rong, "Where are you going?" He replied, "I am going to the capital, Xianyang."

The person in the carriage said, "I am a messenger from Mount Hua. I wish to entrust you with a letter to be delivered to the Lord of the Hao Pool <note>original: "鎬池君" (Hàochí Jūn). A local water deity near the Qin capital.</note>. Since your path to Xianyang takes you past the Hao Pool, look for a large catalpa tree. Beneath the tree, there is a patterned stone; take it and knock against the trunk. Someone will appear to answer you."

He then handed the letter to him. Zheng Rong followed these instructions and knocked on the catalpa tree with the stone. Indeed, someone emerged to receive the letter. 

The following year, the Ancestral Dragon <term>Zulong</term> <note>original: "祖龍". An ancient prophetic epithet for the First Emperor; "Zu" means ancestor, and "Long" means dragon, symbolizing the Emperor. The prophecy famously stated, "The Ancestral Dragon will die this year."</note> passed away.

Zhang Pu <note>original: "張璞" (Zhāng Pú)</note>, whose courtesy name was Gongzhi <note>A courtesy name (zì) was a name given at adulthood to be used by peers as a mark of respect; it often complemented the meaning of the birth name.</note>, was a man of unknown origin. He served as the Governor of Wu Commandery <note>An administrative region centered around modern-day Suzhou.</note>. When he was recalled to the capital, his route took him past Mount Lu <note>original: "盧山" (Lúshān). A mountain in Jiangxi province long associated with spirits and transcendent beings.</note>.

While there, his daughters went to view the interior of a shrine. A maidservant pointed at one of the statues and joked to one of the girls, saying, "I shall marry you to this one."

That night, Zhang Pu’s wife dreamed that the Lord of Mount Lu <note>The presiding deity of the mountain.</note> had arrived to present betrothal gifts. The deity said, "My humble son is unworthy, yet I am moved that you have chosen him. I offer these small tokens to express my gratitude."

When the wife awoke, she found the dream strange. The maidservant then confessed what she had said in jest. Terrified, the wife urged Zhang Pu to set sail and depart as quickly as possible. However, when they reached the middle of the river, the boat would not move. Everyone on the ship was struck with terror. They began throwing their possessions into the water as offerings, yet the boat still would not proceed.

Someone said, "If you cast the daughter into the water, the boat will move forward." The others all said, "The divine intent is now clear. Why should an entire family be destroyed for the sake of a single daughter?"

Zhang Pu said, "I cannot bear to see it." He went up to the flying pavilion <term>feilu: a raised, multi-story cabin or observation deck on a large traditional Chinese ship</term> to lie down, leaving instructions for his wife to submerge the daughter in the water.

The wife, however, took the orphaned daughter of Zhang Pu’s deceased elder brother to take her place. She placed a mat upon the water and sat the girl upon it. The boat then...

A man from the Southern Provinces once dispatched an official to present a rhinoceros-horn hairpin <note>Rhinoceros horn was a highly prized luxury material in ancient China, often believed to possess magical properties or the ability to detect poison.</note> to Sun Quan <note>Sun Quan (182–252 CE) was the founding emperor of the state of Eastern Wu during the Three Kingdoms period.</note>. As the boat passed the Gongting Temple <note>The primary temple of the deity of Mount Lu, located near Lake Poyang.</note>, the official prayed to the spirit for a safe passage. The deity suddenly issued a divine command: "I require your rhinoceros-horn hairpin."

The official was struck with terror and did not dare to respond. In an instant, the hairpin had already moved from his possession and was laid out among the offerings before the deity. The deity then commanded again: "Wait until you reach Stone City <note>Shitou Cheng, the fortified naval base of modern-day Nanjing and the capital of the Eastern Wu kingdom.</note>, and I shall return your hairpin to you."

Having no other choice, the official continued his journey. He was filled with dread, believing he had lost the treasure and would surely face a death sentence for his failure to deliver it. However, just as he reached Stone City, a large carp, three feet in length, leaped into the boat. Upon cutting the fish open, they found the hairpin inside.

<page-num>Seven</page-num>
# Records of Searching for the Supernatural

When Guo Pu <note>Guo Pu (276–324 CE) was a renowned writer, poet, and master of the occult sciences, particularly known for his commentaries on ancient texts and his skill in divination.</note> crossed the Yangtze River, Yin You, the Prefect of Xuancheng, invited him to serve as his adjutant. At that time, a creature appeared that was as large as a water buffalo. It was grey in color and had short legs that resembled those of an elephant. Both its chest and the tip of its tail were white; it possessed great strength but moved sluggishly. 

When it arrived beneath the city walls, the people were all astonished by it. Yin You sent men to lie in ambush and capture it, and he commanded Guo Pu to cast a divination <term>cast a divination|zuòguà: to perform a reading using the I-Ching (Book of Changes) to identify the nature of a supernatural event</term>. The reading resulted in the hexagram *Withdrawal* changing into the hexagram *Decay* <note>original: "遇遯之蠱" (yù Dùn zhī Gǔ). These refer to Hexagram 33 and Hexagram 18 of the I-Ching.</note>. Guo Pu named the creature a "Donkey Mouse." 

Just as the divination was completed, the men in ambush struck the creature with a halberd, piercing it over a foot deep. The district officials went to the local shrine to request permission to kill it, but the shaman reported that the temple deity was displeased, saying: "This is the mouse-messenger belonging to the Lord of Donkey Mountain at Zhuting. It is traveling toward Mount Jing and was merely passing through my domain for a short stay. You must not harm it." 

The creature then departed and was never seen again.

***

Ou Ming of Luling once traveled with a merchant along a route that passed through Lake Pengze. On every trip, regardless of the quantity of goods he carried on his boat, he would cast a portion into the lake, saying he did so as a ritual offering. After several years of this practice, he passed by the lake again. Suddenly, he saw a great road opening up within the lake...

<summary>This page records two supernatural encounters: Guo Pu’s identification of a divine "Donkey Mouse" via divination, and the beginning of Ou Ming’s encounter with a lake deity after years of faithful offerings.</summary>
<keywords>Guo Pu, Yin You, Records of Searching for the Supernatural (Soushen Ji), divination, I-Ching, Donkey Mouse, Lake Pengze, Ou Ming, mountain spirits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the entries from the "Records of Searching for the Supernatural" (Soushen Ji). The previous page concluded the story of a "Donkey Mouse" encountered by the diviner Guo Pu. ## Volume Four

In the winter, amidst the wind and dust, several officials arrived with horses and carriages to wait for Deng Ming <note>The text likely refers to Deng Ming, a merchant known in folklore for his encounter with a lake deity.</note>. They announced they were messengers from the Lord of Qinghong <note>The Lord of Qinghong was a deity associated with Lake Qinghong.</note> and that he requested an audience.

When the officials arrived, Ming saw a government office with guards stationed at the gate. He became very afraid. An official reassured him, saying: "There is nothing to fear. The Lord of Qinghong is moved by the fact that you have always shown him proper etiquette and respect. That is why he has invited you. He will certainly offer you heavy gifts; do not accept them. Instead, ask only for 'As-You-Wish' <term>As-You-Wish|Rúyuàn: A name literally meaning 'to have one’s wishes fulfilled.' In folklore, she is a spirit-maid who brings prosperity.</term>."

Once Ming met the Lord of Qinghong, he indeed requested As-You-Wish. The Lord commanded her to follow Ming as he departed. As-You-Wish was actually one of the Lord of Qinghong's maidservants. After Ming brought her back home, whatever he desired, he immediately obtained. Within a few years, he became immensely wealthy.

***

To the west of Yizhou and the east of Yunnan <note>Yizhou was a large province in southwestern China during the Han and Jin dynasties, covering parts of modern Sichuan and Yunnan.</note>, there is a divine shrine. A chamber has been carved directly into the mountain stone. Within dwells a deity who is worshipped there; he calls himself Master Huang.

It is said that this deity is the spirit of the Old Man of the Yellow Stone <note>original: "黄石公" (Huángshígōng). Legend says he was a mysterious sage who tested the patience of the statesman Zhang Liang before gifting him a manual on military strategy.</note>, the one from whom Zhang Liang received his wisdom. The shrine is a place of purity where no animals are slaughtered for sacrifice. Those who come to pray there bring one hundred coins, a pair of writing brushes, and a cake of ink, which they place upon the stone...

<summary>This page recounts the folk legend of a merchant who gained wealth through a gift from a lake deity and describes a unique, non-sacrificial shrine dedicated to the spirit of the Old Man of the Yellow Stone.</summary>

<keywords>Lord of Qinghong, As-You-Wish (Ruyuan), wealth folklore, Old Man of the Yellow Stone, Zhang Liang, Yizhou, Yunnan, mountain shrines, ritual purity</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues from the description of a mountain shrine in the borderlands of Yizhou and Yunnan. The previous page established the setting of a stone chamber inhabited by an unseen deity.</meta>

# In Search of the Supernatural

When petitioners approach the front of the chamber to pray, they first hear a voice echoing from within the stone room. Shortly after, a spirit-official asks what the person seeks. Once they have stated their request, the deity provides a full account of their future fortune or misfortune. Its physical form is never seen, and this has remained the case until the present day.

***

During the Yongjia era <note>The Yongjia era (307–313 AD) was a period of the Jin Dynasty marked by significant social upheaval.</note>, a deity appeared in Yanzhou <note>Yanzhou was one of the Nine Provinces of ancient China, located in the region of modern-day western Shandong.</note> who called himself Fan Daoji. There was also an elderly woman known as Lady Cheng. This lady had a great passion for music and was a skilled player of the *konghou* <term>konghou|An ancient Chinese plucked string instrument, similar to a harp, that was popular in court and folk music during the Han and Jin dynasties.</term>. Whenever she heard the sound of stringed instruments or singing, she would immediately rise and begin to dance.

***

Dai Wenmou of Peiguo <note>Peiguo was a commandery centered around modern-day Anhui province.</note> lived as a recluse in the Yangcheng Mountains. Once, while he was eating in his guest hall, he suddenly heard a deity calling out to him: "I am an envoy of the Celestial Emperor <note>The Celestial Emperor (Tiandi) is the supreme ruler of the heavens and the head of the celestial bureaucracy in Chinese folk religion.</note>. I wish to descend and rely upon you; would that be acceptable?" 

Wenmou was greatly startled by this. The voice spoke again: "You doubt me." 

Wenmou then knelt and replied: "I live in poverty and fear my humble home is not worthy of your divine descent." 

Subsequently, he swept the house clean and arranged a ritual seat of honor, serving meals every morning and evening with the utmost reverence. Later, he spoke of this privately in his inner chambers to his spouse. His wife remarked: "I fear this may be a demon <note>original: "妖" (yāo) – a term for a malevolent spirit, monster, or deceptive entity that mimics divinity.</note>."

<summary>This page describes various supernatural encounters, including a prophetic stone chamber deity, a music-loving spirit, and a recluse who is visited by a self-proclaimed envoy of the Celestial Emperor.</summary>
<keywords>Soushen Ji, Fan Daoji, Lady Cheng, konghou, Dai Wenmou, Celestial Emperor, Yanzhou, divination</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume 4

"The spirit is merely relying on your residence." 

Wenmou said, "I too have my doubts." 

When it came time for the sacrificial offerings <note>These were ritual meals presented to spirits or ancestors to ensure their favor and sustenance.</note>, the deity spoke, saying: "I followed you because I intended to bring you mutual benefit; I did not expect you to harbor doubts or dissenting thoughts." 

As Wenmou was apologizing and excusing himself, suddenly the sound of dozens of voices shouting filled the hall. He went out to look and saw a great bird of five colors <note>Multi-colored birds in Chinese lore, like the phoenix, are often omens of divine presence or significant shifts in fortune.</note> with dozens of white doves following it. They flew toward the northeast, entered the clouds, and disappeared.

***

<term>Mi Zhu</term>, whose courtesy name was Zizhong, was a native of Qu in the Donghai Commandery <note>Modern-day Lianyungang, Jiangsu. Mi Zhu later became a prominent advisor to the warlord Liu Bei during the Three Kingdoms period.</note>. For generations, his ancestors had been <term>merchant-producers|The term "huozhi" refers to those who grew wealthy through trade and the management of resources, a class often viewed with a mix of respect and suspicion in ancient China.</term>, and his family wealth was valued at tens of millions. 

Once, while he was returning from the capital of Luoyang and was still several dozen *li* <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance; at this time, 20 li would be roughly 8 to 10 kilometers.</note> from home, he saw a beautiful young woman by the side of the road. She asked Zhu if she could hitch a ride in his carriage. 

After they had traveled together for about twenty *li*, the woman thanked him and prepared to depart. She said to Zhu: "I am a messenger from Heaven, sent to burn down the house of Mi Zhu of Donghai. Because I was touched by the kindness you showed in giving me a ride, I am telling you this now." 

Zhu then privately pleaded with her for mercy. The woman replied:

<summary>This page concludes the story of Dai Wenmou's failed divine encounter and begins the famous legend of Mi Zhu meeting the Fire Spirit, who warns him of his home's impending destruction.</summary>

## Page 9

"It cannot be helped; your house must be burned. However, you, sir, may go quickly; I shall walk slowly. At noon, the fire will surely break out."

Zhu then hurried home. Upon arriving, he immediately moved out his property and goods. At noon, a great fire indeed broke out.

***

During the reign of <term>Emperor Xuan of Han|The tenth emperor of the Western Han dynasty (r. 74–48 BCE), known for his effective governance.</term>, there was a man named Yin Zifang in Nanyang. He was naturally very filial, accumulated merit through his kindness, enjoyed giving to charity, and was devoted to honoring the <term>Stove God|Also known as the Kitchen God (Zao Jun), this deity resides in the kitchen and reports a family's conduct to the heavens once a year.</term>. 

On the morning of the <term>La Festival|A traditional winter sacrifice held in the twelfth lunar month; it is the origin of the modern Laba Festival.</term>, while Zifang was preparing the morning meal, the Stove God manifested himself in a visible form. Zifang bowed twice to receive the deity's blessing. His family happened to have a "yellow sheep" <note>original: 黃羊 (huáng yáng). While it literally means "yellow sheep," it likely refers to a specific breed of goat or a sheep with a yellowish coat, which became a traditional sacrifice in the Yin family lore.</note>, so he offered it as a sacrifice to the deity. 

From that time forward, his wealth grew exponentially until he was immensely rich. He owned more than seven hundred *qing* of land <note>A traditional unit of area; 700 *qing* is approximately 10,000 acres, an enormous estate for a private citizen.</note>, and his collection of carriages, horses, and servants rivaled those of a regional lord. 

Zifang once remarked: "My descendants will surely become powerful and great." By the third generation, his words were proven true, and the family flourished. The clan produced four marquises and dozens of regional governors. Because of this, his descendants always continued to sacrifice a yellow sheep to the Stove God on the day of the La Festival.

<summary>This page concludes the tale of Mi Zhu, who was warned by a fire spirit to save his property, and recounts the origin of the Yin family's wealth through a miraculous encounter with the Stove God.</summary>
<keywords>Mi Zhu, Yin Zifang, Stove God, Kitchen God, La Festival, Han Dynasty, Nanyang, supernatural omens, filial piety</keywords>

***

Zhang Cheng of Wu County <note>Modern-day Suzhou, a region historically famous for its silk production.</note> got up during the night and suddenly saw a woman standing in the southern corner of his residence. She raised her hand and beckoned to Cheng, saying:

"This place is your family's **silkworm room**. I am the deity of this land. On the fifteenth day of the first lunar month next year, you should prepare white porridge with a layer of fat floating on top. From that year forward, you will obtain a great harvest of silkworms." The contemporary practice of making *fatty gruel* <note>original: 膏糜 (gāomí). This refers to a ritualistic porridge or congee topped with oil or fat, offered to ensure agricultural success.</note> is modeled after this event.

***

In Yuzhang <note>Modern-day Nanchang in Jiangxi Province.</note>, there was a daughter of the Dai family who had been ill for a long time without recovery. One day, she found a small stone shaped like a human figurine. The girl said to the stone:

"You have the form of a human; could it be that you are a deity capable of curing my <term>chronic illness|sùjí (宿疾), a long-standing or recurring ailment.</term>? If you heal me, I shall hold you in high honor." 

That night, she dreamed of a figure who told her, "I shall indeed bless and protect you." From that time on, her illness gradually subsided. Consequently, she established a shrine for the stone at the foot of the mountain. Because the woman of the Dai family served as a <term>shaman|wū (巫), a medium or priestess who communicates with the spirit world.</term>, the site was named the **Dai Hou Shrine** <note>literally "Shrine of Lord Dai."</note>.

***

Liu Qi, the <term>Magistrate|Xiànzhǎng (羨長), a local administrative title during the Han dynasty.</term> of Hanyang, often used to say, "When I die, I shall surely become a deity." One evening, while intoxicated from drinking, he passed away suddenly, despite having suffered from no prior illness.

Amidst a great storm of wind and rain, his coffin was lost. That night, the sounds of thousands of voices wailing <note>original: 啾聲 (jiū shēng). A term often used in classical literature to describe the shrill, mournful, or chattering sounds of spirits and ghosts.</note> were heard echoing from Jing Mountain. When the local villagers went to investigate the site,

they found that the coffin had already been transformed into a burial mound. Consequently, the name of the peak was changed to Jun Mountain <note>original: 君山 (Jūn Shān), meaning "Lord's Mountain" or "Mountain of the Gentleman," reflecting Liu Qi's new status as a local protector deity.</note>, and a shrine was established there to offer him sacrifices.

<summary>This page narrates the sudden death of the official Liu Qi and the miraculous transformation of his lost coffin into a burial mound on Jing Mountain, leading to his deification.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 5

->Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty<-

---

Jiang Ziwen was a man of Guangling. He was fond of wine and sensual pleasures, and was frivolous and unrestrained in his conduct. He often said of himself that his <term>bones were pure<note>original: "骨清" (gǔ qīng). In ancient Chinese physiognomy, "pure" or "noble" bones were physical indicators that a person was destined for spiritual transcendence or to become a deity after death.</note></term> and that he would surely become a god after he died. At the end of the Han Dynasty, he served as the <term>Commandant of Moling<note>original: "秣陵尉" (Mòlíng wèi). Moling was the ancient name for the region of modern-day Nanjing. The Commandant was a high-ranking military and administrative official responsible for local security.</note></term>. While pursuing bandits to the foot of Mount Zhong, the bandits struck him, wounding his forehead. He untied his official seal-ribbon to bind the wound, and shortly thereafter, he died.

At the beginning of the reign of the First Lord of Wu <note>original: "吳先主" (Wú Xiānzhǔ), referring to Sun Quan, the founder of the Eastern Wu state during the Three Kingdoms period (reigned 222–252 AD).</note>, one of Jiang’s former subordinates encountered Ziwen on a road. Ziwen was riding a white horse and holding a white feather fan, accompanied by an entourage just as he had been during his life. Those who saw him fled in terror. Ziwen pursued them and said: "I am to be the <term>tutelary god of this land<note>original: "土地神" (Tǔdìshén). A local deity responsible for the protection and prosperity of a specific geographic area.</note></term> to bring blessings to you, the common people. You should announce to the populace that they must establish a shrine for me. If they do not, a great calamity will surely follow."

That year, in the summer, a great plague broke out. The common people secretly whispered among themselves...

<summary>This page recounts the origin of the deity Jiang Ziwen, a former official of Moling (Nanjing) who died in battle and returned as a spirit to demand a shrine from the living. His appearance is followed by the onset of a devastating plague, marking the beginning of his transition from a man to a powerful local god.</summary>
<keywords>Jiang Ziwen, Mount Zhong, Nanjing, Moling, Sun Quan, Eastern Wu, tutelary deity, plague, deification, Gan Bao</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Jiang Ziwen from Volume 5. Having appeared as a ghost to his former subordinates, Jiang now begins a campaign of supernatural terror to force the local government to recognize his divinity.</meta>

<header>Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries</header>
<meta>Volume 5</meta>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 5

Fear spread through the region, and many people began to secretly offer sacrifices at shrines to Jiang Ziwen. Soon, Ziwen again spoke through the <term>spirit mediums<note>original: "巫祝" (wūzhù). These were shamans or oracles who acted as intermediaries, allowing spirits to speak through them to the living.</note></term>, saying: "I intend to bring great fortune and protection to the Sun clan <note>The ruling family of the Kingdom of Wu.</note>. You should establish an official shrine for me. If you do not, I shall send insects to enter people’s ears and cause a great plague."

Shortly thereafter, tiny insects, as small as grains of dust or lice, began entering people’s ears. Every person affected died, and the doctors were powerless to treat them. The common people grew increasingly terrified, yet the <term>King of Wu<note>original: "孫王" (Sūn Wáng), referring to Sun Quan.</note></term> still did not believe these events were the work of Ziwen.

Ziwen again descended and spoke through the spirit mediums: "If you do not offer me the proper sacrifices, I will cause a disaster of great fires." 

That year, massive fires broke out. In a single day, dozens of blazes would erupt, eventually reaching even the official palaces. The King’s advisors argued that "when a ghost has a place to return to, it will not become a malevolent spirit" <note>This reflects the traditional Chinese belief that "hungry ghosts" or unappeased spirits cause harm, but once given a temple and offerings, they become protective deities.</note>. They suggested it would be appropriate to appease him.

Thereupon, the King sent an envoy to officially enfeoff Ziwen as the **Marquis of Zhongdu**. He also granted Ziwen's younger brother, Zixu, the title of **Colonel of Changshui**. Both were granted official seals and ribbons of office. A temple was established in their honor, and the name of Mount Zhong was officially changed to **Mount Jiang** <note>original: "蔣山" (Jiǎng Shān). This mountain, now known as Purple Mountain (Zijin Shan) in Nanjing, retained the name "Mount Jiang" for centuries in honor of this deity.</note>. This is the Mount Jiang located to the northeast of modern-day <term>Jiankang<note>original: "建康" (Jiànkāng). The ancient name for Nanjing when it served as the capital for several dynasties.</note></term>. From that time on, the disasters and plagues ceased.

<vocab>Sun Wang (King Sun Quan), Spirit Mediums, Jiang Ziwen, Mount Jiang, Jiankang, Marquis of Zhongdu, Colonel of Changshui, Calamities and Plagues</vocab>

<summary>Jiang Ziwen coerces the King of Wu into deifying him by unleashing a plague of ear-invading insects and a series of mysterious fires. The King eventually relents, granting Jiang a noble title and renaming Mount Zhong in his honor, after which the disasters stop.</summary>

## ...Henceforth, the Cult Grew Greatly

Liu Chifu dreamed that <term>Lord Jiang<note>original: "蔣侯" (Jiǎng Hóu), the posthumous title of Jiang Ziwen as a local deity.</note></term> summoned him to serve as his <term>Registrar<note>original: "主簿" (zhǔbù), a high-ranking clerical position within the divine bureaucracy.</note></term>. The deadline for his arrival was approaching rapidly, so he went to the temple to plead his case. He explained that his mother was old and his children were young, and his family situation was so dire that he begged for mercy and to be released from the summons. 

There was a man from Kuaiji named Wei Guo, who possessed many talents and arts and was skilled in serving the spirits. Liu Chifu begged to recommend Wei Guo to take his place, kowtowing until his forehead bled. 

The <term>Temple Invocator<note>original: "廟祝" (miàozhù), the priest or medium who interprets the god's will.</note></term> said: "The Lord specifically desires your service. Who is this Wei Guo, that you would make such a proposal?" Chifu pleaded persistently, but in the end, his request was not granted. Shortly thereafter, Liu Chifu passed away.

During the <term>Xianning era<note>275–280 AD, during the Western Jin Dynasty.</note></term>, the son of Han Bo (the Minister of Ceremonies), the son of Wang Yun (the Governor of Kuaiji), and the son of Liu Dan (the Grand Master of Splendid Happiness) were traveling together to the Temple of Mount Jiang. In the temple, there were several statues of women that were exceptionally beautiful and refined. 

The young men were intoxicated; they each pointed at a statue and jokingly claimed them as their wives, matching themselves to the figures in jest. That same evening, all three men had the same dream, in which Lord Jiang...

The Lord sent a messenger to communicate with them, saying: "The daughters of my household are all ugly and plain, yet you have condescended to bestow your honored attention upon them. I have therefore fixed a date to welcome you all." 

The young men found these dreams and the instructions within them to be strangely specific. When they began to question one another, they realized that they had each experienced the exact same dream, matching in every detail. At this, they were struck with great terror. They prepared the <term>Three Sacrificial Animals<note>original: "三牲" (sānshēng), the traditional offering of a pig, an ox, and a sheep used in solemn rituals.</note></term> and went to the temple to confess their guilt and beg for mercy.

The appointed time is almost here; how could I allow you to change your minds now?" A short time later, all of the young men died.

In the eastern wilds of Yin County, Kuaiji, there lived a young woman surnamed Wu, whose given name was Wangzi. She was sixteen years old, and her appearance was lovely and charming. 

In her village, there were those who were skilled in the <term>shamanic arts of drum and dance<note>original: "解鼓舞神者" (jiě gǔwǔ shén zhě); these were local mediums who performed rhythmic dances and drumming to summon or appease spirits.</note></term>. They invited her to join them, and she set out accordingly. As she was walking along the embankment, she suddenly saw a nobleman of extraordinary refinement. This nobleman was traveling by boat, accompanied by more than ten <term>attendants<note>original: "從力" (cónglì), laborers or guards serving a high-ranking official.</note></term> who were remarkably orderly and disciplined. He sent one of his men to inquire after her...

<sig>Three</sig>

...[the nobleman] asked Wangzi where she wished to go. She replied by explaining her errand in full. The nobleman said, "I am just now heading to that very place; you may board my boat and we shall travel together." Wangzi declined, not daring to accept. Suddenly, the figure vanished. 

When Wangzi arrived to pay her respects at the divine altar, she saw the nobleman from the boat sitting there with solemn dignity; it was the statue of <term>Lord Jiang<note>original: "蔣侯" (Jiǎng Hóu), the deified spirit of Jiang Ziwen, a local hero-god of the Nanjing region known for his temper and power.</note></term>. The deity asked Wangzi, "Why have you arrived so late?" and then tossed two oranges to her. 

From then on, he manifested his form to her frequently, and their affection grew deep. Whatever her heart desired would immediately drop down to her from the empty air. Once, she had a craving to eat a pair of carp, and two fresh carp arrived instantly, just as she had wished. A wondrous fragrance from Wangzi could be smelled for miles around, and the deity's <term>divine efficacy<note>original: "神驗" (shényàn), referring to the tangible proof of a god's power or the fulfillment of prayers.</note></term> was great. The entire district joined in worshipping him. After three years had passed, Wangzi suddenly developed <term>outside intentions<note>original: "外意" (wàiyì), an idiom suggesting she had developed a romantic interest in a mortal man or a desire to leave the deity's service.</note></term>, and the god immediately severed all contact with her.

# Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries, Volume 5

A man was traveling in a small boat with his young wife, carrying a large knife tucked into the side of the vessel. At dusk, they arrived at a <term>patrol station<note>original: "邏" (luó), a sentry post or military checkpoint common in ancient China for monitoring travelers and maintaining local security.</note></term>.

The officer in charge of the station came out to speak with them, saying, "Lately, this area has been infested with many <term>wild dangers<note>original: "草穢" (cǎo huì), literally 'rank weeds,' a euphemism used here to describe the presence of dangerous bandits or predatory beasts lurking in the undergrowth.</note></term>. You are traveling with a delicate companion and are so lightly armed; it is a very difficult journey. You should stop and stay at the station for the night."

Just as they had finished their exchange and the officer was turning to head back, the wife stepped onto the shore. Immediately, she was snatched away by a tiger. Her husband drew his knife and let out a great shout, intending to pursue the beast. Because he was a devoted follower of <term>Lord Jiang<note>original: "蔣侯" (Jiǎng Hóu), refers to Jiang Ziwen, the deified spirit of a Han dynasty official who became a powerful local god of the Nanjing region.</note></term>, he called out to the god for assistance.

After he had traveled about ten *li*, a figure in <term>black clothing<note>original: "黑衣" (hēi yī); in traditional Chinese tales, divine messengers or spirit guides often appear in simple, distinctive clothing to lead the protagonist.</note></term> suddenly appeared to guide him. The man followed the figure for another twenty *li* until they reached a massive tree. Upon arriving, he found a cave. Hearing the sound of footsteps, the tiger's cubs—thinking their mother had returned—all came running out. The man killed them on the spot, then drew his knife and hid himself by the side of the tree.

He waited there for a long time until the <term>tiger<note>original: "虎" (hǔ); tigers were a significant physical and symbolic threat in ancient southern China, often featuring in tales of the supernatural.</note></term> finally arrived. The beast set the woman down on the ground and began dragging her backward into the cave. The man...

He struck at the tiger’s waist with his knife, severing it in two. After the tiger died, his wife was found to be alive. As dawn approached, she regained her speech. When her husband questioned her, 
she said that when the tiger first seized her, it simply carried her upon its back. Only when it reached that spot did the beast set her down. Her body was otherwise unharmed, 
save for scratches caused by the thickets. He helped her back to the boat. The next night, he dreamed of a person who told him, "Lord Jiang sent me 
to assist you; did you know that?" Upon returning home, he sacrificed a pig at the shrine in gratitude.

In Quanjiao County, Huainan, there was a woman known as the <term>New Bride of the Ding Family<note>original: "丁新婦" (Dīng Xīnfù). In traditional Chinese narrative, women are often identified by their natal surname (Ding) and their status in their husband's household.</note></term>. She was originally a daughter of the Ding family from Danyang. At the age of sixteen, she married into 
the Xie family of Quanjiao. Her mother-in-law was harsh and cruel, assigning her labor with strict quotas. If she failed to meet these limits, she would be <term>flogged and beaten<note>original: "笞捶" (chī chuí), referring to the use of bamboo rods or whips as a form of domestic punishment.</note></term>. It 
became unbearable. On the <term>ninth day of the ninth month<note>original: "九月九日" (jiǔ yuè jiǔ rì). This is the Double Ninth Festival (Chongyang Festival). Traditionally a day for climbing mountains and drinking chrysanthemum wine, this story provides a folkloric origin for why women were excused from labor on this holiday.</note></term>, she committed suicide by hanging. Subsequently, her spirit manifested, and her voice was heard among the common people, speaking 
through <term>shamans and priests<note>original: "巫祝" (wū zhù), intermediaries who communicated with the spirit world.</note></term>, saying: "In compassion for the wives and daughters of every household who work without rest, let them avoid labor on the ninth day of the ninth month. Do not..."

# Jin Yuan Siku Quanshu, Volume 5

Manifesting herself to attend to her affairs, she appeared in physical form wearing <term>pale blue silk garments<note>original: "縹衣" (piǎo yī), a light, sky-blue or sea-green color often associated with the ethereal or the divine in Chinese tradition.</note></term> and a green canopy over her head. Followed by a single maidservant, she arrived at the <term>Niuzhu Ferry<note>original: "牛渚津" (Niúzhǔ Jīn), a historically significant crossing point on the Yangtze River, located in modern-day Ma'anshan, Anhui Province.</note></term> seeking passage across the river.

There were two men sharing a boat while fishing. She called out to them, asking for a ride. The two men laughed and began to flirt with her, saying, "If you agree to be our wife, we will ferry you across."

Matron Ding said, "I thought you were decent men, but you have no sense of propriety. If you were living men, I would cast you into the mud to die; if you were ghosts, I would cast you into the deep water." She then withdrew back into the tall grass.

A short while later, an old man arrived in a boat laden with <term>reeds<note>original: "葦" (wěi), commonly used for fuel, matting, or roofing.</note></term>. The Matron requested a crossing from him. The old man said, "My boat has no cabin or cover; how could you cross exposed to the elements? I fear it is not suitable for your passage."

The Matron replied, "It is no trouble." The old man then cleared a place for her among the reeds to provide a secure spot. Without even appearing to step fully into the boat, she was ferried directly across to the southern bank. As she was departing, she spoke to the old man: "I am a <term>divine spirit<note>original: "鬼神" (guǐ shén), a term covering a spectrum from ghosts to powerful deities. Here, she asserts her status as a local goddess rather than a mere wandering soul.</note></term>, not a human being. I am naturally able to cross on my own, but I wished to make my presence known among the people. For your kindness..."

"Your great kindness in clearing the reeds and ferrying me across fills me with profound gratitude. I must find a way to thank you. Venerable sir, you should return home quickly; you will surely see something unusual and gain something as well."

The old man replied, "I only feared the boat was too damp and uncomfortable for you. How could I dare accept your thanks?"

When the old man returned to the west bank, he saw the two men <note>the rude fishermen who had insulted the Matron on the previous page</note> had capsized in the water. Proceeding forward for several miles, he found thousands of fish leaping by the water’s edge, blown right onto the shore by the wind. The old man then abandoned his cargo of reeds and loaded his boat with fish to take home.

Thus, Matron Ding returned to <term>Danyang<note>original: "丹陽" (Dānyáng), a region south of the Yangtze River in modern-day Jiangsu Province.</note></term>. The people south of the river all call her "Matron Ding." On the <term>Double Ninth Festival<note>original: "九月九日" (jiǔ yuè jiǔ rì), the ninth day of the ninth lunar month.</note></term>, people refrain from working, as it is widely regarded as her <term>death anniversary<note>original: "忌日" (jìrì), a day of mourning or taboo when certain activities are avoided.</note></term>. To this day, shrines dedicated to her can be found in many places.

Wang You, an <term>Attendant-in-Ordinary<note>original: "散騎侍郎" (sǎn qí shì láng), a high-ranking civil official post in the imperial court.</note></term>, was gravely ill. He had already bid his final farewell to his mother. Shortly after, he heard someone announce a visitor: "A certain person from such-and-such a village in such-and-such a commandery, who once served as an <term>Administrative Assistant<note>original: "別駕" (bié jià), a high-ranking provincial official.</note></term>." Wang You had long been familiar with this man's reputation and name. After a brief moment, he suddenly <unclear>lost consciousness</unclear> <note>original: "奄然" (yǎn rán). While this can mean "suddenly died," in the context of "In Search of the Supernatural," it often precedes a near-death experience or a vision.</note>.

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume Five

Upon arriving, the visitor said, “Between you and those of my kind, there is a natural kinship. Furthermore, since we are from the same home district, our feelings for one another are naturally sincere. This year, the state has a great undertaking. Three generals have been dispatched to mobilize and recruit forces. About a dozen of us are serving as <term>Military Advisors<note>original: "參佐" (cān zuǒ), subordinates or staff officers who assisted a high-ranking official or general.</note></term> to the household of <term>Lord Zhao Gongming<note>original: "趙公明" (Zhào Gōngmíng). In early Chinese lore, he was a fearsome commander of spirits or a plague deity, though in later centuries he became widely worshipped as the God of Wealth.</note></term>. Arriving here in such haste, I saw your high gates and grand house, so I came to seek refuge. Our meeting is so fortuitous that it is beyond words.”

Wang You, realizing these visitors were <term>spirits<note>original: "鬼神" (guǐ shén), literally "ghosts and gods." In this context, it refers to the supernatural entities or officials of the underworld.</note></term>, said, “Unluckily, I am gravely ill and death is imminent. Since I have encountered you, I entrust my life to your mercy.”

The visitor replied, “Life must end in death; this is an inevitable truth. The state of being dead is not determined by whether one was noble or lowly during life. I am currently leading three thousand troops and need someone to whom I can entrust the <term>ledger<note>original: "度簿" (dù bù), administrative records or registers, often used in Chinese mythology to track the names and fates of the living and the dead.</note></term>. Such a position in the spirit world is difficult to obtain; you should not refuse it.”

Wang You replied, “My aged mother is advanced in years, and I have no brothers. If I die suddenly, there will be no one to <term>provide for her<note>original: "供養" (gōng yǎng), the traditional duty of a child to provide material and emotional support for their parents in old age.</note></term>.” He then began to sob uncontrollably, his sighs and weeping showing he could not contain his grief. The visitor appeared moved and sorrowful at his plight.

<summary>A dying official named Wang You is visited by spirits serving the deity Zhao Gongming, who offer him an administrative post in the afterlife. Wang You tearfully pleads for his life, citing his duty to care for his elderly mother.</summary>

The visitor said, “Your rank is that of a <term>Palace Attendant<note>original: "常伯" (cháng bó), a title for high-ranking advisors who attended the emperor directly. It literally means 'Constant Chief.'</note></term>, yet your household has no surplus wealth. I just heard your parting words to your honorable mother, and your speech was sorrowful and bitter. Thus, you are a <term>scholar of the state<note>original: "國士" (guó shì), an exceptionally talented person who serves the state with great virtue and integrity.</note></term>. How could I let such a man die? I shall act on your behalf.”

He then stood up and left. He returned the next day, and then the day after that. You asked him, “Since you have promised to let me live, will you fulfill this kindness to the end?”

He replied, “I, <term>the Old One<note>original: "大老子" (dà lǎo zǐ), a colloquial and somewhat self-important way for the spirit to refer to himself, implying seniority and authority.</note></term>, have already given you my word; would I deceive you?” 

You then saw the spirit's followers, numbering several hundred, all about two feet tall. They wore black clothing and military uniforms, with red oil markings used as their insignia. 

You’s family beat drums and offered sacrifices to the spirits. Upon hearing the drumbeats, the spirits all danced in rhythm, their sleeves fluttering with a *sa-sa* <note>original: "颯颯" (sà sà), an onomatopoeia describing the rustling sound of wind or silk garments.</note> sound.

You prepared wine and food for them, but the visitor declined, saying, “There is no need.” He then rose to leave and said to You, “Illness in the human body is like a fire; it must be extinguished with water.” 

He then took a cup of water and <term>sprayed it from his mouth<note>original: "噀" (xùn), a ritualistic spraying of liquid, often used in ancient Chinese medicine and exorcism to drive away 'heat' or demonic influences.</note></term> onto the quilt and poured it over him. He further said, “I shall leave for you a red—”

## Volume Five

The visitor placed ten or so brushes under the straw mat and said, “These may be given to others to be used as <term>hairpins<note>original: "簪" (zān). In ancient China, both men and women used long pins to secure their hair or caps. Here, they serve as protective talismans.</note></term>. These are placeholder names, similar to 'John Doe' or 'Person A and Person B,' indicating specific individuals the spirit intended to take to the underworld.</note></term>, I shall take them both.” 

He then took You’s hand to bid him farewell. 

At that time, You was able to sleep peacefully. In the middle of the night, he suddenly woke and called to his attendants, ordering them to uncover him. He said, “A spirit poured water over me; I am about to be completely drenched.” Upon opening the quilts, they indeed found water. It rested below the top quilt and above the bottom quilt, yet it did not soak into the fabric, appearing instead like dew sitting upon a lotus leaf. When they measured it, they collected three <term>sheng<note>A traditional unit of volume. In the Jin Dynasty, a 'sheng' was roughly 200 milliliters, making this about 740 milliliters of water in total.</note></term> and seven <term>he</term>.

Following this, two-thirds of his illness was cured, and within a few days, it was entirely gone. 

Every person whom the spirit had said would be “taken” subsequently died; only Wang Wenying survived for another half-year before passing. Those whom the spirit had spoken of protecting, as well as the people marked by the <term>red brushes<note>original: "赤筆人" (chì bǐ rén). This refers to the people You protected using the brushes given to him by the spirit.</note></term>, all passed through subsequent illnesses and the chaos of war without any harm.

Originally, there was a <term>prophetic book<note>original: "妖書" (yāo shū), literally 'strange' or 'heretical' book. These were often apocryphal texts predicting political changes or supernatural events, frequently suppressed by the government.</note></term> which stated: “The Supreme Deity has appointed three generals, <term>Zhao Gongming<note>A prominent figure in Chinese folk religion, later known as a God of Wealth, but in this period associated with the transition of souls and the spread of plagues.</note></term> and <term>Zhong Shiji<note>The courtesy name of Zhong Hui, a historical general of the Three Kingdoms period who, after his death, entered the folk pantheon as a spirit general.</note></term>, to each supervise...”

<sig>Li Zhong Yi</sig>

Several ghosts descended to take people away, and no one knew where they were taken. You’s illness was cured. When he later saw this account, it matched everything that had been said regarding <term>Zhao Gongming<note>original: "趙公明" (Zhào Gōngmíng). While popularly known in later periods as a God of Wealth, in this era he was a formidable deity associated with the underworld and the administration of plagues.</note></term>.

During the Han dynasty, Zhou Shi of <term>Xiapi<note>An ancient county in what is now northern Jiangsu province.</note></term> once traveled along the Eastern Sea road. He encountered a minor official carrying a scroll who asked to hitch a ride on his boat. After they had traveled ten or more miles, the official said to Shi, “I have a brief errand to attend to. I shall leave this book in your boat for a moment; be careful not to open it.”

After the official departed, Shi secretly opened the book and looked inside. It was a <term>Register of the Dead<note>original: "諸死人錄" (zhū sǐrén lù). In Chinese folk and Taoist belief, the bureaucracy of the afterlife maintains precise ledgers of every person's lifespan.</note></term>. Looking further down the list of names, he saw his own name recorded there. Shortly thereafter, the official returned. Shi was still looking at the book, and the official said angrily, “I specifically warned you, yet you disregarded my words and looked at it!”

Shi <term>kowtowed<note>original: "叩頭" (kòutóu). A gesture of extreme apology or respect where one kneels and strikes their head against the ground.</note></term> until his forehead bled. After a long time, the official said, “I am moved by the fact that you carried me such a long distance. Though it is impossible to remove your name from this book, if you return home today and do not step outside your gate for three years, you may be able to pass through this fate. Do not speak of having seen my book.” Shi then returned home.

## Volume Five

He did not go out for more than two years, and his entire family found his behavior strange. Eventually, a neighbor passed away, and Shi’s father, growing angry, compelled him to go and offer his condolences.

Shi, having no choice, finally stepped out of the door. Immediately, he saw the same official from the boat. The official said, “I commanded you not to go out for three years, yet now you have come out! What can be done now? Because I could not find you to fulfill my orders, I was repeatedly <term>flogged<note>original: "鞭杖" (biānzhàng). Standard corporal punishment in ancient Chinese administration, here applied to a low-ranking spirit-official for failing his duties.</note></term> with a whip and a rod. Now that I have seen you, there is no way to avoid it. In three days, at midday, I shall come to take you.”

Shi returned home weeping and recounted everything that had happened. His father still refused to believe him, but his mother stayed by his side day and night to keep watch over him. When midday arrived on the third day, the official indeed came to take him, and he died instantly.

Zhang Zhu of <term>Nandun<note>An area in present-day Henan Province.</note></term> was planting grain in his fields when he found a plum pit. He intended to carry it away with him, but noticing a hollow in a <term>mulberry tree<note>Mulberry trees were essential to ancient Chinese life for sericulture (silk production) and often appear in folk tales as sites of transformation or spirits.</note></term> that contained some soil, he planted the pit inside. He then watered it with the remains of his <term>liquid dregs<note>original: "漿" (jiāng). Likely the starchy water from his midday meal of grain or gruel.</note></term>. Later, when other people saw plums growing from the middle of a mulberry tree, the news was passed from one person to another...

# In Search of the Supernatural

### Volume Eight

He announced to those suffering from eye ailments or pain: “Rest beneath the shade of this tree and say, ‘Master Li, please heal my eyes,’ then offer a pig in gratitude.” 

Now, eye pain is a minor affliction that often heals on its own, but as the proverb says, <note>original: "眾犬吠聲" (zhòng quǎn fèi shēng). Literally "one dog barks at a shadow, and a hundred dogs bark at the sound." It refers to how people blindly follow rumors without checking the facts.</note> “when one dog barks, the rest follow.” Soon, even the blind claimed they could see again. The fame of the tree spread far and wide until the area beneath it was constantly crowded with dozens or even hundreds of carriages and horses. Offerings of wine and meat flowed like torrential rain. 

About a year later, Zhang Zhu returned from his distant travels. Seeing the spectacle, he was astonished and said, “What ‘god’ could be here? This is just a tree I planted!” He then went to the tree and chopped it down.

---

When <term>Wang Mang<note>A powerful official who eventually seized the throne from the Han Dynasty, establishing the short-lived Xin ("New") Dynasty (9–23 CE). His reign was marked by his use of "miraculous" omens to justify his rule.</note></term> was serving as the <term>Acting Emperor<note>original: "居攝" (jūshè). This title indicated that Wang Mang was exercising imperial power on behalf of a child emperor while preparing to take the throne himself.</note></term>, an official named Liu Jing submitted a report. 

He stated that Xin Dang, the <term>Pavilion Chief<note>original: "亭長" (tíngzhǎng). A low-level local official in charge of a "pavilion," which served as both a police outpost and a station for traveling officials.</note></term> of Changxing in Linzi County, Qi Commandery, had repeatedly dreamed of a man who told him: 

“I am a messenger from Heaven. The Acting Emperor should now become the true Emperor. If you do not believe me, a new well shall appear within this pavilion.” 

When the Pavilion Chief rose and inspected the grounds, he indeed found a new well in the pavilion, reaching a depth of one hundred feet <note>A "foot" (chi) in the Han Dynasty was approximately 23 centimeters, making the well roughly 23 meters deep.</note>.

# In Search of the Supernatural, Volume Five

<insert>Library Seal of the Qinghua Pavilion</insert>

### The Legend of Master Li

He announced to those suffering from eye ailments or pain: “Rest beneath the shade of this tree and say, ‘Master Li, please heal my eyes,’ then offer a pig in gratitude.” 

<note>The name "Master Li" is a play on words; "Li" (李) is the Chinese word for "plum," referring to the plum pit Zhang Zhu had originally planted in the hollow mulberry tree.</note>

Now, eye pain is a minor affliction that often heals on its own, but as the proverb says, <note>original: "眾犬吠聲" (zhòng quǎn fèi shēng). Literally "when one dog barks at a shadow, a hundred dogs bark at the sound." It refers to how people blindly follow rumors or trends without verifying the truth.</note> “when one dog barks, the rest follow.” Soon, even the blind claimed they could see again. The fame of the tree spread far and wide until the area beneath it was constantly crowded with dozens or even hundreds of carriages and horses. Offerings of wine and meat flowed like torrential rain. 

About a year later, Zhang Zhu returned from his distant travels. Seeing the spectacle, he was astonished and said, “What ‘god’ could be here? This is just a tree I planted!” He then went to the tree and chopped it down.

---

This title indicated that Wang Mang was exercising imperial power on behalf of a child emperor while preparing to take the throne himself.</note></term>, an official named Liu Jing submitted a report. 

He stated that Xin Dang, the <term>Pavilion Chief<note>original: "亭長" (tíngzhǎng). A low-level local official responsible for public order and a roadside "pavilion" which served as a post house for travelers.</note></term>, had discovered a po... <unclear>illegible</unclear>

# By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## In Search of the Supernatural: Volume 6

->Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty<-

Anomalies <note>Original: 妖怪 (yaoguai). In this context, the author refers to supernatural disturbances, monsters, or strange portents rather than just folklore creatures.</note> are essentially the vital essence and spirit of things as they attach themselves to physical objects. When the internal energy is disrupted, changes manifest in the external form of things. The relationship between spirit and energy, and form and substance, is like the relationship between the internal and the external. These phenomena are rooted in the <term>Five Elements<note>Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water; the fundamental building blocks of the universe in traditional Chinese philosophy.</note></term> and are connected to the <term>Five Matters<note>Appearance, speech, vision, hearing, and thought—five human behaviors believed to influence the natural order.</note></term>. Although their waxing and waning, rising and falling, and transformations are ten thousand-fold, the omens of good and bad fortune can all be categorized and analyzed.

In the time of King Jie of the Xia dynasty <note>The last ruler of the Xia, traditionally depicted as a tyrant.</note>, Mount Li disappeared. In the time of the First Emperor of Qin, the Three Mountains disappeared. In the thirty-second year of King Xian of Zhou, the Great Altar of the Soil at Daqiu in the state of Song disappeared. Near the end of the reign of Emperor Zhao of the Han dynasty, the Altar of the Soil at Changyi in Chenliu disappeared. Jing Fang’s <term>Commentary on the Book of Changes<note>An influential Han dynasty text that interpreted natural disasters and celestial movements as political omens.</note></term> states: “When mountains silently move themselves, the world will be engulfed in war, and the <term>altars of soil and grain<note>Original: 社稷 (sheji); a term representing the state and the right to rule.</note></term> will perish.” Therefore, at Shanyin in Kuaiji...

<keywords>Gan Bao, anomalies, Five Elements, Five Matters, Mount Li, Altars of Soil and Grain, Jing Fang, portents, natural disasters</keywords>

# By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 6

In the region of Langya <note>An ancient commandery located in what is now southeastern Shandong province.</note>, there is a place known as **Strange Mountain**. Local legend says that it was originally a mountain located in the sea near Dongwu in Langya. One night, amidst a violent storm and pitch-black darkness, the mountain suddenly appeared at dawn. The common people were astonished by this event, and so they named it **Strange Mountain**. 

At that same time, the mountain in Dongwu County had disappeared in a single night. Those who recognized the mountain's shape and profile realized it had moved to its new location. Today, at the foot of Strange Mountain, there is a settlement called **Dongwu Village**; it was likely given this name to record the mountain’s place of origin.

Additionally, a mountain from Cuizhou in Jiaozhou <note>An ancient province covering parts of modern-day northern Vietnam and the Liangguang region of China.</note> moved to Qingzhou <note>A coastal province in modern-day Shandong.</note>. Generally speaking, the migration of mountains is considered an extreme and ominous anomaly. The specific historical eras in which these two events occurred are not detailed in the records.

The **Golden Cord** <note>Original: 金滕 (Jin Teng), a famous chapter of the *Classic of History* (*Shangshu*) concerning King Wu and the Duke of Zhou.</note> states: 

> "When mountains shift, it is because the ruler does not follow the <term>Way<note>The Dao, or the proper moral and cosmic path of a righteous sovereign.</note></term>, and worthy scholars are not promoted. It may also signify that authority and wealth have departed from the royal house, rewards and punishments are no longer determined by the monarch, and private factions have formed groups that cannot be restrained. These events portend a <term>Change of Era<note>Original: 易世 (Yi Shi), referring to the fall of one dynasty and the rise of another.</note></term> and a change in the dynastic title."

A commentary says: "Those who speak well of the heavens must find evidence in human affairs; those who speak well of human affairs must..."

<summary>This page records legends of mountains physically moving across great distances, framing these geological "anomalies" as divine warnings of political corruption and impending dynastic change.</summary>
<keywords>Strange Mountain, Langya, Dongwu, mountain migration, Book of Documents, political portents, Gan Bao, Jin Dynasty</keywords>

# In Search of the Supernatural
## Page 2

All life is rooted in Heaven; therefore, Heaven possesses the **four seasons**, the sun and moon push one another in their cycles, and cold and heat alternate in their rotation. When the breath of Heaven is in harmony, it becomes **rain**; when in anger, it becomes **wind**; when dispersed, it becomes **dew**; when disordered, it becomes **mist**; when condensed, it becomes **frost and snow**; and when it stands forth, it becomes the **rainbow**. These are the regular patterns of Heaven.

Similarly, humans possess **four limbs** and **five internal organs** <note>The "Wu Zang": Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys.</note>, cycles of waking and sleeping, and the inhalation and exhalation of breath. Our vital essence and energy move back and forth; when they flow, they form the <term>nutritive and defensive systems<note>Original: 榮衛 (Rong Wei). In traditional medicine, these are two types of energy: "Ying" (nutritive) which circulates within the blood vessels, and "Wei" (defensive) which protects the body's surface.</note></term>. When they manifest outwardly, they appear as the **complexion**; when expressed, they become the **voice**. These are also the regular patterns of the human being.

If the four seasons lose their rhythm and the cold and heat clash, then the <term>five planets<note>Original: 五緯 (Wu Wei), the "Five Latitudes." These are the five planets visible to the naked eye: Jupiter, Mars, Saturn, Venus, and Mercury.</note></term> will swell or shrink, stars will move erratically out of their courses, solar and lunar eclipses will occur, and **comets** <note>Original: 彗孛 (Hui Bei). These were historically viewed as "broom stars" or "bushy stars," appearing as omens of political or natural upheaval.</note> will fly across the sky. These are the warning signs of a crisis between Heaven and Earth.

Unseasonable cold and heat are the signs of stagnation and "indigestion" within Heaven and Earth. Stones that stand up or earth that surges upward are the **tumors and growths** of Heaven and Earth. Collapsing mountains and sinking ground are the **ulcers and abscesses** <note>Original: 癰疽 (Yong Ju). These terms specifically refer to deep-seated, painful sores or carbuncles, suggesting the Earth is literally "infected."</note> of Heaven and Earth. Violent winds...

<summary>This page presents a classic Chinese philosophical argument that the universe and the human body operate on the same principles, framing natural disasters as "medical" symptoms of a cosmic imbalance.</summary>
<keywords>macrocosm-microcosm, traditional Chinese medicine, natural disasters as omens, celestial phenomena, Sou Shen Ji, five organs, five planets</keywords>

## Volume 6

**Torrential rain** is the rushing breath of Heaven and Earth. When the life-giving rain does not descend and the rivers and streams dry up, this is the **scorched exhaustion** of Heaven and Earth.

During the era of **King Zhou of Shang** <note>The final, infamously tyrannical ruler of the Shang Dynasty (r. c. 1075–1046 BCE).</note>, large tortoises grew hair and rabbits grew horns. These were omens that soldiers and armor were about to be raised.

In the thirty-third year of **King Xuan of Zhou**, King You was born. In that same year, a horse transformed into a **fox**.

In the second year of **Duke Xian of Jin**, King Hui of Zhou was residing in the state of Zheng. When the people of Zheng entered the royal treasury, many creatures were found to have molted and transformed into **sand-spitters** <term>Yu<note>Original: 蜮 (Yù). A legendary aquatic creature believed to shoot sand at the shadows of people on the bank; if the sand hit the shadow, the person would fall ill or die.</note></term>

In the Zhou era, one zhang was approximately 2.3 meters.</note> in length and over a foot and a half <note>Original: 一尺五寸 (Yi chi wu cun), approximately 35 centimeters.</note> in height. **Jing Fang** <note>A Han dynasty scholar (77–37 BCE) famous for his work on the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) and his theories on catastrophe and omens.</note> states in his *Omens from the Book of Changes*: "If the earth suddenly grows during the four seasons, the divination suggests much good fortune in the spring and summer, but much disaster in the autumn and winter." One night, an entire commandery sank deep into the earth and became a marshy lake; this is the present-day **Lake Lihu** <note>Original: 歷湖 (Lìhú). In the eighth year of **King Ai of Zhou** <note>c. 441 BCE.</note>, a woman in the state of Zheng gave birth to forty children. Twenty of them survived as human beings, while the other twenty died. In the following ninth year, a pig in the state of Jin gave birth to a human. In the seventh year of the **Chiwu** era of the state of Wu <note>244 CE, during the Three Kingdoms period.</note>, a woman gave birth to three sons at once.

In the sixth year of **King Lie of Zhou** <note>c. 370 BCE.</note>, an attendant of the Lord of Biyang in the state of Lin gave birth to two dragons.

In the eighth year of **Duke Zhuang of Lu** <note>694 BCE.</note>, Duke Xiang of Qi was hunting at Beiqiu. He saw a pig, but his attendants cried out, "It is **Prince Pengsheng**!" <note>Prince Pengsheng was a noble whom Duke Xiang had previously murdered to cover up a scandal. The pig was interpreted as his vengeful ghost.</note> The Duke was enraged and shot an arrow at it. The pig stood upright like a human and howled. Struck with terror, the Duke fell from his carriage, injuring his foot and losing his sandal. —**Liu Xiang** <note>A famous scholar of the Han Dynasty (77–6 BCE) known for recording portents and moral tales.</note>

->...this was considered a "Pig Calamity." <note>Original: 豕禍 (shǐhuò). This refers back to the story on the preceding page regarding Duke Xiang of Qi seeing a pig that spoke with the voice of the deceased Prince Pengsheng.</note><-

During the reign of **Duke Zhuang of Lu** <note>r. 693–662 BCE.</note>, an "inner snake" and an "outer snake" fought within the southern gate of the state of Zheng. The inner snake was killed. **Liu Xiang** <note>A prominent Han dynasty scholar (77–6 BCE) and bibliographer who compiled many accounts of omens.</note> considered this a "Snake Portent" <note>Original: 蛇孽 (shéniè), implying a monstrous or unnatural occurrence involving snakes.</note>. **Jing Fang’s** <note>A Han dynasty expert (77–37 BCE) on the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) who linked natural anomalies to government failures.</note> *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When the appointment of the heir-apparent is in doubt, snake-demons take up residence in the gates of the capital."

### Battles

In the nineteenth year of **Duke Zhao of Lu** <note>523 BCE.</note>, dragons fought in the Wei Deep <note>Original: 洧淵 (Wěiyuān). A deep pool or section of the Wei River near the capital of Zheng.</note> outside the gates of Zheng. **Liu Xiang** considered this a "Dragon Portent." **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When the hearts of the masses are uneasy, dragon-demons will battle within their cities."

In the first year of **Duke Ding of Lu** <note>509 BCE.</note>, nine snakes were found wound around a pillar. A divination interpreted this as a sign that the Temple of the Nine Generations was no longer receiving proper sacrifices. Consequently, the **Yang Palace** <note>A shrine dedicated to Duke Yang of Lu, intended to rectify the neglected ancestral lineage.</note> was established.

# Records of the Unnatural
## Volume 6: Portents of Animals and Humans

In the twenty-first year of **Duke Xiao of Qin** <note>341 BCE. Duke Xiao is famous for employing Shang Yang to reform the Qin state.</note>, a horse gave birth to a human. In the twentieth year of **King Zhao** <note>Referring to King Zhaoxiang of Qin, 287 BCE.</note>, a male horse gave birth to a foal and then died. **Liu Xiang** <note>A Han dynasty scholar who specialized in interpreting omens as warnings to the throne.</note> considered these to be "Horse Calamities" <note>Original: 馬禍 (*mǎhuò*). In ancient Chinese thought, horses were associated with the "Yang" principle and the heavens; irregularities in their birth were seen as grave political omens.</note>. **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* <note>Jing Fang (77–37 BCE) was a master of the *I Ching* who developed a system linking natural disasters to specific government failures.</note> states: "When the regional lords divide the ruler's authority, the omen is a male horse giving birth. When there is no Son of Heaven <note>The rightful supreme emperor.</note> presiding above and the feudal lords attack one another, the omen is a horse giving birth to a human."

In the thirteenth year of **King Xiang of Wei** <note>306 BCE.</note>, a woman transformed into a man, took a wife, and fathered a child. **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "A woman transforming into a man is called 'The Prosperity of Yin' <note>Yin (陰) represents the feminine, the submissive, and the dark. For it to "prosper" or dominate was seen as a reversal of the natural order.</note>. It signifies that a person of low status will become a king. A man transforming into a woman is called 'Yin Overcoming Yang'; the fault lies in total destruction." Another interpretation states: "A man transforming into a woman signifies that the punishment of castration <note>Original: 宮刑 (*gōngxíng*), one of the five ancient physical punishments.</note> is being used excessively; a woman transforming into a man signifies that women are controlling the administration of government."

In the fifth year of **King Xiaowen of Qin** <note>251 BCE. He reigned for only a very short time.</note>, while the King was traveling in Quyan <note>A district located in the western regions of the Qin state.</note>, someone presented him with a five-legged ox. At that time, the Qin state was heavily exploiting the labor of the common people <note>An extra limb on a draft animal like an ox was often interpreted as a sign of the "extra" or excessive labor demanded from the peasantry.</note>.

<summary>This page catalogs biological anomalies—such as a male horse giving birth and human gender reversals—interpreting them through the scholarship of Liu Xiang and Jing Fang as omens of political chaos and social upheaval.</summary>
<keywords>Duke Xiao of Qin, King Zhaoxiang of Qin, King Xiang of Wei, Horse Calamity, gender transformation, Yin and Yang, Five-legged ox, Jing Fang, Liu Xiang, omens</keywords>

# Records of the Unnatural
## Volume 6

When the realm rebels against the ruler, **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* <note>Jing Fang (77–37 BCE) was a scholar who interpreted natural disasters as expressions of "Heaven's" displeasure with the Emperor's conduct.</note> states: "When corvée labor <term>繇役 (*yáoyì*): mandatory, unpaid labor for state projects like walls or roads</term> is mobilized excessively and the people's agricultural seasons are stolen, the resulting omen is a cow born with five legs."

In the twenty-sixth year of the **First Emperor of Qin** <note>221 BCE, the year Qin Shi Huang completed his conquest of the other Warring States.</note>, twelve giants appeared in Lintao <note>A border town in modern Gansu, near where the Great Wall began.</note>. They stood five *zhang* tall <note>Approximately 38 feet or 11.5 meters.</note> and their footprints were six *chi* <note>About 4.5 feet or 1.4 meters.</note> long. They were all dressed in barbarian robes. Consequently, the Emperor collected and melted down all weapons in the empire to cast twelve **Bronze Statues** <note>Original: 金人 (*jīnrén*), literally 'Metal Men'. These legendary massive statues were meant to symbolize the disarmament of the people and the permanence of Qin rule.</note> in their likeness.

In the second year of **Emperor Hui of Han** <note>193 BCE.</note>, on the morning of the *guiyou* day of the first lunar month, two dragons appeared in the Wenling Well within the Dongli neighborhood of the Lanling district. They remained until the night of the *yihai* day before departing. **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When the virtuous are harmed, the omen is a dragon appearing in a well." It also says: "When the application of punishments is cruel and wicked, a black dragon will emerge from a well."

In the twelfth year of **Emperor Wen of Han** <note>168 BCE.</note>, in the region of Wu <note>In the southeast, modern-day Jiangsu/Zhejiang area.</note>, a horse was born with horns. They were located in front of the ears and pointed upward; the right horn was three [inches] long—
