# Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 6

[...It] is not proper for [such a creature] to grow horns, just as it is improper for the feudal lords to dare raise an army against the capital. Shortly thereafter, the Disaster of the Seven States <note>A significant rebellion in 154 BCE where seven regional princes challenged the central authority of the Han dynasty.</note> broke out. Later, in the fifth year of the Taishi era of **Emperor Wu of Jin** <note>269 CE. Emperor Wu was the founder of the Jin dynasty.</note>, a seventy-year-old man in Yuancheng grew a horn. This was likely a prophetic response to the usurpation and chaos later caused by **Sima Lun, Prince of Zhao** <note>Sima Lun briefly seized the throne in 301 CE, a key event in the "War of the Eight Princes" that destabilized the Jin dynasty.</note>.

In the third year of **Emperor Jing of Han** <note>154 BCE.</note>, a dog and a pig mated in the city of Handan. At that time, the Prince of Zhao was behaving perversely and rebelliously, eventually joining the other six states in their revolt. **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When the roles of husband and wife are not strictly observed, the resulting anomaly is a dog mating with a pig. This is known as 'overturning virtue,' and the state will experience military conflict <term>兵革 (*bīnggé*): literally 'weapons and leather armor,' a term referring to warfare or armed rebellion</term>."

In the eleventh month of that same year, a flock of white-necked crows and black crows fought one another. In Lü County of the State of Chu, the white-necked crows were defeated and fell into the Si River; the dead numbered in the thousands. **Liu Xiang** believed this was a "White-Black Omen" <note>original: 白黑祥 (*bái hēi xiáng*). Liu Xiang used color-coded categories of omens to explain how imbalances in nature reflected imbalances in the social or political order.</note>.

<summary>This page details biological anomalies—such as a human growing a horn, interspecies mating, and a massive battle between crows—interpreting them as divine warnings of specific political rebellions during the Han and Jin dynasties.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Jing of Han, Emperor Wu of Jin, Prince of Zhao, rebellion, animal omens, Jing Fang, Liu Xiang, Disaster of the Seven States</keywords>

# Records of Searching for the Spirits
## Page 81

...numbering in the thousands. At that time, **Prince Wu of Chu** <note>Liu Wu (died 154 BCE), a member of the imperial family who participated in the Rebellion of the Seven States.</note> was violent, rebellious, and lacked all moral principle; he had even subjected **Lord Shen** <note>Shen Pei, a famous Confucian scholar and advisor whom the Prince had humiliated for his honest counsel.</note> to the punishment of hard labor. While the Prince plotted rebellion alongside the State of **Wu**, the fighting among the flocks of birds served as an omen of armies at war. The "white-necked" birds represented the smaller or subordinate party; it was a clear sign that the smaller party would be defeated. That they "fell into the water" signified that the Prince would meet his death in a watery place. 

**Prince Wu** did not realize the meaning of this sign and subsequently raised his troops to join the State of **Wu** in a great battle against the **Han** imperial forces. His army was defeated and he fled, eventually reaching **Dantu**, where he was decapitated by the people of **Yue**. This fulfilled the omen of the birds falling into the **Si River**.

**Jing Fang’s** <note>A famous Han dynasty scholar and expert on the *I Ching* (Book of Changes).</note> *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When one rebels against their own kin, the resulting anomaly is white and black birds fighting within the capital."

During the rebellion of **Prince Dan of Yan** <note>Liu Dan, who plotted to seize the throne from Emperor Zhao of Han in 80 BCE.</note>, there was also an incident where a bird and a magpie fought over the pond in the palace of **Yan**. The bird fell into the pond and died. The *Treatise on the Five Elements* <note>A section of the *Book of Han* (Hanshu) that records and interprets natural disasters and omens.</note> considers that the Princes of **Chu** and **Yan** were both imperial kin and "vassal ministers" <term>藩臣 (*fānchén*): regional rulers who served as "fences" or protectors of the central throne</term> who, in their arrogance and indulgence, plotted unrighteousness. Both experienced the omen of birds and magpies fighting to the death. Because their actions were the same, the divinations coincided; this is a clear manifestation of the resonance between **Heaven and Man**.

<summary>This page concludes the account of the crow omen, explaining how the birds' deaths predicted the defeat and execution of the rebellious Prince Wu of Chu. It further links these events to the broader Han dynasty theory that natural anomalies serve as divine warnings against political corruption and rebellion.</summary>

In the case of **Yan**, the conspiracy had not yet been launched, and only the **King** <note>Prince Dan of Yan, who committed suicide in 80 BCE after his plot against Emperor Zhao was discovered.</note> took his own life within the palace; therefore, the omen involved only a single bird, and the one whose feathers were the color of water <note>original: "水色者" (shuǐsè zhě). ### The Fighting of Birds and Magpies

Therefore, the **Ox Calamity** appeared: a leg emerging from the back is a symbol of a subordinate offending a superior <note>In traditional Chinese symbolism, the back (top) represents the ruler/superior, while the feet (bottom) represent the subjects/subordinates.</note>.

# In Search of the Supernatural
## Volume Six

->Page Seven<-

Below the Temple of **Guan Xiaowen** <note>Likely referring to a local shrine dedicated to a regional deity or an ancestral figure in a specific township.</note> in the local district, a snake died. Two years later, in the autumn, the incident of **Crown Prince Wei** <note>Also known as the "Witchcraft Scandal" of 91 BCE, where Crown Prince Liu Ju was framed for sorcery, leading to a massive bloody conflict and his eventual suicide.</note> occurred; this crisis was initiated by **Jiang Chong**, a man from the state of **Zhao**.

In the ninth month of the first year of the **Yuanfeng** era <note>80 BCE.</note> during the reign of **Emperor Zhao of Han**, yellow rats in the state of **Yan** were seen holding their own tails in their mouths and dancing in the center of the palace’s **Duan Gate** <note>The principal or ceremonial gate of a royal palace.</note>. The **King** <note>Prince Dan of Yan, who harbored ambitions for the throne.</note> went to observe them, and the rats continued to dance as before. The King ordered his officials to offer a sacrifice of wine and dried meat to the creatures, but the rats did not stop their dancing. After a day and a night, they died. This was an omen of the impending death of the **King of Yan**, who was then plotting a rebellion. **Jing Fang’s** <note>A famous scholar of the Han Dynasty (77–37 BCE) known for interpreting the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) to explain natural disasters.</note> *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When executions are carried out without investigating the true circumstances <note>original: "誅不原情" (zhū bù yuán qíng).</note>, the anomaly of rats dancing at the gate will appear."

In the first month of the third year of the **Yuanfeng** era <note>77 BCE.</note>, at the south of **Laiwu Mountain** in **Mount Tai**, there arose a tumultuous sound like that of several thousand people. When the common people went to investigate the noise, they found a massive stone that had stood itself up. It was fifteen feet tall and measured forty-eight spans <note>term: <term>圍</term> (wéi); a traditional measurement of circumference, roughly the distance of a person’s outstretched arms.</note> in circumference, and it was rooted deep into the earth.

<summary>This page details three supernatural omens from the Han Dynasty: a dead snake prefiguring the Crown Prince Wei scandal, dancing rats signaling the failed rebellion of the King of Yan, and a massive stone at Mount Tai that moved on its own.</summary>

...[The object was] eight feet deep, with three stones serving as its feet. At the location where these stones stood, thousands of white birds gathered beside them. This was an **auspicious omen** <term>Rui (瑞): a favorable sign from heaven or nature indicating a ruler’s virtue or a divine mandate.</term> of the **Restoration** <note>Referring to the "Zhongxing" or mid-reign revival during the reign of Emperor Xuan of Han (r. 74–48 BCE), who stabilized the dynasty after a period of internal strife.</note> of **Emperor Xuan**.

During the reign of **Emperor Zhao**, a massive willow tree in the **Shanglin Garden** <note>The vast imperial park and hunting grounds located near the capital, Chang'an.</note> snapped and fell to the ground. One morning, it suddenly stood back up and began to grow new branches and leaves. Insects began to eat these leaves, and the patterns they left behind formed written characters that said: "**Gongsun Bingyi shall be established.**" <note>This was a cryptic prophecy. "Bingyi" was the childhood name of Liu Xun, the future Emperor Xuan. At the time, he was living as a commoner because his grandfather, the former Crown Prince, had been killed in a coup attempt.</note>

During the same reign, **He, the King of Changyi** <note>Liu He, a grandson of Emperor Wu who was known for his eccentric and often inappropriate behavior; he famously served as Emperor for only 27 days before being deposed.</note>, saw a large white dog. It was wearing a **Fangshan cap** <note>A tall, rectangular style of headwear typically worn by palace attendants or certain officials.</note> but had no tail. 

Later, during the **Xiping** era <note>172–178 CE, during the reign of Emperor Ling of the Eastern Han.</note>, it became a source of amusement within the palace to dress dogs in caps and official sashes for sport and laughter. On one occasion, one such dog suddenly bolted and ran into the gates of the **Ministry of Works** <term>Sikong (司空): One of the highest-ranking cabinet positions in the imperial government, responsible for construction and waterworks.</term>. Those who saw it were struck with terror and alarm at the strangeness of the sight. **Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* states: "When the ruler is not upright and ministers harbor desires to usurp power, the **monster** <term>Yao (妖): a supernatural anomaly or "freak" of nature that serves as a warning of political disorder.</term> of the dog in a cap appears at the gates of the court."

# Records of the Divine
## Vol. 6: Portents and Anomalies

->**The Chronicle of Emperor Xuan and Emperor Yuan**<-

In the first year of the **Huanglong** era <note>49 BCE</note> of **Emperor Xuan of Han**, within the carriage components <note>original: "輅輹" (lufu), referring to the axle or structural parts of the imperial carriage.</note> of the **Weiyang Palace**, a hen transformed into a rooster. Its plumage changed, yet it did not crow, nor did it lead the flock or possess spurs.

In the first year of the **Chuyuan** era <note>48 BCE</note> of **Emperor Yuan**, at the home of a clerk in the **Chancellor’s Office**, a hen that was brooding over her chicks gradually transformed into a rooster. It developed a comb and spurs and began to crow. Later, during the **Yongguang** era <note>43–39 BCE</note>, someone presented a rooster to the court that had grown horns.

The **Treatise on the Five Elements** <note>The *Wuxing Zhi*, a section of the *Book of Han* that records natural disasters and omens as reflections of the government's health.</note> considers these events to be omens of the **Wang clan’s** rise <note>The family of Wang Mang, who would eventually seize the throne and establish the short-lived Xin Dynasty.</note>. **Jing Fang’s** <note>A famous scholar of the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) known for his theories on music and hexagram-based prophecy.</note> **Commentary on the Book of Changes** states: “When a sage lives in the age of **Mingyi** <term>Mingyi (明夷): The 36th hexagram of the I Ching, literally 'Darkening of the Light,' symbolizing a time of hardship where one must hide their brilliance.</term>, he knows the timing and suffers injury; or if unworthy men occupy the high positions, this monster—the horned chicken—is born.” He further states: “When women dominate the government, the state is not at peace; when the hen crows like a rooster, the master lacks glory.”

When the time came to divide the property, the wife, and the children, they could not reach an agreement and brought their dispute to the courts. **Fan Yanshou**, the **Commandant of Justice** <note>The highest legal official in the central government.</note>, ruled on the case, saying: “This...”

"They are not like humans; they should be treated like birds and beasts, which follow the mother and not the father. I request that the three sons be executed and the children be returned to the mother."

Emperor Xuan sighed and said: "Why must matters always follow ancient precedent? A judgment such as this may be called 'according to reason' and 'satisfying to human sentiment.'" <note>The Emperor is praising a flexible application of the law that acknowledges the emotional and social reality of the case rather than strictly adhering to archaic codes.</note>

**Yanshou** <note>Han Yanshou, a famous Han dynasty official known for his emphasis on etiquette and moral transformation.</note> became increasingly observant of human affairs and learned the proper use of punishments. However, it is not yet known whether his discourse on **human portents** <term>Renyao (人妖): In this historical context, the term refers to "human anomalies" or unnatural behaviors and births that were believed to predict political disaster.</term> would be verified by future events.

In the eighth month of the second year of the **Yongguang** era <note>42 BCE</note> of **Emperor Yuan of Han**, it rained grass from the heavens. The leaves were twisted and knotted together, becoming as large as pellets. 

In the first month of the third year of the **Yuanshi** era <note>3 CE</note> of **Emperor Ping**, it rained grass again, appearing just as it had during the **Yongguang** era. 

**Jing Fang’s Commentary on the Book of Changes** <note>A classic text by the scholar Jing Fang, who linked changes in the natural world to the conduct of the imperial court.</note> states: "When the ruler is stingy with rewards and salaries, when trust declines and worthy men depart the court, the resulting omen is a rain of grass."

# Records of Searching for Spirits

### Page 9

In the shrine of **Tuomao Township** <note>original: 橐茅鄉; a locality in the Han dynasty</note>, there was a large locust tree. An official had it cut down, but that very night, the tree stood back up again in its original place.

The commentary says: Generally, whenever a tree that is withered or severed rises again, it is an omen of something that was discarded being restored to power. This was the response to the rise of **Shizu** <term>Shizu (世祖): The temple name of Emperor Guangwu, who "restored" the Han Dynasty by establishing the Eastern Han after the usurpation of Wang Mang.</term>.

---

In the ninth month of the fourth year of the **Jianshi** era <note>29 BCE</note> of **Emperor Cheng of Han**, south of the city of **Chang'an**, rats were seen carrying yellow straw and cypress leaves into the trees. They built nests in the cypress and elm trees of commoners' homes, though they were most numerous in the paulownia and cypress trees. Inside these nests, there were no young rats, only dozens of pieces of dried rat dung.

At the time, the court officials believed this portended a coming flood disaster. However, others argued: "Rats are thieving, lowly creatures that usually come out at night and hide during the day. Now, they leave their holes in broad daylight and climb into the trees; this is a sign that **lowly people will soon occupy high and noble positions**."

The paulownia and cypress trees were the location of the garden of **Empress Weisi** <note>Wei Zifu, the Empress of Emperor Wu, who rose from a lowly singing girl to become Empress</note>. Later, **Empress Zhao** <note>Zhao Feiyan, a dancer of humble origins who became the Empress of Emperor Cheng</note> rose from a similarly lowly and obscure background to reach the highest imperial rank, matching the status of the **Wei** family.

<summary>This page describes two significant omens: a fallen tree that miraculously righted itself to predict the Han Dynasty's restoration, and rats nesting in trees to foretell the rise of commoner women to the rank of Empress.</summary>
<keywords>Tuomao Township, Emperor Guangwu (Shizu), Emperor Cheng of Han, Empress Zhao Feiyan, Empress Wei Zifu, omens, portents, natural anomalies</keywords>

Empress Zhao <note>original: 趙后; referring to Zhao Feiyan, the consort of Emperor Cheng</note> was of the same lowly class as these omens; she ultimately produced no sons and became a source of harm to the imperial line. In the following year, there was a further omen of a swallow burning its own nest and killing its chicks.

---

In the first year of the **Heping** era <note>28 BCE</note> of **Emperor Cheng**, two men from **Chang'an** named **Shi Liang** and **Liu Yin** were living together. A figure appearing to be human appeared in their room. When they struck it, the figure turned into a dog and ran away. Later, several people wearing armor and carrying bows and crossbows arrived at Liang’s house. Liang and his companions fought them; some were killed and others wounded, but upon inspection, they were all found to be dogs. These events continued from the second month until the sixth month before they finally ceased. 

According to the **Great Plan** <term>Great Plan (洪範): A chapter of the *Book of Documents* used by Han scholars to correlate natural disasters with the "Five Elements" and government failures</term>, these events are classified as **dog disasters** <note>original: 犬禍; interpreted as a warning of social disorder</note>. They are said to be the divine punishment for the "fault of words not being followed" <note>This refers to the sovereign failing to heed good advice or issuing improper commands</note>.

---

In the second month of the first year of the **Heping** era, on the day of *Gengzi*, a swallow in the **Shansang Valley** of **Mount Tai** burned its own nest. A man named **Sun Tong** and several others heard the sounds of a great flock of birds and magpies coming from the mountains. When they went to investigate, they saw that the nest and all the chicks within had fallen to the ground.

In a pond, there were three **guangu** <term>guangu (雚穀): A type of water bird, likely a cormorant or heron; their name was often associated with fire omens in ancient Chinese phonology</term> that were burned to death. The tree they occupied was four spans in circumference, and their nest was located fifty-five feet above the ground. The **Book of Changes** <note>original: 易; the *I Ching*</note> states: "The bird burns its nest; the traveler first laughs and then wails aloud." <note>original: 鳥焚其巢旅人先笑後號咷</note> This was later seen as a precursor to the calamity of the changing era <note>referring to the fall of the Western Han dynasty and the usurpation by Wang Mang</note>.

In the autumn of the fourth year of the **Hongjia** era of **Emperor Cheng** <note>17 BCE</note>, it rained fish at **Xindu** <note>in modern-day Hebei province</note>; the fish were all five inches or less in length. Then, in the spring of the first year of the **Yongshi** era <note>16 BCE</note>, four giant fish emerged from the **North Sea** <note>Beihai</note>; they were sixty feet long and ten feet high. 

In the third year of the **Jianping** era of **Emperor Ai** <note>4 BCE</note>, seven giant fish emerged at **Pingdu** in **Donglai**; they were eighty feet long and eleven feet high. They were all found dead.

In the second year of the **Xiping** era of **Emperor Ling** <note>173 CE</note>, two giant fish emerged from the sea at **Donglai**; they were eighty to ninety feet long and over twenty feet high. The **Commentary on the Book of Changes by Jing Fang** <note>original: 京房易傳</note> states: "When giant fish are seen repeatedly in the ocean, it signifies that wicked men are being promoted while virtuous men are being silenced by the throne."

In the tenth month of the third year of the **Jianping** era of **Emperor Ai** <note>4 BCE</note>, at **Suixiang** in **Xiping**, **Runan** <note>located in modern-day Henan province</note>, a tree fell to the ground and sprouted a branch shaped like a human being. At this time, **Wang Mang** was serving as the **Grand Marshal** <term>Grand Marshal (大司馬 - Da Sima): The highest military and administrative post in the Han government</term>; the seeds of his plot to harm the Emperor began at this moment.

In the third month of the second year of the **Suihe** era of **Emperor Cheng**, at **Xiangping** in **Tianshui** <note>modern-day Gansu province</note>, a swallow gave birth to a sparrow. The swallow nursed and fed it until it was fully grown, at which point they both flew away together. The **Commentary on the Book of Changes by Jing Fang** states: "When treacherous ministers are within the state, the calamity is represented by a swallow giving birth to a sparrow; the power of the feudal lords will be diminished." --- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronicling of natural omens and biological anomalies during the reign of Emperor Ai of the Western Han dynasty. It follows a sequence of portents—including strange growths and animal mutations—that were historically interpreted as warnings of the impending collapse of the Liu imperial family and the rise of female or outsider influence in the court.</meta>

It is said: "If a creature gives birth to something not of its own kind, its offspring shall not inherit the throne." <note>This opening statement establishes the core theme of the page: that biological "monsters" or "anomalies" are direct signs of a broken line of succession.</note>

In the third year of the **Jianping** <note>4 BCE</note> era of <term>Emperor Ai of Han</term> <note>original: 漢哀帝; reigned 7–1 BCE</note>, at **Dingxiang** <note>a commandery in northern China, near modern-day Inner Mongolia</note>, a mare gave birth to a foal with only three legs. It followed the rest of the herd to eat and drink. The **Treatise on the Five Elements** <note>original: 五行志; a section of the *Book of Han* that records natural disasters as political omens</note> considers the horse to be a symbol of the state's military power; a horse with only three legs is therefore a sign of being unfit for service or use.

In the third year of the **Jianping** era of **Emperor Ai**, at **Lingling** <note>modern-day Hunan province</note>, there was a tree lying dead on the ground. Its circumference was sixteen feet and its length was one hundred seventy-seven feet. Local people had cut off the root section, which was over nine feet long, and the wood was entirely withered. However, after three months, the tree suddenly stood upright again in its original location.

The **Commentary on the Book of Changes by Jing Fang** <note>original: 京房易傳; a famous Han-era text that used natural disasters to critique government failings</note> states: "When the righteous path is abandoned and licentiousness arises, the omen of 'wood' is seen when a severed trunk reattaches itself. This indicates that a <term>consort or empress</term> <note>original: 妃后; refers to the high-ranking women of the imperial palace</note> will attain conspicuous power. This is manifested when a fallen tree stands back up, or when a severed, withered trunk returns to life."

In the fourth month of the fourth year of the **Jianping** era of **Emperor Ai** <note>3 BCE</note>, at **Fangyu** in **Sanyang** <note>modern-day Shandong province</note>, a woman named **Tian Wufan** gave birth to a child... <note>Historical records usually complete this entry by describing a miraculous or strange birth, often involving a child who could speak immediately, signaling further social upheaval.</note>

<summary>This page records three specific omens from the reign of Emperor Ai: a three-legged foal, a dead tree that miraculously stood back up, and a strange birth. These events are interpreted through Han cosmology as signs of military weakness and the improper rise of imperial consorts to power.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Ai of Han, Jianping era, Dingxiang, Lingling, Jing Fang, Treatise on the Five Elements, omens, portents, wood-spirit anomalies, biological mutations</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Volume Six

Two months prior, a child cried from within the womb. When the child was born, it was not raised <note>The term "not raised" (buju) often implies the child was abandoned or considered a bad omen at birth.</note> and was buried by the side of the road. Three days later, someone passing by heard the sound of the child crying. The mother then dug the child up and raised it.

In the summer of the fourth year of the **Jianping** era <note>3 BCE</note> of **Emperor Ai**, the people of the capital and the surrounding provinces gathered together in the streets and alleys. They set up gaming tables and equipment, singing and dancing to offer sacrifices to the <term>Queen Mother of the West</term> <note>original: 西王母; an ancient and powerful goddess associated with immortality and cosmic order</note>. They also circulated writings which said: "The Mother tells the people: those who wear this charm shall not die. If you do not believe my words, look beneath the door hinges; there you shall find white hair." This movement continued until autumn and then finally ceased. <note>This describes a real historical event: a massive, spontaneous religious movement triggered by a drought, which the Han court viewed with great alarm as a sign of social collapse.</note>

During the **Jianping** era of **Emperor Ai**, in **Yuzhang** <note>near modern-day Nanchang, Jiangxi</note>, there was a man who transformed into a woman. He married a husband and gave birth to a son. 

**Chen Feng** of **Chang’an** said: "This is a case of <term>Yang transforming into Yin</term> <note>original: 陽變為陰; in traditional Chinese philosophy, Yang represents the male/ruling principle and Yin represents the female/subject principle. Such a transformation was seen as a sign that the Emperor was losing his masculine authority to subordinates or women.</note>. It is a sign that the imperial succession is about to end. The image of one who should be a man instead giving birth signifies a 'self-generated' lineage that does not follow the proper order. The fact that he married and bore a son suggests the dynasty will last for only one more generation before being cut off." Indeed, later **Emperor Ai** passed away and **Emperor Ping**... <note>Emperor Ping was the last of the Western Han before the throne was usurped by Wang Mang.</note>

<summary>This page chronicles three major "omens": a child surviving burial, a mass religious cult dedicated to the Queen Mother of the West, and a gender-defying biological anomaly. All are used by Han-era historians to justify the claim that the Han dynasty's mandate to rule was failing.</summary>

# The Emperor Passes and Wang Mang Usurps the Throne

In the second month of the first year of the **Yuanshi** era <note>1 CE</note> of **Emperor Ping** of the **Han**, in **Guangmu** of the **Shuofang** region <note>a commandery in the northern frontier, near modern-day Inner Mongolia</note>, a woman named **Zhao Chun** died of an illness. After she had been placed in her coffin for seven days, she suddenly appeared outside of it. She claimed to have seen her deceased father, who told her: "You are only twenty-seven years old; it is not yet your time to die." 

The Grand Administrator, a man named **Tan**, reported this event to the court. The scholarly interpretation stated: "When the ultimate <term>Yin</term> <note>the passive, "lower," or dark principle</note> transforms into <term>Yang</term> <note>the active, "upper," or light principle</note>, those of lower status will rise above their superiors." This omen of the dead returning to life was seen as a portent of **Wang Mang’s** eventual usurpation of the imperial throne <note>original: 王莽簒位; Wang Mang was a commoner-turned-official who overthrew the Han, fitting the "lower rising above the higher" interpretation</note>.

In the sixth month of the first year of the **Yuanshi** era of **Emperor Ping**, a woman in the capital of **Chang'an** gave birth to a child with two heads on separate necks. The faces looked toward one another. The infant had four arms and a single chest, with all limbs facing forward. Upon the buttocks, there was an eye approximately two inches long. 

**Jing Fang’s** *Commentary on the Book of Changes* <note>original: 易傳 (Yi Zhuan); Jing Fang was a famous Han scholar who interpreted the I Ching to predict political disasters</note> states: "Isolated and estranged, one sees a pig covered in mud." <note>This quote from Hexagram 38 of the I Ching refers to a state of profound alienation and filth.</note> This omen of a person born with two heads signifies those of lower rank struggling against each other for power <note>original: 下相攘; suggesting political infighting or a grab for authority</note>.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of supernatural omens from the Han Dynasty. While the previous page focused on the rise of the usurper Wang Mang through the lens of the dead returning to life and multi-headed infants, this section provides a systematic guide for interpreting biological anomalies—termed "monstrous wonders"—and records a specific avian omen from the later reign of Emperor Zhang.</meta>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections

### Volume 6

->**Records of Searching for the Supernatural**<-

The same principles apply to humans as to the **six domestic animals** <note>original: 六畜; the horse, ox, goat, pig, dog, and chicken—the primary livestock of ancient China</note>. If their heads and eyes are positioned below, this is called "losing the superior." It indicates that the administration is about to undergo a change. The emergence of such <term>monstrous wonders</term> <note>original: 妖 (yāo); strange or supernatural occurrences believed to be warnings from Heaven</note> serves as a reprimand for the loss of the correct path, with each deformity reflecting a specific category of failure.

If there are two necks below a single head, it signifies a lack of unity in the state. Having more hands than is natural indicates that the ruler is employing wicked or "deviant" officials <note>original: 邪 (xié); meaning crooked or corrupt</note>. Having too few feet indicates that those in lower positions cannot fulfill their duties, or that the ruler is failing to employ those of lower rank. 

In general, if lower body parts grow from the upper body, it signifies a lack of respect toward superiors. If upper body parts grow from the lower body, it signifies a **desecration of status** <note>original: 媒瀆 (méidú); a blurring of the sacred boundaries between high and low social classes</note>. If a creature is born as a species other than its own, it signifies licentiousness and social chaos. 

If a human is born unnaturally large and connected at the top, it signifies that a transformation is "complete." If a child is able to speak at birth, it signifies a ruler’s love for falsehoods. All these various monsters are interpreted in this manner; if the ruler does not change his ways, these portents will culminate in disaster.

In the first year of the **Yuanhe** era <note>84 CE</note> of **Emperor Zhang** of the **Han**, in **Gaoliu** of the **Dai** Commandery <note>located in the northern frontier, near the modern border of Shanxi and Hebei provinces</note>, a bird gave birth to a chick with three legs. It was as large as a domestic chicken and red in color. On its head, it had a horn over an inch long.

When <term>Emperor Huan of Han</term> ascended the throne, a great snake was seen upon the <term>Deyang Hall</term> <note>The "Hall of Virtuous Light," the primary ceremonial hall within the southern imperial palace of Luoyang</note>. <term>Chunyu Yi</term>, the Market Master of the capital <term>Luoyang</term>, remarked: "A snake possesses scales and armor, which are symbols of soldiers. Appearing here within the inner sanctum suggests that the high officials of the <term>Pepper Chamber</term> <note>original: 椒房 (jiāofáng); a metonym for the Empress’s relatives, so named because the walls of the Empress's living quarters were plastered with pepper for warmth and fragrance</note> are destined to face the force of armored troops." Following this prediction, he resigned his office and fled into hiding. By the second year of the <term>Yanxi</term> era [159 AD], the <term>General-in-Chief</term> <term>Liang Ji</term> was indeed executed, his entire family was arrested and tried, and the capital was filled with marching soldiers.

---

In the third year of the <term>Jianhe</term> era [149 AD] during the reign of <term>Emperor Huan</term>, in the autumn during the seventh month, it rained meat in <term>Lian County</term> of the <term>Beidi</term> region. The pieces resembled mutton ribs, with some being as large as a human hand. At this time, <term>Empress Dowager Liang</term> was acting as regent, and her brother <term>Liang Ji</term> held absolute power. He had arbitrarily ordered the execution of the <term>Grand Commandant</term> <note>One of the three highest-ranking civil and military officials in the Han government</note> <term>Li Gu</term> and the official <term>Du Qiao</term>. The entire world felt the weight of this injustice. Subsequently, the Liang clan was utterly destroyed.

# Imperial Collection of the Four Books, Volume 6

The <term>toothache smile</term> <note>original: 齲齒笑 (qǔchǐ xiào)</note> and <term>melancholy brows</term> <note>original: 愁眉 (chóuméi)</note> were styles where the eyebrows were drawn thin and curved. The "toothache smile" made one look as if they were in pain, showing joy that was not truly cheerful. These fashions originated with <term>Sun Shou</term>, the wife of the <term>General-in-Chief Liang Ji</term>. The capital of <term>Luoyang</term> was captivated by them, and they were soon imitated throughout the <term>Middle Kingdom</term> <note>original: 諸夏 (Zhūxià); a term for the various states of China</note>. 

However, these were "Heavenly Warnings," as if to say: "Soldiers and horses are coming to arrest you. These women will soon be in sorrow, knitting their brows and weeping. The officers and soldiers will seize them so violently they will break their backs, and their hair buns will be knocked askew. Even if they force a smile and try to speak, all joy and spirit will be gone." By the second year of the <term>Yanxi</term> era [159 AD], the entire Liang clan was indeed executed.

# Collected Works of Jintuo, Volume 6

...it continued until the first year of the Zhongping era of Emperor Ling <note>184 AD</note>, when Zhang Jue arose. He called himself the "Yellow Heaven" and commanded a force of three hundred and sixty thousand men. They all wore yellow turbans on their heads; for this reason, the world called them the "Yellow Turban Rebels." The rebellion first rose in Ye and the forces gathered in Zhending. Zhang Jue deceived and deluded the common people, proclaiming:

->**"The Azure Heaven is already dead; the Yellow Heaven shall be established. In this, the year of <term>Jiazi</term>, there shall be great luck under heaven!"**<-
<note>The "Azure Heaven" represented the Han Dynasty, which ruled under the elemental phase of Fire (red/green associations), while "Yellow Heaven" represented the new era the rebels hoped to usher in. "Jiazi" is the first year of the traditional sixty-year sexagenary cycle, symbolizing a fresh start.</note>

The fact that they rose in Ye was a sign that the "Grand Work" <note>original: 業 (yè); a pun on the city name 鄴 (Yè)</note> of the world was beginning. That they gathered in Zhending was a sign of "True Stability" <note>original: 真定 (zhēndìng); the literal meaning of the city's name</note>. The common folk bowed to one another in the streets, rushing to join the faith. This was especially prevalent in the <term>Jing and Yang provinces</term> <note>Regions covering modern-day Hubei, Hunan, Anhui, and Jiangsu</note>. People abandoned their property and wandered aimlessly along the roads; the dead were beyond counting.

Zhang Jue and his followers first raised their standard of revolt in the second month. By the twelfth month of that same winter, they were utterly defeated. However, from the time of the <term>Restoration of Emperor Guangwu</term> <note>The start of the Eastern Han, c. 25 AD</note> until the uprising of the Yellow Turbans, a period of not quite two hundred and ten years had passed. At this point, the world fell into great chaos and the <term>Mandate of the Han</term> <note>original: 漢祚 (hànzuò); the "throne-fate" or dynastic line of the Han</note> was severed and extinguished. This truly fulfilled the prophecy of the **Three-Seven Cycle**. <note>As established on the previous page, "Three-Seven" refers to 210 years (3 x 7 x 10), a numerological period of destiny in ancient Chinese historiography.</note>

During the <term>Jianning era</term> <note>168–172 AD</note> of <term>Emperor Ling</term>, it was fashionable for men to wear "long robes" that were remarkably short on the bottom, while women favored "long skirts" with tops that were remarkably short. The relationship between husband and wife represents the two primary forces of <term>Yin and Yang</term>, the deepest of all human affections. When they turn to devouring one another, it shows that Yin and Yang are encroaching upon and destroying each other. Once Emperor Ling passed away, the world fell into utter disorder. The sovereign committed the violence of wanton executions, while ministers committed the treason of abduction and assassination. # In Search of the Supernatural

### Sixteen

...his hair and beard were both lush. <note>original: 鬒 (zhěn); this refers to thick, black hair, completing the description of the phantom "Yellow Man" from the preceding page.</note> In the tenth month of the fifth year <note>176 AD</note>, on the day of *Renwu*, the <term>Scholar-trees</term> <note>original: 槐 (huái); the Sophora japonica, traditionally associated with high-ranking officialdom</note> beside the main hall—each measuring six or seven spans in circumference—uprooted themselves and stood upside down, with their roots above and their branches below. Furthermore, during the <term>Zhongping era</term> <note>184–189 AD</note>, six or seven *li* northwest of the city of <term>Chang’an</term>, a human face with a beard grew inside a hollow tree. According to the <term>Great Plan</term> <note>original: 洪範 (Hóngfàn); a classic text used by court diviners to interpret natural disasters as criticisms of the Emperor’s conduct</note>, these are all instances of "wood failing to be crooked or straight." <note>In traditional Chinese cosmology, when the "Element of Wood" is disturbed, plants grow in distorted ways, signaling that the ruler’s laws are not being obeyed and the social order is inverted.</note>

In the first year of the <term>Guanghe era</term> <note>178 AD</note> of Emperor Ling, in the quarters of the Palace Attendants within the Southern Palace, a hen attempted to transform into a rooster. Its entire plumage was like that of a rooster, but its comb had not yet changed. <note>Such gender reversals in animals were viewed as dire omens of "Yin" (the feminine or subordinates) encroaching upon "Yang" (the masculine or the Emperor), often interpreted as a warning against the rising power of palace eunuchs.</note>

In the second year of the Guanghe era <note>179 AD</note>, a woman outside the <term>Shangxi Gate</term> <note>the "Upper West Gate"</note> of Luoyang gave birth to a child with two heads and separate shoulders, but sharing a single chest, both facing forward. Deeming this an ill omen, the parents abandoned the child on the ground. From that time onward, the Imperial Court fell into utter disorder, and political power resided within private houses. There was no distinction between the superior and the inferior—this was the symbolism of the "two heads" <note>representing divided authority or two "heads" of state</note>. Later, the warlord <term>Dong Zhuo</term> murdered the Empress Dowager and...

She was deposed and died in prison out of grief.</note> led to the Empress being deposed and discarded, only to be further harmed later. In the fourth year of the **Guanghe era** <note>181 AD</note>, a man nine feet tall dressed in white clothes appeared within the quarters of the <term>Palace Attendants</term> <note>original: 中黃門 (Zhōnghuángmén); eunuch officials who served in the inner palace</note> in the Southern Palace. An attendant named Xie Bu shouted a question at him: "What kind of person are you, wearing white and recklessly entering the private palace quarters?" 

The man in white replied: "I am **Liang Boxia**. The Emperor of Heaven has sent me to be the Son of Heaven." When Bu moved forward to seize him, the figure suddenly vanished.

In the seventh year of the **Guanghe era** <note>184 AD</note>, along the roadsides within the boundaries of Chenliu, Jiyang, and Changyuan, as well as <term>Yuanju</term> and <term>Lihu</term> in the Jiyin and Dongjun commanderies, the grasses that grew all took on the shapes of humans. They appeared to be holding weapons and crossbows; others took the forms of oxen, horses, dragons, snakes, birds, and beasts. Their colors were distinct in white and black, and they were fully equipped with feathers, fur, heads, eyes, feet, and wings. They were not merely vague resemblances, but were extraordinarily lifelike. 

Ancient sayings refer to this as a **Grass Omen** <note>original: 草妖 (cǎoyāo); literally "grass demon" or "botanical monstrosity," interpreted as a sign that the "wood" element of the world is out of balance due to bad governance</note>. In this same year, the **Yellow Turban Rebellion** <note>original: 黃巾賊 (Huángjīnzéi); a massive peasant uprising led by Zhang Jue that signaled the beginning of the end for the Han Dynasty</note> broke out, and the power of the Han subsequently grew weak and frail.

### Records of the Later Han

In the first year of the **Zhongping era** <note>184 AD</note>, on the *Renshen* day of the sixth month, a man named **Liu Cang** from Luoyang, who lived outside the Upper West Gate, had a wife who gave birth to a son with two heads sharing a single body. Later, during the **Jian'an era** <note>196–220 AD</note>, a woman also gave birth to a boy with two heads sharing one body.

In the eighth month of the third year of the **Zhongping era** <note>186 AD</note>, over ten thousand sparrows gathered atop the **Huailing** <note>The mausoleum of Emperor Cheng of the Western Han</note>. At first, they chirped with extreme sorrow; then, they fell into a chaotic brawl, slaughtering one another. Their severed heads were left hanging from the branches of trees and thorny bushes. This continued until the sixth year <note>189 AD</note>, when **Emperor Ling** passed away.

The mausoleum is a symbol of height and greatness. The sparrow <note>original: 雀 (què)</note> is a pun for "nobility" <note>original: 爵 (jué); the word refers to titles of rank and the sacrificial vessels used by the elite</note>. This was a <term>Heavenly Warning</term> <note>original: 天戒 (tiānjiè); the cosmological belief that nature reacts to the Emperor's failures</note>, as if to say: "Those who harbor desires for rank and salary <note>original: 爵禄 (juélù)</note> and who occupy high and wealthy positions shall turn back to harm one another until they meet their total destruction."

During the Han dynasty, at weddings and joyous banquets in the capital, it became the custom to perform **puppet plays** <note>original: 魁櫑 (kuí lěi); an ancient form of puppet theater</note>. After the guests were tipsy with wine, they would follow the performance by singing **dirges** <note>original: 挽歌 (wǎngē); funeral songs</note>. The puppet play was originally music for a house of mourning, and the dirge was meant for those holding the ropes of a hearse to sing in harmony. This was a Heavenly Warning, as if to say the State...

# Lord Cao Rose from the Commoner Class
<note>Refers to Cao Cao’s rise to power despite not coming from the highest tier of the traditional aristocracy.</note>

In the seventh year of the **Jian’an era** of **Emperor Xian** <note>202 AD</note>, a man in **Yuexi** <note>a commandery in modern-day Sichuan and Yunnan</note> transformed into a woman. At that time, the official **Zhou Qun** submitted a memorial to the throne, noting that a similar transformation had occurred during the reign of **Emperor Ai** <note>r. 7–1 BC</note>, and that such an event portended a change in the ruling dynasty. By the twenty-fifth year of the era <note>220 AD</note>, **Emperor Xian** was indeed deposed and enfeoffed as the **Duke of Shanyang** <note>marking the official end of the Han Dynasty and the beginning of the Wei</note>.

<margin>Records of Searching for the Supernatural</margin>

At the beginning of the **Jian’an era**, a nursery rhyme spread through **Jingzhou** <note>a strategic and prosperous central province</note> which said:

*“Between eight and nine years, the decline shall begin;*
*By the thirteenth year, there shall be nothing left.”*

This was a reference to the fact that since the **Restoration** <note>the beginning of the Eastern Han, c. 25 AD</note>, **Jingzhou** had remained uniquely peaceful and whole. When **Liu Biao** <note>a powerful warlord</note> served as the Governor, the people were prosperous and happy. However, the "beginning of decline" arrived in the ninth year of **Jian’an** <note>204 AD</note>, referring to the death of **Liu Biao’s** wife and the subsequent death or dispersal of his various generals. The prophecy that "by the thirteenth year <note>208 AD</note> nothing shall remain" foretold that **Liu Biao** himself would die, and his territory would subsequently be lost and ruined. At that time...

In **Huarong** <note>a county in modern-day Hunan</note>, there was a woman who suddenly began to wail and cry out, saying, "A great mourning is at hand!" Her speech was so erratic and strange that the county authorities, deeming her words to be heretical and seditious, cast her into prison. After she had been held for more than a month, she suddenly began to weep within her cell and cried, "**Liu Jingzhou** <note>referring to the Governor of Jing Province, Liu Biao</note> has died this very day." 

**Huarong** was located several hundred *li* <note>a traditional unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile</note> from the provincial capital. The authorities immediately dispatched a messenger to investigate the claim, and they found that **Liu Biao** had indeed died. The county then released the woman from prison. Later, she sang a song that went: "Who would have thought that **Li Li** would become a man of high rank?" Not long after, **Lord Cao** <note>Cao Cao</note> pacified **Jing Province** and appointed **Li Li** (whose courtesy name was **Jianxian**, from **Zhuo Commandery**) as the Inspector of **Jing Province**.

In the first month of the twenty-fifth year of the **Jian’an era** <note>January 220 AD</note>, **Wei Wu** <note>the posthumous title of Cao Cao</note> was in **Luoyang** constructing the **Jianshi Hall**. He ordered a **Zhuolong tree** <note>literally "Washing Dragon Tree," a legendary or sacred tree</note> to be felled, but as it was cut, blood flowed out from the trunk. Furthermore, when a pear tree was dug up to be moved, its roots were injured and it also bled. **Wei Wu** was deeply unsettled and sickened by these events, and he took to his bed. He passed away that same month. This year became the first year of the **Huangchu era** <note>220–226 AD</note> for **Wei Wen** <note>the posthumous title of Cao Pi, Cao Cao's son and the first Emperor of the Wei Dynasty</note>.

<summary>This page records two supernatural omens from the end of the Han Dynasty: a woman's accurate prophecy of Liu Biao's death and the bleeding trees that portended the death of Cao Cao.</summary>

In the first year of the **Huangchu** era of **Wei** <note>220 AD, the first year of Cao Pi's reign</note>, a hawk was born in a swallow’s nest within the **Weiyang Palace**. Its beak and talons were both bright red. 

Later, during the **Qinglong** era <note>233–237 AD</note>, when **Emperor Ming** <note>Cao Rui</note> began the construction of the **Lingxiao Pavilion** <note>The "Sky-Reaching" or "Cloud-Consuming" Pavilion</note>, a magpie built its nest upon the rising structure. The Emperor questioned the scholar **Gao Tanglong** about this. **Long** replied: "The *Classic of Poetry* says, 'The magpie has a nest, but the dove dwells in it.' <note>original: "維鵲有巢、維鳩居之" — a metaphor for someone taking over what another has built</note> Now, while your Majesty is raising this palace, a magpie comes to nest. This is a sign that the palace will not be completed before your person is unable to dwell within it."

---

At the beginning of the **Jiaping** era <note>249 AD</note> of the **King of Qi** <note>Cao Fang</note>, a **demon horse** <note>original: 妖馬 (yāomǎ)</note> emerged from the **Baima River** <note>White Horse River</note>. At night, it passed by the edge of the imperial pastures, neighing and calling out. All the other horses answered its call. The following morning, the people saw its tracks; they were as large as a **bushel** <note>original: 斛 (hú), a large grain measure, suggesting massive size</note>. The tracks continued for several *li* before returning into the river.

---

In the first year of the **Jingchu** era <note>237 AD</note>, a swallow gave birth to a creature with a massive shell at the home of **Li Gai** in the state of **Wei**. The creature’s body was shaped like a hawk, but its beak resembled that of a swallow. **Gao Tanglong** remarked: "This is a great and strange anomaly for the House of **Wei**. You must guard against **hawk-soaring ministers** <note>original: 鷹揚之臣 (yīngyáng zhī chén) — a metaphor for powerful, predatory officials, likely a veiled reference to the rising Sima clan</note> within the **palace screens** <note>original: 蕭牆 (xiāoqiáng) — a term referring to the inner court or domestic threats</note>."

Afterward, **Emperor Xuan** <note>Sima Yi (179–251 AD), the general who seized power in Wei; he was posthumously honored as an emperor by his grandson, the founder of the Jin Dynasty</note> rose up and executed **Cao Shuang** <note>A powerful regent and kinsman to the Wei imperial family</note>, and consequently took possession of the **House of Wei**.

---

In the fifth year of the **Jingyao** era of **Shu** <note>262 AD, during the reign of the last Shu emperor, Liu Shan</note>, a massive tree within the palace snapped for no reason. **Qiao Zhou** <note>A prominent scholar and minister known for his knowledge of divination and the classics</note> was deeply troubled by this, but he had no one he could speak to about it. He then wrote upon a pillar:

-> "Numerous and Great, the time of meeting is near. That which is granted, what shall it be?" <-

He further explained that "Cao" <note>original: 曹 (Cáo)</note> means "Great." "Numerous and Great" <note>original: 眾而大 (zhòng ér dà)</note> meant that the world should soon converge <note>under a new power</note>. "That which is granted, what shall it be?" was a question of whether anyone else would be established as a ruler. After the Kingdom of **Shu** was destroyed, everyone regarded **Zhou's** words as a proven prophecy.

---

On the first day of the eighth month, in the first year of the **Taiyuan** era of **Sun Quan** of **Wu** <note>251 AD</note>, a great wind arose. The rivers and seas surged and overflowed until the water was eight feet deep on level ground. It uprooted two thousand trees at **Gaoling**, and even stone steles were shifted by the force. Two gates of the **Wu** capital were blown off. The following year, **Sun Quan** <note>The founding Emperor of Eastern Wu</note> died.

# In Search of the Supernatural
## Volume 6

In the sixth month of the first year of the **Wufeng** era of **Sun Liang** of **Wu** <note>254 AD; Sun Liang was the second emperor of Eastern Wu, known for his brief and troubled reign</note>, the barnyard grass in **Jiaozhi** <note>A commandery encompassing parts of modern-day northern Vietnam and southern China</note> transformed into rice. Long ago, when the **Three Miao** <note>An ancient tribal confederacy described in the *Classic of History* as rebels against the Sage Kings</note> were about to perish, the five grains mutated in their seeds. This event was a "grass anomaly" <note>original: 草妖 (cǎo yāo); a term used in traditional Chinese omenology to describe botanical freaks of nature that predict political disaster</note>. Following this, **Sun Liang** was deposed.

In the fifth month of the second year of the **Wufeng** era of **Sun Liang** of **Wu** <note>255 AD</note>, a massive stone on **Lili Mountain** in **Yangxian County** <note>In modern-day Yixing, Jiangsu Province</note> stood upright by itself. This was a prophetic response to the fact that **Sun Hao** <note>The grandson of Sun Quan; his father had been a disgraced crown prince</note> would eventually emerge from a deposed and disgraced family to reclaim the imperial position.

In the fourth year of the **Yongan** era of **Sun Xiu** of **Wu** <note>261 AD; Sun Xiu was the third emperor of Wu and Sun Liang's brother</note>, a commoner from **Anwu** named **Chen Jiao** died, but returned to life after seven days, digging his way out of his tomb. This was an auspicious sign that **Sun Hao**, though coming from a disgraced household, would eventually take the throne.

In the later years of **Sun Xiu**, the style of clothing changed: the upper garments were made long while the lower garments were made short. Furthermore, people would stack five or six collars on top of each other, while the lower robes <note>original: 裳 (sháng); referring to the skirt-like lower garment worn by both men and women</note> were only one or two parts of the total length. This was an omen of the extravagance and wastefulness of the upper classes, while those below were pressured by poverty—a symbolic image of "surplus at the top and insufficiency at the bottom."

<summary>This page records several botanical, geological, and supernatural omens during the Eastern Wu period that supposedly predicted the fall of Emperors Sun Liang and Sun Xiu and the unlikely rise of Sun Hao.</summary>
<keywords>Eastern Wu, Sun Liang, Sun Hao, Sun Xiu, omens, Jiaozhi, Lili Mountain, resurrection, clothing styles, dynastic decline</keywords>

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections

## In Search of the Supernatural, Volume 7
### Written by Gan Bao of the Jin Dynasty

Initially, during the reigns of Emperors **Yuan** and **Cheng** of the **Han** dynasty <note>Regnal periods spanning 48 BC to 7 BC; this was a period of increasing political instability toward the end of the Western Han</note>, men of foresight declared: "When the years of the **Wei** are harmonious, a stone shall split open more than three thousand *li* <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly 500 meters or 1/3 of a mile</note> to the west. It will be tied to five horses, and the inscription will read: 'Greatly punish the **Cao** family.'" <note>original: "大討曹" (Dà tǎo Cáo). This is a retroactive prophecy concerning the Cao family, who would later found the Cao Wei state and eventually be supplanted by the Sima family of the Jin dynasty</note>

When the state of **Wei** first rose to power, a stone horse indeed split open in **Liugu** <note>Literally "Willow Valley"</note> of **Zhangye** <note>A strategic commandery in the Hexi Corridor, modern-day Gansu province</note>. It was first sighted during the **Jian’an** era <note>196–220 AD, the final years of the Han</note>, took its full shape during the **Huangchu** era <note>220–226 AD, the reign of the first Wei Emperor, Cao Pi</note>, and its inscriptions were fully completed during the **Taihe** era <note>227–233 AD, the reign of Cao Rui</note>.

The stone measured seven *xun* <note>An ancient unit of length, approximately 8 feet or 2.4 meters</note> in circumference and one *ren* <note>Roughly 7 to 8 feet</note> in height at its center. It possessed a greenish texture with white patterns. Images of dragons, horses, <term>qilin <note>A mythical hooved chimerical creature, often translated as 'Chinese unicorn,' whose appearance signals the arrival of a sage or a change in dynasty</note></term>, deer, phoenixes, and immortals were all brilliantly and clearly displayed upon it. This event served as a divine omen of the successive rise of the **Wei** and **Jin** dynasties.

By the third year of the **Taishi** era of the **Jin** dynasty <note>267 AD, during the reign of Sima Yan, who unified China</note>, **Jiao Sheng**, the Governor of **Zhangye**, submitted a memorial to the throne regarding its preservation within the commandery as a testament to the nation's destiny...

<summary>This page describes a prophetic geological anomaly in Zhangye—a stone that "split open" to reveal divine images and an inscription foretelling the downfall of the Cao Wei dynasty and the rise of the Jin. It serves to legitimize the Jin dynasty's rule through ancient prophecy and supernatural signs.</summary>
<keywords>Zhangye, prophetic stone, Cao Wei dynasty, Jin dynasty, Gan Bao, omens, Liugu, Jiao Sheng</keywords>

# By Imperial Order: The Complete Library in Four Sections

## Records of Inscriptions on Metal and Stone, Volume 1 (Upper)

Diagrams and inspections of the current stone inscriptions show varying counts of characters; I have carefully presented the diagrams above. Examining the text, there are depictions of five horses. One image shows a person wearing a flat-topped official cap <term>幘 (zé): a traditional headcloth or cap worn by officials</term> holding a halberd <term>戟 (jǐ): a spear-like weapon with a crescent blade</term> and riding upon a horse. Another image resembles the shape of a horse but the characters are incomplete. Among the legible characters are **"Metal"**, **"Middle"**, **"Grand Marshal"** <note>original: 大司馬 (Dà Sīmǎ), one of the highest military offices in ancient China</note>, **"King"**, **"Great Good Fortune"**, **"Upright"**, and **"Opening Longevity."** One sequence forms a complete line stating: "The Metal shall take it." <note>original: "金當取之" (Jīn dāng qǔ zhī). In the theory of the Five Phases, the Jin dynasty was associated with the element of Metal, signifying it would "take" the mandate from the Wei dynasty, which was associated with Earth</note>

At the beginning of the **Taishi** era <note>265–274 AD</note> of **Emperor Wu of Jin**, clothing styles were narrow at the top and voluminous at the bottom. Those wearing these robes would all "oppress the waist" <note>厭腰 (yàn yāo): a style where the belt or fabric tightly constricted the midsection, making the upper body appear disproportionately small</note>. This was an omen of a weak and shrinking sovereign, while the ministers were unrestrained and flamboyant. By the end of the **Yuankang** era <note>291–299 AD</note>, women began wearing "double-crotch" garments <note>兩襠 (liǎng dāng): a type of vest or chest-protector worn over other clothes</note> over their cross-collar robes. This was a sign of "the internal emerging to the external" <note>A metaphorical warning that women or palace insiders were interfering in external state affairs, which was seen as a violation of the natural order</note>.

Those who commissioned carriages prioritized fragile and delicate designs, frequently changing their shapes. They all used white bamboo splints <note>白篾 (bái miè): thin strips of white bamboo or reed</note> for the trimmings. These were vestiges of the appearance of ancient funeral carriages—a prophetic sign of the calamities that would soon befall the **Jin** dynasty.

<summary>This page catalogs the specific characters and imagery found on a prophetic stone, alongside cultural omens in fashion and transportation that signaled the decline of the Jin dynasty.</summary>
<keywords>Jin dynasty, Emperor Wu, omens, stone inscriptions, Taishi era, Yuankang era, prophetic fashion, Five Phases</keywords>

# In Search of the Supernatural

## Volume 2

The folding stool <term>胡床 (húchuáng): literally "barbarian bed," a portable folding chair introduced from nomadic tribes to the north and west</term> and the Northern platter are vessels of the Di people. Qiang-style boiling and Northern-style roasting are the foods of the Di. Since the **Taishi** era <note>265–274 AD, the beginning of the Jin dynasty</note>, China has prized these things. Noblemen and wealthy households must possess these items, and at auspicious banquets for honored guests, they are always served first. This was a prophetic omen <note>The adoption of "barbarian" customs by the elite was seen by traditionalists as a sign that the Central Plains would soon be "overrun" by these nomadic groups</note>.

In the fourth year of the **Taikang** era <note>283 AD</note>, in Kuaiji Commandery <note>located in modern-day Zhejiang Province</note>, the shore crabs <term>蟛蜞 (péngqí): a species of small marsh crab</term> and the larger crabs all transformed into rats. They were so numerous they covered the fields. They consumed the rice crops, creating a disaster. When they first transformed, they possessed fur and flesh but lacked bones, and they were unable to move across the embankments between the fields. After several days, they all became female <term>牝 (pìn): the female of a species, associated here with an excess of Yin energy and social disorder</term>.

In the first month of the fifth year of the **Taikang** era <note>284 AD</note>, two dragons appeared inside a well at the Armory. The Armory is the place where the sovereign’s instruments of awesome power and defense are treasured and stored. Its buildings are deep and secluded, not a place where dragons should dwell. Seven years after this event, the vassal kings <note>the princes of the imperial Sima family</note> began to mutually... <note>The text cuts off, referring to the "War of the Eight Princes," a period of civil war that began in 291 AD</note>

<summary>This page details cultural and natural anomalies during the early Jin Dynasty, specifically the adoption of foreign "barbarian" customs and the miraculous transformation of crabs into rats, serving as warnings of impending civil war.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Volume 7

...twenty-eight years later, there were indeed Liu and Shi <note>referring to Liu Yuan and Shi Le, non-Han leaders who established their own kingdoms during the collapse of the Western Jin</note> who usurped the Imperial Regalia <term>神器 (shénqì): literally "divine vessels," a metaphor for the throne and the right to rule</term>; both of them had the word "Dragon" in their names <note>Original: 皆字曰龍 (jiē zì yuē lóng). Liu Yuan’s courtesy name was Yuanhai, "Prime Sea," but he claimed the heritage of the Han "dragon" emperors; Shi Le was associated with dragon omens at his birth</note>.

In the sixth year of the **Taikang** era <note>285 AD</note> of **Emperor Wu of Jin**, a two-legged tiger was captured in Nanyang. A tiger is the essence of Yin but resides within the Yang; it is a beast belonging to the element of Metal <note>In the Five Elements (Wuxing) system, the tiger is associated with the West and the element of Metal</note>. "Nanyang" <note>literally "Southern Sun/Yang"</note> is a name associated with the element of Fire. The essence of Metal entering Fire causes it to lose its proper form. This was a demonic omen <term>妖 (yāo): a supernatural anomaly or "monstrous" occurrence</term> signifying chaos within the royal household.

In the eleventh month of the seventh year [of Taikang], on the day of *Jingchen*, a four-horned beast appeared in Hejian. This was a warning from Heaven, as if to say: horns are a symbol of soldiers and weaponry. The number four is a symbol of the four directions. This meant that war and armor would rise up from all four directions. Later, the **Prince of Hejian** <note>Sima Yong, a key figure in the civil wars of the Jin dynasty</note> indeed mobilized troops from the four directions to act as the catalyst for rebellion <term>亂階 (luànjiē): literally "the steps to chaos" or the ladder of revolt</term>.

In the ninth year of the **Taikang** era <note>288 AD</note>, north of the border in **Youzhou**, there was a dead ox's head that spoke. At that time, the Emperor suffered from frequent illnesses and was deeply concerned with the succession of the state, yet he did not entrust the future to those of supreme impartiality. This anomaly was the cosmic response to the threat of impending disorder.

<summary>This page records several animal-related omens from the late 3rd century AD, including a two-legged tiger and a speaking ox head, interpreted as warnings of the collapse of the Jin Dynasty and the civil war involving the Prince of Hejian.</summary>

### In Search of the Supernatural
#### Volume 7

During the **Taikang** era <note>280–289 AD</note>, two carp appeared on the roof of the Imperial Armory. The Armory is the headquarters of the military; because fish possess scales <note>Original: 鱗甲 (línjiǎ). In Chinese, the word for scales is the same as the word for "armor," creating a direct symbolic link between fish and soldiers</note>, they are classified alongside the military. 

Furthermore, fish represent the extreme of *Yin* <note>the principle of water, darkness, and the feminine</note>, while the roof of a building represents the "Great *Yang*" <note>the principle of the sun, height, and the Emperor</note>. Fish appearing on a roof symbolized a catastrophe of "armed metal" <note>warfare</note>—the ultimate *Yin*—violating the Great *Yang*. 

When **Emperor Hui** began his reign, the Empress’s father, **Yang Jun**, was executed. Arrows were exchanged within the very gates of the Palace. The Empress Dowager was deposed to the status of a commoner and died in a secluded palace <note>Empress Yang Zhi, who was starved to death in 292 AD</note>. By the end of the **Yuankang** era <note>291–299 AD</note>, **Empress Jia** had seized total control; she slandered and murdered the Crown Prince, only to be executed and deposed shortly thereafter. Within a span of ten years, the disaster of a "Mother Empress" arose twice. This was the fulfillment of the omen. From this point forward, chaos and rebellion were fully formed. 

**Jing Fang’s** *Transformations in the Book of Changes* <note>a classic text on interpreting omens and environmental anomalies</note> states:

> When fish leave the water and fly onto the roads, war is about to break out.

When shoes <term>屨 (jù): ancient footwear made of hemp, silk, or leather</term> were first created, women’s shoes had round toes and men’s shoes had square toes. This was a deliberate design intended to distinguish between males and females. However, reaching...

It also begins a discussion on how changes in fashion, specifically footwear, reflect the breakdown of social order.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume One

During the **Taikang** era <note>280–289 AD</note>, women all wore square-toed shoes <note>original: 方頭屨 (fāngtóu jù)</note> that were indistinguishable from those worn by men. This was interpreted as an omen <note>original: 徵 (zhēng)</note> of **Empress Jia’s** subsequent period of total domestic jealousy and her eventual usurpation of masculine authority.

During the **Jin** dynasty, when women styled their hair, they would use silk to bind the loops of the bun tightly once it was formed. This style was known as the **“Tied-Child Bun”** <note>original: 擷子髻 (xié zǐ jì). The name suggests binding or capturing</note>. This fashion originated within the Imperial Palace, but the entire world quickly and harmoniously adopted it. In the final years of the era, there eventually occurred the “Embracing of the Emperors” incidents <note>original: 懷惠之事 (Huái Huì zhī shì). This is a wordplay on the names of Emperor Huai and Emperor Hui, suggesting they would be "captured" or "bound" like the hairstyle</note>.

During the **Taikang** era, a dance known as **“The Jin World at Peace”** <note>original: 晉世寧 (Jìn shì níng)</note> was performed throughout the empire. In this dance, the performers would press their hands down to hold cups and plates, flipping them over and back again. The lyrics of the accompanying song said: *“The world of Jin is at peace; cups and plates are flipped and turned.”* This flipping motion was extremely precarious. While cups and plates are merely vessels for wine, naming the dance “The Jin World at Peace” was a commentary on the people of that time. It suggested they were living only for the moment, focused on food and drink <note>original: 苟且飲食 (gǒuqiě yǐnshí)</note>, while their wisdom failed to see the approaching danger. They were as vulnerable as a vessel held in a hand that is constantly being overturned.

<summary>This page describes how women's fashion—specifically square shoes and "tied" hairstyles—and the "Jin World at Peace" dance served as symbolic warnings of the impending political chaos under Empress Jia and the eventual capture of the Jin emperors.</summary>

<keywords>Taikang era, Empress Jia, gender-crossing fashion, Tied-Child Bun, Jin World at Peace dance, omens, Book of Jin, cultural portents</keywords>

# In Search of the Supernatural
## Page 41

Toward the end of the **Taikang** era <note>280–289 AD</note>, the people of the capital at **Luoyang** began singing a song titled **"Breaking the Willow"** <note>original: 折楊柳 (Zhè Yángliǔ)</note>. The lyrics of this song began with descriptions of the hardships and suffering of soldiers in armor, but concluded with themes of being captured, seized, and executed. Later, when **Yang Jun** <note>a powerful regent whose surname, Yang, is the same character as "Willow"</note> was executed and the **Empress Dowager** was imprisoned until she died of starvation, it was seen as the prophetic fulfillment of the "Willow" song.

In the first year of the **Taixi** era of **Emperor Wu of Jin** <note>290 AD</note>, a horse in the **Liaodong** region grew horns. These horns grew beneath its two ears and were three inches long. Soon after, the Emperor passed away <note>literally "the Emperor's carriage rested," a polite term for imperial death</note>, and the Imperial House was poisoned by the calamities of war.

During the **Yuankang** era <note>291–299 AD</note> of **Emperor Hui of Jin**, the ornaments worn by women included the **"Five Martial Accoutrements"** <note>original: 五佩兵 (wǔ pèi bīng); miniature charms shaped like weapons</note>. Furthermore, they used materials such as gold, silver, ivory, and tortoiseshell <note>original: 瑇瑁 (dàimào)</note> to craft miniature battle-axes, polearms, spears, and halberds, which they wore in their hair to serve as hairpins <note>original: 笄 (jī)</note>. The distinction between men and women is one of the great principles of the state; for this reason, clothing and food are categorized into different ranks. Now, when women...

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature
## Volume 7

Using weapons as decoration even as war was supposedly ending <note>referring to the end of the "Breaking the Willow" crisis mentioned on the previous page</note> was an extreme manifestation of these monstrous omens. Consequently, the tragic events involving **Empress Jia** <note>Jia Nanfeng, the infamously ruthless consort of Emperor Hui</note> followed.

In the third year of the **Yuankang** era of the **Jin Dynasty** <note>293 AD</note>, during the leap second month, the six bells positioned before the main palace hall all began to shed tears. This continued for five *ke* <note>original: 五刻; a traditional unit of time, approximately 72 minutes</note> before finally stopping. In the preceding year, **Empress Jia** had murdered the **Empress Dowager Yang** <note>Yang Zhi, the step-mother of the Emperor</note> at **Jinyong City** <note>a fortress-prison in the northwest of the capital, Luoyang</note>. Because **Empress Jia** remained unrepentant in her wickedness, the bells wept as if in grief for the victim.

During the reign of **Emperor Hui**, there was a person in the capital city of **Luoyang** who possessed both male and female sexual organs on a single body. This person could perform both roles and was said to be exceptionally lustful. The wars and chaos that ravaged the world were the result of the *qi* <note>vital energy or cosmic breath</note> of the male and female principles being in total disorder; thus, these monstrous forms <note>original: 妖形 (yāoxíng)</note> were made manifest.

<summary>This page records supernatural omens during the reign of Emperor Hui, specifically weeping palace bells and a hermaphroditic individual, interpreting them as divine reactions to Empress Jia's cruelty and the collapse of the natural gender order.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature
## Volume 7

In the middle of the **Yuankang** era <note>291–299 AD</note> of **Emperor Hui**, there was a girl in **Anfeng** <note>a commandery in modern-day Anhui province</note> named **Zhou Shining**. When she was eight years old, she gradually began transforming into a man. By the time she was seventeen or eighteen, her temperament and disposition had become entirely masculine; however, her female anatomy had not completely vanished, and her male anatomy was not fully developed. She eventually took a wife, but they remained childless. <note>The text suggests that such biological anomalies mirrored the "disorder of the cosmic breath" at court.</note>

In the third month of the fifth year of the **Yuankang** era <note>295 AD</note>, a massive serpent appeared in **Linzi** <note>an ancient city in modern-day Shandong</note>. It was approximately ten *zhang* long <note>original: 十許丈; roughly 100 feet or 30 meters</note> and carried two smaller snakes on its back. It entered through the north gate of the city, passed directly through the marketplace, and went into the shrine dedicated to **King Jing of Chengyang** <note>Liu Zhang, a Han Dynasty prince who was posthumously worshipped as a local deity</note> of the **Han Dynasty**, where it vanished from sight.

In the same month of the fifth year of **Yuankang**, blood flowed upon the ground in **Lü County** <note>located in modern Jiangsu</note> for a distance of more than one hundred paces from east to west. Eight years later, **Feng Yun** <note>a rebel general who joined the uprisings during the War of the Eight Princes</note> incited a revolt in **Xuzhou**, resulting in the deaths and injuries of tens of thousands of people.

In the seventh year of **Yuankang** <note>297 AD</note>, a thunderbolt struck and shattered the **Gaomei Stone** south of the city. This stone was located within the **Gaomei Palace**, a shrine specifically used for offering sacrifices to pray for the birth of children. <note>The destruction of a fertility shrine by lightning was seen as a dire omen for the continuation of the imperial line.</note>

<summary>This page records several supernatural portents from the Yuankang era, including a case of spontaneous sex change, a giant serpent, and a rain of blood that foretold a bloody rebellion. These events were interpreted by contemporary historians as warnings of the impending collapse of the Jin Dynasty's social and political order.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature
## Volume 7

...was because **Empress Jia** <note>Jia Nanfeng, the famously ruthless and manipulative consort of Emperor Hui</note> was consumed by jealousy and spite and was planning to murder **Minhuai** <note>Sima Yu, the Crown Prince and posthumously named Minhuai, whom Empress Jia framed and executed in 300 AD</note>. Therefore, Heaven grew angry, and the eventual execution of **Empress Jia** was the divine response to these omens.

During the **Yuankang** era <note>291–299 AD</note>, people throughout the realm began to imitate one another by fashioning **ebony canes** <term>wuzhang; dark wooden crutches</term> to support their armpits. Later, they gradually added metal spikes to the tips so that when the user stopped walking, the cane could be planted to stand upright. By the time of the reigns of **Emperor Huai** and **Emperor Min**, the Imperial House suffered many calamities, and the central capital fell into ruin. **Emperor Yuan** <note>Sima Rui, who moved the capital south to Jiankang, beginning the Eastern Jin</note>, acting as a regional official, cultivated virtue in the east and sustained the world. This was the fulfillment of the omen of "supporting the armpits" <note>the term for supporting the armpits, *zhuye*, is used here as a pun for a supportive official "propping up" the failing state from the periphery</note>.

During the **Yuankang** era, the sons of the nobility gathered to drink with their hair disheveled and their bodies naked. They trifled with maidservants and concubines in the presence of others. Those who opposed such behavior were accused of ruining the mood, and those who criticized it were met with ridicule. The ambitious men of that age were ashamed if they were not included in such circles. Shortly thereafter, the **Rebellion of Liu and Shi** <note>the devastating uprisings led by Liu Yuan and Shi Le which led to the loss of northern China</note> occurred.

<summary>This page interprets the fashion of "ebony canes" and the hedonistic behavior of the Jin nobility as prophetic warnings of the dynasty's impending collapse and the need for a "supporting" official to save the state.</summary>
